# Daily Summary: 2025-05-21

### EEG-to-Text Translation: A Model for Deciphering Human Brain Activity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13936v1)
- **Authors**: Saydul Akbar Murad, Ashim Dahal, Nick Rahimi
- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancement of large language models like Gemini, GPT, and others, bridging the gap between the human brain and language processing has become an important area of focus. To address this challenge, researchers have developed various models to decode EEG signals into text. However, these models still face significant performance limitations. To overcome these shortcomings, we propose a new model, R1 Translator, which aims to improve the performance of EEG-to-text decoding. The R1 Translator model combines a bidirectional LSTM encoder with a pretrained transformer-based decoder, utilizing EEG features to produce high-quality text outputs. The model processes EEG embeddings through the LSTM to capture sequential dependencies, which are then fed into the transformer decoder for effective text generation. The R1 Translator excels in ROUGE metrics, outperforming both T5 (previous research) and Brain Translator. Specifically, R1 achieves a ROUGE-1 score of 38.00% (P), which is up to 9% higher than T5 (34.89%) and 3% better than Brain (35.69%). It also leads in ROUGE-L, with a F1 score of 32.51%, outperforming T5 by 3% (29.67%) and Brain by 2% (30.38%). In terms of CER, R1 achieves a CER of 0.5795, which is 2% lower than T5 (0.5917) and 4% lower than Brain (0.6001). Additionally, R1 performs better in WER with a score of 0.7280, outperforming T5 by 4.3% (0.7610) and Brain by 3.6% (0.7553). Code is available at https://github.com/Mmurrad/EEG-To-text.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "R1 Translator," a new model for decoding EEG signals into text. The model combines a bidirectional LSTM encoder with a pretrained transformer-based decoder (BART). The LSTM encoder extracts sequential dependencies from EEG embeddings, which are then fed into the BART decoder for text generation.  Experiments on the ZuCo dataset (versions 1 and 2) show that R1 Translator outperforms existing models, namely T5 Translator and Brain Translator, across various metrics like ROUGE, SacreBLEU, WER, and CER.  The paper emphasizes the two-stage fine-tuning strategy and demonstrates improved performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Performance Improvements:**  The paper presents clear evidence of improved performance compared to two baseline models across a range of metrics. The consistent outperformance across multiple datasets (ZuCo V1, ZuCo V2, and combined) strengthens the claims. The reported gains in ROUGE and BLEU scores, along with lower WER and CER, are statistically significant and compelling.
    * **Model Architecture:** The combination of an LSTM encoder with a pretrained BART decoder is a reasonable architectural choice. The LSTM effectively captures temporal dependencies in the EEG data, while BART provides strong language generation capabilities.  The two-stage fine-tuning approach seems sensible for adapting the pretrained BART model to the specific task.
    * **Thorough Evaluation:** The authors perform a thorough evaluation using multiple versions of the ZuCo dataset and several standard evaluation metrics. The inclusion of both "teacher forcing" and "non-teacher forcing" scenarios provides a more comprehensive assessment of the model's capabilities.
    * **Code Availability:** The availability of code enhances reproducibility and allows other researchers to build upon this work.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the performance gains are significant, the novelty of the approach is somewhat incremental.  Combining LSTMs with pretrained transformers is not a groundbreaking innovation. Many recent NLP models apply these approaches. The primary innovation appears to be the specific architecture configuration and training strategy tailored for EEG-to-text translation.
    * **Dataset Limitations:** The reliance on the ZuCo dataset is a significant limitation. As the authors themselves acknowledge, ZuCo may not fully represent the diversity of real-world EEG data. Generalization to other types of text and languages is not demonstrated. Furthermore, the nature of the ZuCo data limits the complexity and nuances that can be decoded; reading movie reviews and wikipedia articles is a fairly constrained task.
    * **Lack of Real-Time Evaluation:** The model is not tested in real-time or clinical settings. This limits the assessment of its practical applicability and robustness to noise and artifacts that might be present in real-world EEG recordings.  The current evaluation focuses on offline decoding of processed EEG data.
    * **Interpretability:**  The paper doesn't focus much on the interpretability of the model. Understanding how the model maps specific EEG patterns to specific textual outputs would be valuable.  A deeper analysis of the learned EEG representations and their relationship to linguistic elements is missing.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The paper would be significantly strengthened by ablation studies investigating the impact of various architectural choices and fine-tuning strategies. This would allow for a more thorough understanding of the individual components' contributions to the overall performance. For instance, what's the impact of the bidirectional LSTM versus a simple LSTM.

* **Significance:**
    * The work addresses an important problem with real-world implications for brain-computer interfaces and assistive technologies.
    * The improved performance compared to existing models suggests that this approach could be useful for enhancing communication for individuals with motor impairments.
    * The publicly available code facilitates further research and development in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid engineering effort with demonstrable performance improvements over existing methods for EEG-to-text translation. However, the limited novelty in the core architecture and reliance on a potentially narrow dataset temper the overall impact. While the results are encouraging, more work is needed to address the dataset limitations, enhance the model's interpretability, and validate its real-world applicability. Therefore, a score of 6 is appropriate.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### MLZero: A Multi-Agent System for End-to-end Machine Learning Automation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13941v1)
- **Authors**: Haoyang Fang, Boran Han, Nick Erickson, Xiyuan Zhang, Su Zhou, Anirudh Dagar, Jiani Zhang, Ali Caner Turkmen, Cuixiong Hu, Huzefa Rangwala, Ying Nian Wu, Bernie Wang, George Karypis
- **Abstract**: Existing AutoML systems have advanced the automation of machine learning (ML); however, they still require substantial manual configuration and expert input, particularly when handling multimodal data. We introduce MLZero, a novel multi-agent framework powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) that enables end-to-end ML automation across diverse data modalities with minimal human intervention. A cognitive perception module is first employed, transforming raw multimodal inputs into perceptual context that effectively guides the subsequent workflow. To address key limitations of LLMs, such as hallucinated code generation and outdated API knowledge, we enhance the iterative code generation process with semantic and episodic memory. MLZero demonstrates superior performance on MLE-Bench Lite, outperforming all competitors in both success rate and solution quality, securing six gold medals. Additionally, when evaluated on our Multimodal AutoML Agent Benchmark, which includes 25 more challenging tasks spanning diverse data modalities, MLZero outperforms the competing methods by a large margin with a success rate of 0.92 (+263.6\%) and an average rank of 2.28. Our approach maintains its robust effectiveness even with a compact 8B LLM, outperforming full-size systems from existing solutions.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "MLZero: A Multi-Agent System for End-to-end Machine Learning Automation" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MLZero, a novel multi-agent system designed for end-to-end machine learning automation across diverse data modalities with minimal human intervention. MLZero uses a hierarchical architecture powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) and incorporates a cognitive perception module, a semantic memory, and an episodic memory. The perception module transforms raw multimodal inputs into perceptual context. The semantic memory is used for enhanced knowledge retrieval, and the episodic memory for error detection and correction. The system iteratively generates and refines code, ultimately producing ready-to-use models and predictions. The authors evaluate MLZero on MLE-Bench Lite and on their new Multimodal AutoML Agent Benchmark, demonstrating superior performance in both success rate and solution quality compared to existing AutoML systems.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of a multi-agent architecture, a cognitive perception module, and the integration of semantic and episodic memory to facilitate end-to-end automation is a novel approach in the field of AutoML. The use of LLMs in AutoML is not entirely new, but MLZero's comprehensive architecture and specific memory modules are significant differentiators.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in existing AutoML systems, which often struggle to handle multimodal data effectively and require substantial manual configuration. By automating the entire ML lifecycle from data preprocessing to model building, MLZero has the potential to democratize ML and make it accessible to a wider audience.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Architecture:** MLZero's multi-agent design, coupled with the memory modules, provides a robust and flexible framework for handling diverse ML tasks.
    *   **End-to-End Automation:** The system automates the entire ML pipeline, reducing the need for human intervention and expertise.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results on both MLE-Bench Lite and the Multimodal AutoML Agent Benchmark demonstrate superior performance compared to existing systems. The introduction of a new benchmark is also a valuable contribution.
    *   **Effective Use of LLMs:** The paper addresses limitations of LLMs (like hallucination and outdated knowledge) through semantic and episodic memory.
    *   **Robustness demonstrated through experiments with a smaller (8B) LLM.**

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** The system heavily relies on LLMs, which can be computationally expensive and have biases. While the paper mitigates some LLM limitations, the system's overall performance is still tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLMs.
    *   **Black Box Nature:** Like many LLM-based systems, MLZero can be difficult to interpret. Understanding *why* the system makes certain decisions or generates specific code remains a challenge.
    *   **Limited Evaluation on Real-World Problems:** While the benchmarks used in the paper are diverse, further evaluation on complex, real-world problems would be valuable.
    *   **Limited discussion of how the design would handle adversarial attacks on the multimodal agents.**
    *   **Scalability and Generalizability:** The experimental results are promising, but it's unclear how well MLZero would scale to extremely large datasets or generalize to entirely new domains outside of the tested ones.

*   **Potential Influence:** MLZero's approach has the potential to significantly influence the field of AutoML. It demonstrates the power of multi-agent systems and structured memory in automating complex ML tasks. The paper could inspire further research into:

    *   Developing more interpretable and explainable LLM-based AutoML systems.
    *   Exploring new techniques for mitigating the limitations of LLMs in AutoML.
    *   Creating more robust and generalizable AutoML systems that can handle a wider range of real-world problems.
    *   Incorporating causal analysis to prevent unintended consequences of poorly configured models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of AutoML by presenting a novel architecture and demonstrating its effectiveness on challenging benchmarks. It addresses a critical gap in existing systems and has the potential to democratize ML. While there are some limitations related to LLM dependency and interpretability, the overall impact is substantial. The strong empirical results and the introduction of a new benchmark further strengthen the paper's contribution.

### Visual Instruction Bottleneck Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13946v1)
- **Authors**: Changdae Oh, Jiatong Li, Shawn Im, Yixuan Li
- **Abstract**: Despite widespread adoption, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) suffer performance degradation when encountering unfamiliar queries under distribution shifts. Existing methods to improve MLLM generalization typically require either more instruction data or larger advanced model architectures, both of which incur non-trivial human labor or computational costs. In this work, we take an alternative approach to enhance the robustness of MLLMs under distribution shifts, from a representation learning perspective. Inspired by the information bottleneck (IB) principle, we derive a variational lower bound of the IB for MLLMs and devise a practical implementation, Visual Instruction Bottleneck Tuning (Vittle). We then provide a theoretical justification of Vittle by revealing its connection to an information-theoretic robustness metric of MLLM. Empirical validation of three MLLMs on open-ended and closed-form question answering and object hallucination detection tasks over 45 datasets, including 30 shift scenarios, demonstrates that Vittle consistently improves the MLLM's robustness under shifts by pursuing the learning of a minimal sufficient representation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Vittle (Visual Instruction Bottleneck Tuning), a novel method to improve the robustness of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) under distribution shifts. Vittle leverages the information bottleneck (IB) principle by incorporating a bottleneck layer within the LLM architecture. This layer aims to learn minimal sufficient representations by discarding irrelevant information and retaining task-relevant signals. The authors theoretically justify Vittle's approach and empirically validate its effectiveness across a variety of tasks and datasets, including those involving different types of input perturbations and long-tail distributions. The experimental results demonstrate that Vittle consistently enhances robustness without sacrificing performance on standard benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the information bottleneck principle has been applied in other contexts, the paper makes a significant contribution by: (1) Formulating a variational lower bound of IB explicitly tailored to the complexities of autoregressive multimodal instruction tuning. (2) Providing a practical and scalable implementation (Vittle) that is integrated into the LLM architecture (LLM backbone itself) to influence representations directly. (3) Theoretically justifying and experimentally validating how Vittle enhances MLLM robustness under distribution shifts.  The key strength here is the application of IB *within* the instruction tuning of MLLMs, where IB constraints are enforced on representations to improve robustness.
*   **Significance:** MLLM robustness is a critical concern in real-world deployment. By offering a lightweight and theoretically grounded approach, Vittle addresses a key limitation of existing MLLMs that are known to be fragile under minor variations. The experiments are comprehensive, covering a wide range of shifts and tasks, and provide solid evidence of Vittle's benefits. The results demonstrate improvements across multiple MLLMs architectures.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical justification for the approach.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation across diverse tasks, shift types, and datasets.
    *   Practical and scalable implementation.
    *   Clear and well-written paper.
    *   Demonstrated compatibility with different MLLMs architectures.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the approach is lightweight, the training time increase is still present (upto 20%).
    *   The paper focuses mainly on the *internal* representations within the LLM itself. Although a modular approach, the choice and specifics of which layers to bottleneck might require architecture-specific tuning (i.e., layer 24 for a 7B model). The model could be more general with a training strategy that involves *more* layer-specific bottleneck layers.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on robust MLLM development. Vittle's IB-based approach provides a useful and effective framework for tackling distribution shift problems. The fact that it requires a relatively small computational cost and annotation effort compared to large-scale data augmentation makes it a valuable contribution.
*  **Discussion:** The fact that Vittle is effective at mitigating hallucinations, or what seems to be overreliance on a single modality suggests that there's an interesting discussion to be had about the trade-offs in these representation. While Vittle induces good inductive biases, it would be interesting to measure it with specific interpretability methods (e.g., concept activation vectors) so that it may be useful to inspect those features to analyze *why* that level of inductive bias is useful.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and valuable approach to improve the robustness of MLLMs under distribution shifts. While IB isn't a completely new concept, the way it's applied within MLLM instruction tuning, with theoretical grounding and strong empirical validation, makes this a significant contribution. The comprehensive experiments provide strong evidence of the method's effectiveness and the code will make this widely accessible to the community. While it is not a revolutionary breakthrough, the approach addresses a vital problem in a practical and effective way, thus deserves a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### FlashThink: An Early Exit Method For Efficient Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13949v1)
- **Authors**: Guochao Jiang, Guofeng Quan, Zepeng Ding, Ziqin Luo, Dixuan Wang, Zheng Hu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown impressive performance in reasoning tasks. However, LLMs tend to generate excessively long reasoning content, leading to significant computational overhead. Our observations indicate that even on simple problems, LLMs tend to produce unnecessarily lengthy reasoning content, which is against intuitive expectations. Preliminary experiments show that at a certain point during the generation process, the model is already capable of producing the correct solution without completing the full reasoning content. Therefore, we consider that the reasoning process of the model can be exited early to achieve the purpose of efficient reasoning. We introduce a verification model that identifies the exact moment when the model can stop reasoning and still provide the correct answer. Comprehensive experiments on four different benchmarks demonstrate that our proposed method, FlashThink, effectively shortens the reasoning content while preserving the model accuracy. For the Deepseek-R1 and QwQ-32B models, we reduced the length of reasoning content by 77.04% and 77.47%, respectively, without reducing the accuracy.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FlashThink: An Early Exit Method For Efficient Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FlashThink, a method designed to improve the efficiency of Large Language Models (LLMs) in reasoning tasks. The core idea is that LLMs often generate unnecessarily lengthy reasoning chains, even for simple problems. FlashThink addresses this by using a verification model to determine when the LLM has generated sufficient reasoning content to produce the correct answer, allowing for an early exit from the reasoning process.  The method splits reasoning content into chunks using delimiter tokens, then uses a verification model to decide after each chunk whether to continue or stop. The verification model can be a pre-existing one, or fine-tuned (FT2). The authors experiment with DeepSeek-R1 and QwQ series models on benchmarks like GSM8K, MATH, GPQA Diamond, and DROP. Their results show that FlashThink can significantly reduce the length of reasoning content while maintaining, and sometimes improving, accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of early exiting LLM reasoning chains is not entirely new. However, the authors present a relatively simple, model-agnostic method that doesn't require retraining the main LLM. The use of a separate verification model that can be further fine-tuned (FT2) is a practical approach to dynamically adjusting the reasoning process based on input and data distribution, which is beneficial. However, the approach is not ground-breaking, nor conceptually challenging; rather, it presents a practical engineering optimization to an existing problem.

*   **Significance:**  Improving the efficiency of LLMs is a crucial area of research. LLMs are computationally expensive, and reducing the length of reasoning chains can lead to significant savings in inference time and resources. The paper presents compelling empirical results showing that FlashThink can substantially reduce reasoning content length. The fact that FlashThink appears not only to maintain accuracy but even *improve* it in some cases is an intriguing finding. This suggests that eliminating extraneous "overthinking" can actually improve performance, implying that standard training methods might be encouraging LLMs to generate redundant or even distracting information. The empirical findings bolster the significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The method is relatively simple and easy to implement.
    *   The method is model-agnostic and doesn't require retraining the main LLM.
    *   The verification model can be fine-tuned for specific tasks.
    *   The paper provides clear and detailed experimental results.
    *   The paper explores multiple datasets with different difficulty levels.
    *   The code is clearly documented, and the authors have provided information on implementation details, which makes the replication straightforward.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The choice of delimiter tokens might influence the effectiveness of the method. The authors don't explore the sensitivity of the method to different delimiter choices.
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of *why* FlashThink improves accuracy in some cases. What characteristics of the reasoning process are being eliminated? Understanding this could lead to further improvements. It is not well-explained within the scope of the paper.
    *   The use of a verification model adds complexity. While they show it enhances FT's ability to reduce content length, it introduces another model to manage and optimize. In comparison to purely algorithmic optimization, the added complexity could be seen as a drawback.
    *   The limitations section notes they only discussed verification models implemented as generative models but large language models, which somewhat limits the scope of their findings, which would be better strengthened using different model architectures.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on efficient reasoning in LLMs. The idea of dynamically adjusting the reasoning process based on a verification signal is a valuable concept. The FlashThink method could be used as a building block for more sophisticated techniques. It could also inform the design of training methods that encourage LLMs to generate more concise and focused reasoning content.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

FlashThink presents a practical, empirically supported approach to a significant problem in LLM research. It offers a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating that early exit strategies, guided by a separate verification model, can improve the efficiency of reasoning tasks without sacrificing accuracy. The method's simplicity, model-agnostic nature, and the observed improvements in accuracy are its key strengths. However, the paper has a few weaknesses, including limited analysis of why certain model architectures improve accuracy, which influences its overall novelty, therefore it can't be considered as highly disruptive, and more so incremental.

### Through a Compressed Lens: Investigating the Impact of Quantization on LLM Explainability and Interpretability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13963v1)
- **Authors**: Qianli Wang, Mingyang Wang, Nils Feldhus, Simon Ostermann, Yuan Cao, Hinrich Schütze, Sebastian Möller, Vera Schmitt
- **Abstract**: Quantization methods are widely used to accelerate inference and streamline the deployment of large language models (LLMs). While prior research has extensively investigated the degradation of various LLM capabilities due to quantization, its effects on model explainability and interpretability, which are crucial for understanding decision-making processes, remain unexplored. To address this gap, we conduct comprehensive experiments using three common quantization techniques at distinct bit widths, in conjunction with two explainability methods, counterfactual examples and natural language explanations, as well as two interpretability approaches, knowledge memorization analysis and latent multi-hop reasoning analysis. We complement our analysis with a thorough user study, evaluating selected explainability methods. Our findings reveal that, depending on the configuration, quantization can significantly impact model explainability and interpretability. Notably, the direction of this effect is not consistent, as it strongly depends on (1) the quantization method, (2) the explainability or interpretability approach, and (3) the evaluation protocol. In some settings, human evaluation shows that quantization degrades explainability, while in others, it even leads to improvements. Our work serves as a cautionary tale, demonstrating that quantization can unpredictably affect model transparency. This insight has important implications for deploying LLMs in applications where transparency is a critical requirement.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of quantization techniques on the explainability and interpretability of Large Language Models (LLMs). It explores how different quantization methods (GPTQ, AWQ, and Integer Quantization) at varying bit widths affect the performance of two explainability methods (counterfactual examples and natural language explanations) and two interpretability approaches (knowledge memorization analysis and latent multi-hop reasoning analysis). The study combines automatic evaluations with a user study to assess the quality of explanations generated by quantized LLMs.  The findings reveal that quantization can significantly and unpredictably affect model transparency, with the direction of the effect depending on the quantization method, explainability/interpretability approach, and evaluation protocol.  The authors find that automatic metrics overestimate the extent of degradation, and human evaluations reveal inconsistencies with automatic scores.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a gap in the literature by examining the impact of quantization on explainability and interpretability, which are crucial for understanding and trusting LLMs. While quantization itself is a well-established area, and some work has been done on quantization of LLMs, this paper is novel in the specific focus on *explainability* and *interpretability* in the *context of quantization*. Existing work generally focuses on task performance, bias, or other capabilities. The consideration of different explainability and interpretability methods, different types of quantization, and a human evaluation adds to the novelty. The observation that quantization can sometimes *improve* explainability is also a novel finding.
*   **Significance:**  The findings have significant implications for the deployment of LLMs in scenarios where transparency is critical (e.g., healthcare, finance). The paper demonstrates that simply deploying quantized models without considering the impact on explainability can lead to unintended consequences.  Highlighting the unreliability of automatic metrics and the divergence from human evaluations provides a cautionary tale for researchers and practitioners.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-motivated and clearly articulates the research gap.
    *   The experimental design is comprehensive, covering a range of LLMs, quantization methods, explainability/interpretability approaches, and evaluation metrics.
    *   The inclusion of a user study adds valuable insights that complement the automatic evaluations.
    *   The paper is well-written and easy to follow.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments are limited to English datasets, which limits the generalizability of the findings to multilingual settings.
    *   The study only considers weight-only quantization and not other forms, such as weight-activation quantization or quantization-aware training.
    *   The chosen explainability and interpretability methods, while representative, are not exhaustive.  There are many other methods that could have been explored.
    *   The scope of LLM model families is limited (Qwen and Llama, but not others like Mistral).
    *   While the user study is valuable, the sample size is relatively small and could be expanded for stronger statistical power.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in LLM compression and deployment by raising awareness of the importance of considering explainability and interpretability. It could also encourage the development of new quantization techniques that are specifically designed to preserve or even enhance model transparency.  It may also prompt more critical evaluation of automatic metrics in the context of explainability and the use of human-centered evaluation methods.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper is not a groundbreaking theoretical breakthrough, it makes a valuable empirical contribution to the field. The novelty lies in the specific focus on explainability and interpretability under quantization, the breadth of the experimental setup, and the inclusion of human evaluations. The findings have significant practical implications for LLM deployment. The weaknesses are primarily related to the scope of the experiments. For example, restricting to one language, limiting the exploration of interpretability methods, and focusing only on post-training quantization limit the scope of the experiments. Therefore, the score should reflect the valuable and insightful contribution the paper makes while acknowledging limitations related to the scope.

### Truth or Twist? Optimal Model Selection for Reliable Label Flipping Evaluation in LLM-based Counterfactuals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13972v1)
- **Authors**: Qianli Wang, Van Bach Nguyen, Nils Feldhus, Luis Felipe Villa-Arenas, Christin Seifert, Sebastian Möller, Vera Schmitt
- **Abstract**: Counterfactual examples are widely employed to enhance the performance and robustness of large language models (LLMs) through counterfactual data augmentation (CDA). However, the selection of the judge model used to evaluate label flipping, the primary metric for assessing the validity of generated counterfactuals for CDA, yields inconsistent results. To decipher this, we define four types of relationships between the counterfactual generator and judge models. Through extensive experiments involving two state-of-the-art LLM-based methods, three datasets, five generator models, and 15 judge models, complemented by a user study (n = 90), we demonstrate that judge models with an independent, non-fine-tuned relationship to the generator model provide the most reliable label flipping evaluations. Relationships between the generator and judge models, which are closely aligned with the user study for CDA, result in better model performance and robustness. Nevertheless, we find that the gap between the most effective judge models and the results obtained from the user study remains considerably large. This suggests that a fully automated pipeline for CDA may be inadequate and requires human intervention.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of judge model selection on the reliability of label flipping evaluation in LLM-based counterfactual data augmentation (CDA). It defines four types of relationships between the counterfactual generator and the judge model (same model, same family, independent with/without fine-tuning, and distilled models). Through extensive experiments using state-of-the-art LLM-based methods, various datasets, generator models, and judge models, complemented by a user study, the authors demonstrate that judge models with an independent, non-fine-tuned relationship to the generator provide the most reliable label flipping evaluations.  The paper finds that aligning the generator and judge model relationship with user study results improves model performance and robustness. They conclude that a fully automated pipeline for CDA may be inadequate and requires human intervention due to the discrepancy between automated evaluation and human judgment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic investigation of the impact of judge model selection criteria on label flipping reliability in LLM-based CDA. Prior work has explored counterfactual generation and evaluation, but this paper provides a detailed analysis of how the *relationship* between generator and judge models affects the quality of the evaluation and, subsequently, downstream performance when using CDA. The specific categorization of these relationships (same, same family, independent with/without fine-tuning, distilled) is a well-defined framing that helps analyze the problem.
*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for researchers and practitioners using CDA to improve LLMs.  The paper demonstrates that relying on superficially "good" judge models (e.g., those with high downstream performance) can be misleading, and that carefully choosing a judge model with an independent relationship to the generator is crucial for reliable label flipping evaluation. This can lead to more effective CDA and, ultimately, more robust and higher-performing models. The identification of the need for human intervention in the evaluation process is a practical and valuable insight, highlighting the limitations of relying solely on automated pipelines.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Experimental Design:** The paper presents a well-designed and executed experimental setup. The authors explore a diverse range of models, datasets, and relationships between generator and judge models.
    *   **User Study Validation:** The inclusion of a user study provides valuable ground truth and validates the findings derived from automated evaluation metrics. The user study adds significant weight to the conclusions.
    *   **Clear and Organized Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it easy to follow the research question, methodology, and results. The definitions are clear, and the tables effectively summarize the key findings.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers practical guidelines for selecting appropriate judge models for label flipping evaluation, benefiting researchers and practitioners involved in CDA.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scope of Languages:** The study is limited to English datasets, which may limit the generalizability of the findings to other languages.
    *   **Model Families:** The study primarily focuses on models from the Qwen and Llama families, potentially overlooking nuances specific to other model architectures. While they include older architecture like BERT and RoBERTa, these are not used as generator models.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** While label flip rate is a common metric, there could be discussion on alternative or complementary evaluation techniques to more fully characterize the quality of counterfactuals. The paper mentions other factors like similarity and fluency in the Related Work, but does not include metrics for evaluating these in their experimental results.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:** While the paper mentions difficulties in identifying label flipping, a more in-depth discussion of specific failure cases and the limitations of LLMs in capturing nuances would be valuable.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the design and evaluation of CDA pipelines, leading to more reliable and effective training of LLMs. By highlighting the importance of careful judge model selection, the paper can help researchers avoid common pitfalls and improve the quality of their results.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**
The paper offers a novel and significant contribution to the field of counterfactual data augmentation. The systematic investigation of judge model selection, coupled with the validation through user studies, provides valuable insights and practical guidance. The findings can influence how researchers and practitioners approach CDA, leading to more effective model training. While the limitations related to language and model family scope exist, they do not diminish the core contribution and the potential influence of the paper on the field. A score of 8 reflects the paper's strengths in research design, user study validation, and practical implications while acknowledging the limitations that prevent it from reaching a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Toward Effective Reinforcement Learning Fine-Tuning for Medical VQA in Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13973v1)
- **Authors**: Wenhui Zhu, Xuanzhao Dong, Xin Li, Peijie Qiu, Xiwen Chen, Abolfazl Razi, Aris Sotiras, Yi Su, Yalin Wang
- **Abstract**: Recently, reinforcement learning (RL)-based tuning has shifted the trajectory of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), particularly following the introduction of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). However, directly applying it to medical tasks remains challenging for achieving clinically grounded model behavior. Motivated by the need to align model response with clinical expectations, we investigate four critical dimensions that affect the effectiveness of RL-based tuning in medical visual question answering (VQA): base model initialization strategy, the role of medical semantic alignment, the impact of length-based rewards on long-chain reasoning, and the influence of bias. We conduct extensive experiments to analyze these factors for medical MLLMs, providing new insights into how models are domain-specifically fine-tuned. Additionally, our results also demonstrate that GRPO-based RL tuning consistently outperforms standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) in both accuracy and reasoning quality.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the OCR output.

**Summary**

The paper investigates the application of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO)-based reinforcement learning (RL) to medical visual question answering (VQA) using multimodal large language models (MLLMs). The authors analyze several key aspects affecting the effectiveness of RL fine-tuning: initialization strategy (training from scratch vs. fine-tuning), medical semantic alignment, the impact of length-based rewards on reasoning, and the influence of bias. They conduct experiments using the Qwen2-VL-2B model on a subset of the PMC-VQA dataset and demonstrate that GRPO-based RL tuning outperforms standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT). The paper also highlights the importance of incorporating medical semantic knowledge and addresses potential biases introduced by normalization techniques. Finally, it shows the utility of unbiased GRPO.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper addresses a relevant and timely problem: adapting powerful language models to specialized domains like medicine, where accuracy and clinical relevance are paramount. The application of GRPO to medical VQA is a logical step, given the recent successes of GRPO in other multimodal tasks.  However, the core idea isn't radically novel, as other works have already explored RL in similar contexts.

The *primary* novel contribution is a systematic analysis of different factors influencing the effectiveness of GRPO-based RL *specifically* for medical VQA.  The investigation of initialization strategies (fine-tuning vs. scratch), medical semantic alignment, the pitfalls of length-based rewards, and bias mitigation using Dr.GRPO are all valuable contributions. The analysis is *insightful*, and the results are well-supported by experiments.

*Strengths:*

*   **Systematic Analysis:** The paper's strength lies in its structured and thorough exploration of the design space for RL-based medical VQA tuning. It doesn't just apply GRPO; it dissects *how* and *why* certain design choices impact performance.
*   **Practical Insights:** The paper provides actionable insights for practitioners working on medical MLLMs. The findings regarding medical semantic alignment and bias mitigation are particularly valuable.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments are well-designed, and the results clearly demonstrate the superiority of GRPO-based RL over SFT in this context.
* Addresses an important issue of trustworthiness of LM outputs, and the need to promote clinical alignment.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the analysis is valuable, the core methodology (GRPO) is not new. The application to medical VQA is a relatively straightforward extension.
*   **Limited Model Scope:** The experiments are primarily conducted on Qwen2-VL-2B, which is a relatively small model. The generalizability of the findings to larger, more powerful MLLMs is not fully established.
*   **Dataset Subset:** Using a subset of PMC-VQA limits the scope. While necessary for focused evaluation, it might not capture the full complexity of real-world medical data.
*   **Limited comparison:** While it shows GRPO-based tuning superior to SFT, it should include other recent relevant techniques, such as parameter-efficient fine-tuning.

*Impact and Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence the development of more accurate and reliable medical MLLMs. The insights on medical semantic alignment and bias mitigation could be incorporated into future RL-based tuning frameworks. The work also highlights the limitations of length-based rewards and the importance of careful reward design.
The paper provides a foundation for future research on RL-based medical VQA. It serves as a guideline of best-practices, where other studies could build upon.

*Justification for Score:*

Considering the paper's strengths and weaknesses, a score of 6 seems appropriate. The research provides valuable insights into the application of GRPO to medical VQA. Although it's a natural extension of existing techniques, the systematic analysis and practical insights are valuable contributions. The use of a well-known model and the lack of comparison again other techniques reduces the overall score. While the scope is somewhat limited by the model size and dataset subset, the work is well-executed and addresses an important problem.

Score: 6

- **Score**: 6/10

### DRP: Distilled Reasoning Pruning with Skill-aware Step Decomposition for Efficient Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13975v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxuan Jiang, Dawei Li, Frank Ferraro
- **Abstract**: While Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have demonstrated success in complex reasoning tasks through long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, their inference often involves excessively verbose reasoning traces, resulting in substantial inefficiency. To address this, we propose Distilled Reasoning Pruning (DRP), a hybrid framework that combines inference-time pruning with tuning-based distillation, two widely used strategies for efficient reasoning. DRP uses a teacher model to perform skill-aware step decomposition and content pruning, and then distills the pruned reasoning paths into a student model, enabling it to reason both efficiently and accurately. Across several challenging mathematical reasoning datasets, we find that models trained with DRP achieve substantial improvements in token efficiency without sacrificing accuracy. Specifically, DRP reduces average token usage on GSM8K from 917 to 328 while improving accuracy from 91.7% to 94.1%, and achieves a 43% token reduction on AIME with no performance drop. Further analysis shows that aligning the reasoning structure of training CoTs with the student's reasoning capacity is critical for effective knowledge transfer and performance gains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DRP: Distilled Reasoning Pruning with Skill-aware Step Decomposition for Efficient Large Reasoning Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Distilled Reasoning Pruning (DRP), a hybrid framework to improve the efficiency of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) in complex reasoning tasks. DRP combines inference-time pruning with tuning-based distillation. It utilizes a teacher model to perform skill-aware step decomposition of the student model's reasoning trace, prunes redundant or unnecessary steps, and then distills the resulting concise reasoning paths into a smaller student model.  This allows the student model to reason both efficiently (fewer tokens) and accurately. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of DRP on mathematical reasoning datasets, showing significant reductions in token usage without compromising (and often improving) accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of combining pruning and distillation is not entirely novel. However, the authors' specific approach – skill-aware step decomposition followed by teacher-guided pruning within the student model's original reasoning structure – represents a worthwhile incremental innovation.  The skill-aware decomposition seems to be the key to the paper’s successes and separates it from previous simpler methods. The study also clearly shows the limits of just using short CoTs in isolation for direct distilation, which again contributes to its merit.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in the field of LRMs: the tendency to overthink and generate excessively verbose reasoning chains, which increases computational cost and may hurt accuracy.  The demonstrated improvements in token efficiency without sacrificing accuracy are compelling and suggest that DRP could be a useful technique for deploying LRMs in resource-constrained environments. The finding that aligning training CoTs with the student's reasoning capacity is vital is also an important contribution.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are thorough and well-designed. The authors test DRP on a range of mathematical reasoning datasets, including out-of-domain benchmarks, providing strong evidence of its effectiveness and generalizability.  The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of the framework.
    * **Clear Explanation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the DRP framework and its underlying principles. The figures and tables are informative and help to illustrate the key concepts and results. The code implementations are made available which is a plus.
    * **Practical Implications:** The DRP framework could be readily applied to existing LRMs to improve their efficiency.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Teacher Model Dependency:** The framework relies on a powerful teacher model (e.g., GPT-4o) to perform skill-aware step decomposition and pruning. This introduces a dependency on external resources.  It might also raise concerns about whether the student model truly "understands" the reasoning process or simply imitates the teacher's pruned reasoning paths.
    * **Generalizability beyond mathematical reasoning:**  The paper focuses exclusively on mathematical reasoning tasks. It is unclear how well DRP would generalize to other types of reasoning, such as common-sense reasoning or logical inference.
    * **Fine-grained evaluation:** A detailed human evaluation of the coherence and quality of the generated rationales after DRP could significantly strengthen the contribution. While the quantitative results are convincing, a qualitative analysis would offer deeper insights.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LRM efficiency by introducing DRP, a practical and well-validated framework for distilling and pruning reasoning traces. The skill-aware step decomposition is a key innovation that improves the effectiveness of the pruning process. The experimental results are compelling, and the analysis provides insights into the underlying mechanisms of DRP. However, the reliance on a strong teacher model and the limited scope of the evaluation (mathematical reasoning only) somewhat limits the paper's overall impact.  While the combination of distillation and pruning has been explored before, the novel skill-aware decomposition combined with solid empirical validation warrants a high rating.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Combining Deterministic Enhanced Conditions with Dual-Streaming Encoding for Diffusion-Based Speech Enhancement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13983v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Shi, Xugang Lu, Kazuki Shimada, Tatsuya Kawahara
- **Abstract**: Diffusion-based speech enhancement (SE) models need to incorporate correct prior knowledge as reliable conditions to generate accurate predictions. However, providing reliable conditions using noisy features is challenging. One solution is to use features enhanced by deterministic methods as conditions. However, the information distortion and loss caused by deterministic methods might affect the diffusion process. In this paper, we first investigate the effects of using different deterministic SE models as conditions for diffusion. We validate two conditions depending on whether the noisy feature was used as part of the condition: one using only the deterministic feature (deterministic-only), and the other using both deterministic and noisy features (deterministic-noisy). Preliminary investigation found that using deterministic enhanced conditions improves hearing experiences on real data, while the choice between using deterministic-only or deterministic-noisy conditions depends on the deterministic models. Based on these findings, we propose a dual-streaming encoding Repair-Diffusion Model for SE (DERDM-SE) to more effectively utilize both conditions. Moreover, we found that fine-grained deterministic models have greater potential in objective evaluation metrics, while UNet-based deterministic models provide more stable diffusion performance. Therefore, in the DERDM-SE, we propose a deterministic model that combines coarse- and fine-grained processing. Experimental results on CHiME4 show that the proposed models effectively leverage deterministic models to achieve better SE evaluation scores, along with more stable performance compared to other diffusion-based SE models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the challenge of providing reliable conditions for diffusion-based speech enhancement (SE) models. It investigates the impact of using deterministic SE models (DCCRN, DPRNN, TF-GridNet) as conditions for diffusion, exploring both deterministic-only and deterministic-noisy conditions. The authors propose a "Repair-Diffusion Model for SE" (RDM-SE) that explicitly incorporates deterministic repair into the encoder, mitigating distortions caused by the deterministic models.  The RDM-SE uses dual-streaming encoding to better utilize both deterministic-only and deterministic-noisy features. Furthermore, they introduce a granularity progressive deterministic model called Coarse Fisrt then Fine Enhancement (COFFEE) which combines coarse and fine-grained processing within a UNet structure to improve performance and stability of diffusion. Experiments on the CHiME4 dataset demonstrate that the proposed models effectively leverage deterministic models to achieve better SE evaluation scores and stable performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents several novel components:

*   **Systematic Investigation of Deterministic Conditions:** While prior work has explored combining deterministic and diffusion models, this paper's systematic investigation of different deterministic SE architectures (DCCRN, DPRNN, TF-GridNet) as conditions is a valuable contribution. The analysis of deterministic-only vs. deterministic-noisy conditions is insightful.
*   **Repair-Diffusion Model (RDM-SE):** The RDM-SE with its deterministic repair mechanism is a novel approach to explicitly address distortions introduced by deterministic models used as conditions. The frequency-axis downsampling choice to preserve deterministic information for repair is a well-reasoned design decision.
*   **Dual-Streaming Encoding:**  The dual-stream encoding to leverage both deterministic-only and deterministic-noisy conditions is a clever approach that may improve speech perception.
*   **Granularity Progressive Deterministic Model (COFFEE):** Combining coarse and fine-grained processing is a good strategy to address the stability issues of fine-grained models when used with diffusion methods.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a critical challenge in diffusion-based SE: providing accurate conditions. By intelligently integrating deterministic enhancement, it demonstrates improvements in both objective metrics and subjective listening experiences.  The work highlights the importance of carefully considering the characteristics of the deterministic models used as conditions and provides practical strategies for improving the performance and stability of diffusion-based SE.

**Strengths:**

*   **Thorough Investigation:** The paper presents a comprehensive experimental evaluation on a standard dataset (CHiME4).
*   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The authors clearly articulate the problem of noisy conditions in diffusion-based SE and the limitations of using deterministic models directly.
*   **Well-Justified Design:**  The architectural choices in RDM-SE and COFFEE are well-motivated and aligned with the paper's core arguments.
*   **Good Writing Quality:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** While the RDM-SE demonstrates improvements, the magnitude of improvement might be considered incremental, especially when compared to SOTA deterministic models.
*   **Complexity:** The proposed architecture is relatively complex, which may hinder its adoption.
*   **Limited Datasets:** While CHiME4 is a widely used dataset, it would have been good to see results on other challenging datasets as well.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence the field of diffusion-based SE by:

*   Providing valuable insights into the use of deterministic models as conditions.
*   Introducing a new architecture (RDM-SE) that addresses the limitations of existing approaches.
*   Highlighting the importance of deterministic repair in diffusion models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-executed and insightful study on a relevant problem in diffusion-based speech enhancement. The novel components contribute to a better understanding of how to leverage deterministic models as conditions and propose a promising architecture. However, the improvements are somewhat incremental, and the complexity of the architecture might limit its widespread adoption. The results shown on multiple types of real data are pretty convincing. Therefore, the score is:

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Hallucination Tax of Reinforcement Finetuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13988v1)
- **Authors**: Linxin Song, Taiwei Shi, Jieyu Zhao
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement finetuning (RFT) has become a standard approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, its impact on model trustworthiness remains underexplored. In this work, we identify and systematically study a critical side effect of RFT, which we term the hallucination tax: a degradation in refusal behavior causing models to produce hallucinated answers to unanswerable questions confidently. To investigate this, we introduce SUM (Synthetic Unanswerable Math), a high-quality dataset of unanswerable math problems designed to probe models' ability to recognize an unanswerable question by reasoning from the insufficient or ambiguous information. Our results show that standard RFT training could reduce model refusal rates by more than 80%, which significantly increases model's tendency to hallucinate. We further demonstrate that incorporating just 10% SUM during RFT substantially restores appropriate refusal behavior, with minimal accuracy trade-offs on solvable tasks. Crucially, this approach enables LLMs to leverage inference-time compute to reason about their own uncertainty and knowledge boundaries, improving generalization not only to out-of-domain math problems but also to factual question answering tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper identifies and systematically studies a side effect of reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) of large language models (LLMs), which they term the "hallucination tax." This refers to the degradation in refusal behavior after RFT, causing models to confidently produce hallucinated answers to unanswerable questions. The authors introduce SUM (Synthetic Unanswerable Math), a dataset of unanswerable math problems, to investigate this issue. They show that standard RFT can reduce model refusal rates, increasing the tendency to hallucinate.  The paper demonstrates that incorporating a small amount of SUM data (10%) during RFT restores appropriate refusal behavior with minimal accuracy trade-offs on solvable tasks. Crucially, the authors show this approach allows models to reason about their uncertainty and knowledge boundaries, improving generalization to out-of-domain tasks like factual QA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Important Problem:** The paper addresses a critical and often overlooked aspect of RFT: its impact on model trustworthiness and epistemic humility.  This is increasingly relevant as LLMs are deployed in high-stakes applications.
    *   **Systematic Study:** The authors move beyond anecdotal evidence of degraded refusal behavior and provide a systematic and quantitative analysis of the hallucination tax.
    *   **SUM Dataset:** The introduction of the SUM dataset is a valuable contribution.  It provides a controlled and high-quality benchmark for evaluating and mitigating the hallucination tax in a reasoning-based context.  The design principles behind SUM (e.g., subtle information gaps) are well-considered.
    *   **Effective Mitigation Strategy:** The proposed strategy of augmenting RFT with SUM data is simple, effective, and generalizable. The small amount of SUM data needed (10%) makes it practical.
    *   **Generalization:** The demonstration that SUM training improves refusal behavior in out-of-domain tasks (factual QA) is a strong indicator of the method's potential.
    *   **Rigorous Experimental Setup:** The paper presents a comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple LLMs, datasets, and training regimes.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the concepts, methods, and results.
    *   **Good Discussion:** The paper clearly identifies the implications of the hallucination tax and possible directions to mitigate this issue.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Domain Specificity of SUM:** While the generalization results are promising, the SUM dataset is limited to mathematical reasoning. The extent to which this approach translates to other domains where reasoning differs remains an open question. Other reasoning contexts such as counterfactual or common sense could potentially benefit from similar analyses.
    *   **Limited Negative Impact Evaluation:** While the paper highlights that SUM has minimal accuracy loss, further work to refine SUM generation so that the impact can be more minimized could prove valuable in many application settings.
    *   **Rule-Based Reward Function:** The study relies on a simple rule-based reward function in the RFT setup. More advanced reward shaping techniques or learned reward models could potentially lead to even better results and a more nuanced understanding of the trade-offs.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Insights:** While the paper provides a strong empirical demonstration, it offers relatively little in the way of theoretical explanation for why RFT degrades refusal behavior or why SUM is an effective mitigation strategy. Further theoretical analysis could strengthen the paper's contribution.
    *   **Dependency on the "I don't know" response.** The method used here depends heavily on the model producing the text "I don't know", which might not be a universal response if there are different instructions set. It would be more beneficial if the models could abstain without replying in such a specific way.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper demonstrates that this problem exists and provides a methodology for identifying and solving such a problem, making this of medium novelty and importance. The identification and systematic study of the hallucination tax in RFT is novel and significant. It challenges the assumption that improving reasoning capabilities with RFT automatically leads to more reliable LLMs. The SUM dataset and the proposed mitigation strategy provide concrete tools for researchers and practitioners to address this important issue. The generalization results to out-of-domain tasks suggest that the benefits of SUM training extend beyond mathematical reasoning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution by highlighting a critical side effect of RFT and providing a practical solution. While the domain specificity of SUM and the limited theoretical insights are drawbacks, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The paper is well-executed, clearly presented, and addresses an increasingly important problem in the field of LLMs.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### When LLMs meet open-world graph learning: a new perspective for unlabeled data uncertainty
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13989v1)
- **Authors**: Yanzhe Wen, Xunkai Li, Qi Zhang, Zhu Lei, Guang Zeng, Rong-Hua Li, Guoren Wang
- **Abstract**: Recently, large language models (LLMs) have significantly advanced text-attributed graph (TAG) learning. However, existing methods inadequately handle data uncertainty in open-world scenarios, especially concerning limited labeling and unknown-class nodes. Prior solutions typically rely on isolated semantic or structural approaches for unknown-class rejection, lacking effective annotation pipelines. To address these limitations, we propose Open-world Graph Assistant (OGA), an LLM-based framework that combines adaptive label traceability, which integrates semantics and topology for unknown-class rejection, and a graph label annotator to enable model updates using newly annotated nodes. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate OGA's effectiveness and practicality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Open-World Graph Assistant (OGA), a novel framework designed to address data uncertainty in text-attributed graphs (TAGs), particularly the issue of limited labeling in open-world environments. OGA uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate the processing of unlabeled nodes by first employing an adaptive label traceability (ALT) method for unknown-class rejection, which integrates both semantic and topological information.  Subsequently, it introduces a graph label annotator (GLA) for annotating unknown-class nodes, enabling them to contribute to model training. The authors create a benchmark, define evaluation metrics, and perform experiments to show the effectiveness and practicality of OGA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques and the overall pipeline for handling unlabeled data in open-world graph learning, particularly focusing on the under-explored task of unknown class annotation (UCA). Integrating LLMs for both known class classification/rejection and UCA is a relatively fresh approach. Adaptive Label Traceability is a notable concept for UCR by smoothly incorporating both semantics and structure. The automated end-to-end pipeline is a distinct contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical problem in graph machine learning – dealing with unlabeled data and open-world assumptions. By automating the process of identifying and annotating unknown classes, the research has the potential to reduce the need for manual labeling, which is a costly bottleneck in real-world applications. The proposed framework shows potential for improving graph ML models' adaptability and performance in dynamic environments. The systematic benchmark and standardized performance metrics contribute to future research by establishing a foundation for evaluations in this domain.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Formulation:** Clearly defines the problem of unlabeled data uncertainty in TAGs.
    *   **Methodology:** The combination of ALT and GLA is well-motivated and reasonably explained.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:**  The paper includes extensive experiments covering various aspects (known-class classification, unknown-class identification, annotation quality, and post-annotation performance).
    *   **Theoretical Analysis:** Includes some theoretical justification for the proposed methods.
    *   **Comprehensive benchmark and evaluation:** The authors have standardized 5 performance metrics from 4 key aspects which enhances the contribution of the work.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The framework is complex, involving multiple components (ALT, GLA, LLMs).
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework relies heavily on LLMs, raising concerns about computational cost and the potential impact of LLM biases on the results. While the experiments demonstrated impressive numbers, the model is likely to perform differently (probably worse) if a different LLM is used.
    *   **Limited comparison in theory**: The theory comparison between the methods is not as thorough as the experimental work. A deeper theory comparison could justify the score increase.
    *   **Limited Ablation**: The ablation study could provide a deeper insight into the model. It could be improved by removing both the LLM and topology parts of the model at the same time.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by directing attention towards LLM-enhanced open-world graph learning frameworks, particularly those focused on automating unlabeled data handling. The proposed benchmark and metrics could be adopted by other researchers in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel framework addressing a relevant problem in the field of graph machine learning. While the framework is complex and relies heavily on LLMs, the thorough experimental evaluation and theoretical justification provide evidence of its effectiveness. It also introduces standardized benchmark and evaluation metrics. The work has limitations, but it makes a significant contribution by opening up a new research direction and establishing a practical approach for automating data annotation in open-world graph learning scenarios.
