### DecIF: Improving Instruction-Following through Meta-Decomposition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13990v1)
- **Authors**: Tingfeng Hui, Pengyu Zhu, Bowen Ping, Ling Tang, Yaqi Zhang, Sen Su
- **Abstract**: Instruction-following has emerged as a crucial capability for large language models (LLMs). However, existing approaches often rely on pre-existing documents or external resources to synthesize instruction-following data, which limits their flexibility and generalizability. In this paper, we introduce DecIF, a fully autonomous, meta-decomposition guided framework that generates diverse and high-quality instruction-following data using only LLMs. DecIF is grounded in the principle of decomposition. For instruction generation, we guide LLMs to iteratively produce various types of meta-information, which are then combined with response constraints to form well-structured and semantically rich instructions. We further utilize LLMs to detect and resolve potential inconsistencies within the generated instructions. Regarding response generation, we decompose each instruction into atomic-level evaluation criteria, enabling rigorous validation and the elimination of inaccurate instruction-response pairs. Extensive experiments across a wide range of scenarios and settings demonstrate DecIF's superior performance on instruction-following tasks. Further analysis highlights its strong flexibility, scalability, and generalizability in automatically synthesizing high-quality instruction data.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DecIF: Improving Instruction-Following through Meta-Decomposition":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DecIF, a novel framework for generating high-quality instruction-following data for large language models (LLMs).  DecIF operates autonomously, using only LLMs to synthesize data without relying on pre-existing documents or external resources.  It achieves this through a two-stage process: 1) Instruction Synthesis, where instructions are decomposed into meta-information (domains, requests, scenarios) to control diversity and richness, and 2) Response Construction, where responses are generated and rigorously evaluated based on atomic-level evaluation criteria derived from the instructions. The framework includes consistency checks for instructions and response filtering to ensure quality. The authors demonstrate that LLMs trained on data generated by DecIF outperform existing methods on several instruction-following benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable and novel approach to instruction-following data generation. DecIF's strength lies in its complete autonomy and reliance on internal LLM capabilities, making it highly flexible and scalable. The meta-decomposition strategy is a strong contribution, allowing for fine-grained control over the diversity and contextual richness of the generated instructions.  The rigorous evaluation process further strengthens the quality of the dataset.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The fully autonomous approach to instruction-following data generation is a significant departure from methods that rely on external resources. The meta-decomposition technique offers a novel and effective way to control the generation process.
*   **Significance:**  The demonstrated improvements on instruction-following benchmarks highlight the potential for DecIF to enhance LLM capabilities. The data generated by DecIF has the potential to improve LLM alignment, which is a key challenge in the field.
*   **Flexibility and Scalability:** The approach is highly flexible as it can be used with different base LLMs and does not depend on specific resources. This makes it easily scalable for generating large datasets.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The atomic-level evaluation and filtering strategy in the Response Construction stage adds to the robustness and quality of generated data.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **LLM Dependence:**  While the autonomy of DecIF is a strength, it also makes it heavily reliant on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. The quality of the generated data is ultimately limited by the LLMs' understanding and generation abilities. As such it can have a hard time in generating datasets requiring very precise or niche real-world understanding.
*   **Filtering Bias:** As described in the limitations the current validation process may bias the dataset toward less complex instructions. There is opportunity in better instruction generation strategies in the future.
*   **Single-Turn Focus:** The current implementation focuses on single-turn instruction-following. Extending DecIF to multi-turn dialogues would further increase its applicability.
*   **Evaluation of Generalization:** While the paper evaluates the impact of DecIF generated data on instruction-following, it could be strengthened with more extensive evaluations of how this data affects other LLM capabilities, such as reasoning and knowledge retrieval.
*   **Limited Exploration of Hyperparameters:** While the appendix details prompt templates, it would benefit from further details on the experimental sweep used for the prompt selection and to determine parameters such as the temperature, top_p, and the number of requested samples.

**Overall:**

DecIF represents a significant step forward in instruction-following data generation. While it has some limitations, its strengths in autonomy, flexibility, and rigorous evaluation make it a valuable contribution to the field. The meta-decomposition strategy and the high quality of the generated data have the potential to influence the development of more capable and aligned LLMs.

**Score: 8.0**

**Rationale:**

DecIF demonstrates strong novelty and significance in its approach to instruction-following data generation. The methodology is well-reasoned and empirically supported with solid results. However, the reliance on LLM capabilities and the limited scope of the evaluation prevent it from achieving a higher score. The identified weaknesses provide clear directions for future research to address and strengthen the framework. As future studies can demonstrate the ability of this system to generate high-quality niche information or multi-step conversations, the impact of this paper would likely be increased.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Divide by Question, Conquer by Agent: SPLIT-RAG with Question-Driven Graph Partitioning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13994v1)
- **Authors**: Ruiyi Yang, Hao Xue, Imran Razzak, Hakim Hacid, Flora D. Salim
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems empower large language models (LLMs) with external knowledge, yet struggle with efficiency-accuracy trade-offs when scaling to large knowledge graphs. Existing approaches often rely on monolithic graph retrieval, incurring unnecessary latency for simple queries and fragmented reasoning for complex multi-hop questions. To address these challenges, this paper propose SPLIT-RAG, a multi-agent RAG framework that addresses these limitations with question-driven semantic graph partitioning and collaborative subgraph retrieval. The innovative framework first create Semantic Partitioning of Linked Information, then use the Type-Specialized knowledge base to achieve Multi-Agent RAG. The attribute-aware graph segmentation manages to divide knowledge graphs into semantically coherent subgraphs, ensuring subgraphs align with different query types, while lightweight LLM agents are assigned to partitioned subgraphs, and only relevant partitions are activated during retrieval, thus reduce search space while enhancing efficiency. Finally, a hierarchical merging module resolves inconsistencies across subgraph-derived answers through logical verifications. Extensive experimental validation demonstrates considerable improvements compared to existing approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Divide by Question, Conquer by Agent: SPLIT-RAG with Question-Driven Graph Partitioning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPLIT-RAG, a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework designed to improve the efficiency and accuracy of knowledge retrieval in large language models (LLMs), especially when dealing with large knowledge graphs.  SPLIT-RAG addresses limitations of traditional RAG systems by employing a multi-agent architecture and question-driven semantic graph partitioning. The framework first partitions the knowledge graph into semantically coherent subgraphs based on training question attributes. Lightweight LLM agents are then assigned to these subgraphs, and only relevant partitions are activated during retrieval, reducing the search space. Finally, a hierarchical merging module resolves inconsistencies across subgraph-derived answers. The paper demonstrates improved performance compared to existing approaches through experimental validation on several KGQA benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits reasonable novelty. While the individual components, such as graph partitioning, multi-agent systems, and RAG frameworks, are not entirely new, their combination and application in the presented way offer a fresh approach to knowledge retrieval. The question-driven graph partitioning, using training questions to inform the graph segmentation process, is a key differentiating factor and contributes to the paper's novelty. The multi-agent approach further enhances this novelty.

*   **Significance:** The work's significance lies in addressing the efficiency-accuracy trade-off in RAG systems, a critical challenge when scaling to large knowledge graphs.  The proposed SPLIT-RAG framework effectively reduces search space and improves response latency, which is highly relevant to practical applications. The conflict-resolution mechanism further contributes to the significance of this work, ensuring greater reliability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem statement:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing RAG systems when faced with large knowledge graphs.
    *   **Well-defined approach:**  SPLIT-RAG's architecture is well-defined and modular, consisting of distinct components.
    *   **Question-driven partitioning:** The paper offers effective question-aware graph partition scheme, leading to efficiency.
    *   **Multi-agent architecture:** The multi-agent approach provides an efficient and scalable architecture for knowledge retrieval.
    *   **Conflict Resolution:** The hierarchical merging module resolves inconsistencies across subgraph-derived answers.
    *   **Extensive experimentation:** The paper features thorough experimental validation on diverse KGQA benchmarks, comparing SPLIT-RAG against several baselines.
    *   **Ablation study:** The ablation study effectively demonstrates the contribution of individual components to the overall performance.
    *   **Model Flexibility:** The paper validates that applying lightweight models on multi-agent can be more efficient.
    *   **Information-Preserving Partitioning and Computational Efficiency:** The paper includes some theoretical analysis of the proposed partitioning.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:**  The SPLIT-RAG framework is complex, involving several stages and components, making it potentially challenging to implement and tune.
    *   **Dependency on Training Questions:** The framework's performance heavily relies on the quality and coverage of the training questions used for graph partitioning.
    *   **Limited theoretical Analysis:** The theoretical aspects of the paper, such as convergence properties and optimality guarantees, could benefit from more in-depth analysis. Theorem 3, for instance, is missing a proof for the inequality.
    *   **Scalability of Partitioning:** While the paper addresses retrieval efficiency, the scalability of the graph partitioning process itself (algorithm 1) for extremely large knowledge graphs might be a limitation, though some work has been down to improve on that.
    *   **Lack of comparison to other partitioning techniques:**  The paper briefly mentions graph partitioning algorithms but doesn't extensively compare against them within the context of RAG.
    *   **Presentation could be improved**: Many mathematical equations are not well-formatted, which might hinder understanding.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of RAG systems by providing a more efficient and accurate approach to knowledge retrieval in large knowledge graphs. The question-driven graph partitioning and multi-agent architecture can be adopted and further developed by other researchers. It might be applicable beyond knowledge graphs in situations requiring faster, more specific information retrieval. The theoretical analysis is reasonable for the space and is a nice touch.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field. While the framework is complex and relies on training question data, the performance gains and efficiency improvements demonstrated through experiments justify its significance. SPLIT-RAG's innovative combination of graph partitioning, multi-agent systems, and conflict resolution mechanisms offers a promising direction for future research in RAG systems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper is novel and significant and presents a well-defined approach with comprehensive experimental validation and theoretical justifications. The 7.5 score reflects the combination of reasonably high novelty and impact, balanced by the complexity of the approach, the dependence on high-quality training data, and areas for improvement in the theoretical analysis, formatting, and some unvalidated statements about scalable partitioning of extremely large graphs.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Activation-Guided Consensus Merging for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14009v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxuan Yao, Shuqi Liu, Zehua Liu, Qintong Li, Mingyang Liu, Xiongwei Han, Zhijiang Guo, Han Wu, Linqi Song
- **Abstract**: Recent research has increasingly focused on reconciling the reasoning capabilities of System 2 with the efficiency of System 1. While existing training-based and prompt-based approaches face significant challenges in terms of efficiency and stability, model merging emerges as a promising strategy to integrate the diverse capabilities of different Large Language Models (LLMs) into a unified model. However, conventional model merging methods often assume uniform importance across layers, overlooking the functional heterogeneity inherent in neural components. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{A}ctivation-Guided \textbf{C}onsensus \textbf{M}erging (\textbf{ACM}), a plug-and-play merging framework that determines layer-specific merging coefficients based on mutual information between activations of pre-trained and fine-tuned models. ACM effectively preserves task-specific capabilities without requiring gradient computations or additional training. Extensive experiments on Long-to-Short (L2S) and general merging tasks demonstrate that ACM consistently outperforms all baseline methods. For instance, in the case of Qwen-7B models, TIES-Merging equipped with ACM achieves a \textbf{55.3\%} reduction in response length while simultaneously improving reasoning accuracy by \textbf{1.3} points. We submit the code with the paper for reproducibility, and it will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Activation-Guided Consensus Merging (ACM), a novel plug-and-play model merging framework for large language models (LLMs).  ACM aims to integrate diverse capabilities of different LLMs by determining layer-specific merging coefficients based on the mutual information (MI) between activations of pre-trained and fine-tuned models.  The authors argue that existing merging methods often overlook the functional heterogeneity of neural components by assuming uniform importance across layers.  ACM assigns lower weights to layers with higher MI (greater similarity) to reduce redundancy, and higher weights to layers with lower MI (significant divergence) to preserve task-specific capabilities. Experimental results on Long-to-Short (L2S) reasoning and general merging tasks demonstrate ACM's superiority over existing baseline methods.  Specifically, they show reduced response length and improved reasoning accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its activation-guided approach to model merging, specifically leveraging mutual information to determine layer-specific weights. While other activation-based methods exist, using MI to balance redundancy reduction and task-specific capability preservation offers a unique contribution. The idea of tying the merging coefficient to the functional role and activations of each layer is a good one, especially given that different layers have different roles (e.g., the lm_head layer for L2S).

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important problem: efficiently integrating diverse capabilities into a single LLM without requiring extensive training or facing instability issues.  The L2S framework is a valuable direction, and the ACM approach offers a practical and effective way to realize it. The experiments provide solid evidence of ACM's effectiveness on several benchmarks. The code release is also a big plus. The fact it can reduce redundancy (response length) while improving performance (accuracy) is a significant benefit, especially for practical deployments.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-defined methodology.
    *   Strong experimental results on L2S and general merging tasks.
    *   Plug-and-play nature allows easy integration with existing methods.
    *   Reduces computational redundancy while preserving task-specific capabilities.
    *   Code release promotes reproducibility.
    *   Addresses a relevant and timely problem in LLM research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper's evaluation, although thorough, could be extended to include larger models (70B+) if computational constraints permit. While the authors acknowledged this limitation, it's important to evaluate the scalability of ACM.
    *   The method focuses on model merging within the *same* architecture, and there is no discussion of *heterogeneous* architectures.
    *   More in-depth analysis of *why* MI is an effective measure for this task would strengthen the theoretical justification. Though its effectiveness has been empirically demonstrated, a more nuanced explanation of MI's connection to weight salience within this specific context would be beneficial.
    *   Discussion of the limitations surrounding responsible AI, fairness, and bias would further improve the paper.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   ACM has the potential to become a standard technique for model merging, especially in scenarios where efficiency and diverse capabilities are required.
    *   The method could inspire new research directions in activation-guided optimization and adaptive parameter weighting.
    *   The L2S framework and ACM combination could lead to more practical and efficient LLM deployments in real-world applications.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

ACM presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of model merging. It addresses a critical challenge of integrating diverse capabilities into LLMs while maintaining efficiency and stability. The experimental results provide strong evidence of its effectiveness and its plug-and-play nature makes it readily adoptable. Although limitations exist concerning larger model evaluation and theoretical depth, the paper makes a substantial advance that warrants a high score. This research provides a new perspective and practical solution to model merging.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### AUTOLAW: Enhancing Legal Compliance in Large Language Models via Case Law Generation and Jury-Inspired Deliberation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14015v1)
- **Authors**: Tai D. Nguyen, Long H. Pham, Jun Sun
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of domain-specific large language models (LLMs) in fields like law necessitates frameworks that account for nuanced regional legal distinctions, which are critical for ensuring compliance and trustworthiness. Existing legal evaluation benchmarks often lack adaptability and fail to address diverse local contexts, limiting their utility in dynamically evolving regulatory landscapes. To address these gaps, we propose AutoLaw, a novel violation detection framework that combines adversarial data generation with a jury-inspired deliberation process to enhance legal compliance of LLMs. Unlike static approaches, AutoLaw dynamically synthesizes case law to reflect local regulations and employs a pool of LLM-based "jurors" to simulate judicial decision-making. Jurors are ranked and selected based on synthesized legal expertise, enabling a deliberation process that minimizes bias and improves detection accuracy. Evaluations across three benchmarks: Law-SG, Case-SG (legality), and Unfair-TOS (policy), demonstrate AutoLaw's effectiveness: adversarial data generation improves LLM discrimination, while the jury-based voting strategy significantly boosts violation detection rates. Our results highlight the framework's ability to adaptively probe legal misalignments and deliver reliable, context-aware judgments, offering a scalable solution for evaluating and enhancing LLMs in legally sensitive applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AUTOLAW, a framework aimed at enhancing legal compliance in large language models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of existing legal evaluation benchmarks, which often lack adaptability and the ability to handle nuanced regional legal distinctions. AUTOLAW employs two main components: (1) adversarial data generation to synthesize case law reflecting local regulations, and (2) a jury-inspired deliberation process where a pool of LLM-based "jurors" collaboratively decide on violation detection tasks. Jurors are ranked by their legal expertise, and the deliberation process aims to minimize bias and improve detection accuracy. The paper evaluates AUTOLAW on three benchmarks (Law-SG, Case-SG, Unfair-TOS) and shows that adversarial data generation improves LLM discrimination, while the jury-based voting strategy boosts violation detection rates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of adversarial data generation and a jury-inspired deliberation process within a legal compliance framework for LLMs presents a significant degree of novelty. While adversarial techniques and ensemble methods (like jury voting) are individually established, their integration in this context, specifically targeting regional legal compliance in LLMs, is relatively novel. The dynamic synthesis of case law is also a strong point.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial issue: ensuring that LLMs are legally compliant and trustworthy, especially in jurisdictions with specific legal nuances. Failure to do so can have severe consequences. The ability to adapt to dynamic regulatory landscapes is a key contribution. The improvement in violation detection rates compared to baseline approaches is encouraging and demonstrates AUTOLAW's potential impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a practical problem and articulates the limitations of current approaches.
    *   **Technical Approach:** The AUTOLAW framework is well-defined and combines several techniques in a sensible way. The jury selection mechanism, where jurors are chosen based on expertise, adds a layer of sophistication.
    *   **Evaluation:** The evaluation is reasonably comprehensive, using multiple benchmarks and assessing various aspects of AUTOLAW's performance.
    *   **Clear Results:** The paper presents convincing evidence that AUTOLAW improves legal compliance compared to standard methods.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Geographical Scope:** The evaluation is heavily focused on Singaporean law. While Singapore serves as a good case study, the generalizability of the framework to other legal systems needs further exploration and justification. Is the Singaporean legal system uniquely suitable for this approach?
    *   **LLM Jury's biases:** The whole approach is based on LLMs making judgements, and we know LLMs incorporate biases and have factual inaccuracies. This means the whole approach inherits these flaws and would be difficult to remove or correct.
    *   **Simplified Legal Scenarios:** The generated case law may oversimplify real-world legal complexities, leading to performance gains that might not translate directly to practical applications. The limitations section acknowledges this but could be strengthened by exploring more complex scenarios.
    *   **Juror diversity:** The range of LLMs used as jurors is fairly narrow. What happens when the range of LLMs is expanded?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of more robust and legally compliant LLMs. The approach of dynamic evaluation using scenario-based probes could become a standard practice. The idea of a jury-inspired deliberation process could be adopted and refined in various ways.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper demonstrates a novel approach with significant potential, the limitations related to geographical scope, simplified scenarios, and some lack of in-depth comparisons (e.g., the comparison is relatively simple between the selected jurors) constrain its current impact. These aspects need more detailed exploration and broader evidence.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Cyclic Diffusion for Inference Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14036v1)
- **Authors**: Gyubin Lee, Truong Nhat Nguyen Bao, Jaesik Yoon, Dongwoo Lee, Minsu Kim, Yoshua Bengio, Sungjin Ahn
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have demonstrated strong generative capabilities across domains ranging from image synthesis to complex reasoning tasks. However, most inference-time scaling methods rely on fixed denoising schedules, limiting their ability to allocate computation based on instance difficulty or task-specific demands adaptively. We introduce the challenge of adaptive inference-time scaling-dynamically adjusting computational effort during inference-and propose Adaptive Bi-directional Cyclic Diffusion (ABCD), a flexible, search-based inference framework. ABCD refines outputs through bi-directional diffusion cycles while adaptively controlling exploration depth and termination. It comprises three components: Cyclic Diffusion Search, Automatic Exploration-Exploitation Balancing, and Adaptive Thinking Time. Experiments show that ABCD improves performance across diverse tasks while maintaining computational efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Adaptive Cyclic Diffusion for Inference Scaling":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adaptive Bi-directional Cyclic Diffusion (ABCD), a novel inference framework for diffusion models designed to address the challenge of adaptive inference-time scaling.  ABCD reframes diffusion model inference as a flexible search process, allowing for dynamic adjustment of computational effort based on task difficulty and solution quality. The framework consists of three core components: Cyclic Diffusion Search (CDS), Automatic Exploration-Exploitation Balancing (AEEB), and Adaptive Thinking Time (ATT). CDS enables iterative refinement through bi-directional diffusion cycles, AEEB dynamically controls exploration depth by distributing particles across different re-noising levels, and ATT provides a principled stopping criterion by monitoring solution quality evolution. The paper demonstrates ABCD's effectiveness across diverse tasks, including control planning, maze solving, Sudoku, and molecule generation, showcasing improved performance, flexibility, computational efficiency and solution quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of adaptive inference-time scaling in diffusion models is itself an important problem. While inference scaling has been explored (Best-of-N, Beam Search etc.), ABCD proposes a more holistic approach. The key novelty lies in the synergistic combination of the three components: CDS, AEEB, and ATT.  The bi-directional cycling is a potentially beneficial way to escape local minima, and the automatic balancing is a more elegant approach compared to fixed back steps as it is instance-aware. The adaptive stopping criterion helps allocate computation to what is needed only.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of existing diffusion models: the reliance on fixed inference procedures. By enabling adaptive computation, ABCD has the potential to improve the performance and efficiency of diffusion models across various applications. The experiments demonstrate significant gains in several complex tasks. Improved flexibility and reduction in compute are essential to increase accessibility. The task set is diverse, including continuous and discrete domains, improving generalizability. This improves efficiency by allocating resources better, and provides potentially higher performance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem formulation and motivation.
    *   Well-defined framework with three distinct and synergistic components.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across diverse tasks.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in performance, flexibility, and computational efficiency.
    *   The paper has solid evaluations on multiple datasets and benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The framework introduces additional hyperparameters (e.g., temperature pool size, κ for ATT), which may require careful tuning. Although the AEEB is instance-aware, there could still be variance in the hyperparameter settings.
    *   The theoretical properties of ABCD are not thoroughly examined. A deeper understanding of its convergence behavior and sample complexity would be valuable.
    *   The discussion about scalability to high-dimensional output spaces is limited. More research is needed to address this challenge.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on inference-time scaling in diffusion models. The adaptive framework provides a promising direction for developing more efficient and robust generative models. There is potential for extending and improving upon the proposed method, and its successful application across various domains suggests broad applicability.

**Justification for Score:**

ABCD is not an incremental extension of existing methods. The holistic framework represents a significant step towards adaptive inference in diffusion models. The paper is well-written, well-motivated, and supported by strong experimental results. While there are certain limitations to address, the proposed method has the potential to make an impact on the field. Given the significant novelty and potential impact, but acknowledging limitations in theoretical analysis and scalability discussions, the score is justified.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ProMind-LLM: Proactive Mental Health Care via Causal Reasoning with Sensor Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14038v1)
- **Authors**: Xinzhe Zheng, Sijie Ji, Jiawei Sun, Renqi Chen, Wei Gao, Mani Srivastava
- **Abstract**: Mental health risk is a critical global public health challenge, necessitating innovative and reliable assessment methods. With the development of large language models (LLMs), they stand out to be a promising tool for explainable mental health care applications. Nevertheless, existing approaches predominantly rely on subjective textual mental records, which can be distorted by inherent mental uncertainties, leading to inconsistent and unreliable predictions. To address these limitations, this paper introduces ProMind-LLM. We investigate an innovative approach integrating objective behavior data as complementary information alongside subjective mental records for robust mental health risk assessment. Specifically, ProMind-LLM incorporates a comprehensive pipeline that includes domain-specific pretraining to tailor the LLM for mental health contexts, a self-refine mechanism to optimize the processing of numerical behavioral data, and causal chain-of-thought reasoning to enhance the reliability and interpretability of its predictions. Evaluations of two real-world datasets, PMData and Globem, demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed methods, achieving substantial improvements over general LLMs. We anticipate that ProMind-LLM will pave the way for more dependable, interpretable, and scalable mental health case solutions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ProMind-LLM: Proactive Mental Health Care via Causal Reasoning with Sensor Data":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ProMind-LLM, an approach to mental health risk assessment that combines objective behavioral sensor data with subjective textual mental records.  It addresses the limitations of existing LLM-based approaches that primarily rely on subjective textual data, which can be unreliable. ProMind-LLM incorporates a pipeline consisting of domain-specific pretraining, a self-refine mechanism for processing behavioral data, and causal chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning to improve the reliability and interpretability of predictions. The paper evaluates ProMind-LLM on two real-world datasets and demonstrates improved performance over general LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the integration of objective sensor data alongside subjective records within an LLM framework for mental health risk assessment.  Existing works have largely focused on textual data or, at best, used sensor data in isolation or as a minor complement. The self-refine mechanism and the causal CoT reasoning framework contribute to the overall novelty. However, each of these elements individually is not entirely novel, but their combined application and adaptation for this specific problem is.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate.  Mental health risk assessment is a crucial area, and improving the reliability and interpretability of such assessments is valuable. If the proposed system demonstrably increases the accuracy and reliability of mental health risk assessments it could improve patient care. The paper makes a contribution by addressing the limitation of relying solely on subjective user-provided data. The limitations section does point out that the evaluation data is limited to a couple of datasets, the approach needs to be validated in the real world, and the models have only been tested on a small scale.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a real-world problem with practical significance.
    *   Presents a well-defined and relatively complete system.
    *   Includes domain-specific training to better represent mental health contexts
    *   Thorough evaluation with comparisons to strong baselines.
    *   Includes an ablation study to highlight the importance of each component
    *   Addresses interpretability with CoT reasoning and human evaluation

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Relies on a small number of public datasets, potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings. Real-world trials and longitudinal studies with diverse populations are vital.
    *   The evaluation is performed on LLMs of modest size.
    *   While CoT improves interpretability, a more in-depth qualitative analysis of the reasoning process and its potential biases would be beneficial.
    *   Ethical risk evaluation acknowledges challenges in productionizing the system.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research in the field of mental health and AI by demonstrating the value of incorporating objective data and emphasizing the importance of model reliability and interpretability. It may encourage further exploration of multimodal approaches and the development of more robust and transparent mental health assessment tools.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:** The paper introduces a novel approach by integrating objective sensor data with subjective mental records within an LLM framework for mental health risk assessment, a significant but moderately high-impact real-world problem. The self-refine mechanism and causal CoT reasoning add to the novelty of the proposed solution. However, the limitations of the limited data, need for real-world trials, moderate model sizes, and ethical risk evaluation keep the score from being higher.


- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Adversarially Pretrained Transformers may be Universally Robust In-Context Learners
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14042v1)
- **Authors**: Soichiro Kumano, Hiroshi Kera, Toshihiko Yamasaki
- **Abstract**: Adversarial training is one of the most effective adversarial defenses, but it incurs a high computational cost. In this study, we show that transformers adversarially pretrained on diverse tasks can serve as robust foundation models and eliminate the need for adversarial training in downstream tasks. Specifically, we theoretically demonstrate that through in-context learning, a single adversarially pretrained transformer can robustly generalize to multiple unseen tasks without any additional training, i.e., without any parameter updates. This robustness stems from the model's focus on robust features and its resistance to attacks that exploit non-predictive features. Besides these positive findings, we also identify several limitations. Under certain conditions (though unrealistic), no universally robust single-layer transformers exist. Moreover, robust transformers exhibit an accuracy--robustness trade-off and require a large number of in-context demonstrations. The code is available at https://github.com/s-kumano/universally-robust-in-context-learner.
- **Summary**: Here's a breakdown of the paper, a critical evaluation, and an assigned score:

**Summary**

The paper investigates the potential for adversarially pre-trained transformers to serve as robust foundation models, eliminating the need for adversarial training in downstream tasks. The authors theoretically demonstrate that, leveraging in-context learning, a single adversarially pre-trained transformer can generalize robustly to multiple unseen tasks without further training. This robustness stems from the model prioritizing robust features and resisting attacks that exploit non-predictive features. The paper also identifies limitations, such as conditions under which no universally robust single-layer transformer exists, accuracy-robustness trade-offs, and the requirement for a large number of in-context demonstrations.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of using adversarially pre-trained foundation models for robust in-context learning is novel and potentially important. The paper presents a theoretical framework and supporting analysis, a relatively unexplored area in the context of transformer robustness and adaptation.  The theoretical analysis of *universal* robustness through in-context learning is a clear contribution.  The specific focus on separating robust and non-robust features is a known concept, but applying this directly to pre-trained transformers with in-context learning offers a fresh perspective.

*   **Significance:** If the theoretical findings hold in more realistic, complex settings, the implications are significant. It could lead to a more efficient and scalable approach to training robust models, avoiding the high computational cost of adversarial training for each downstream task.  The work addresses a central practical challenge in deploying robust machine learning systems. The identification of limitations is equally important, guiding future research to address these weaknesses.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong theoretical grounding: The paper provides rigorous mathematical analysis and proofs.
    *   Addresses a practical problem: The work tackles the computational burden of adversarial training.
    *   Clear articulation of assumptions and limitations: The paper is careful to define the data distributions, attack models, and situations under which the results hold. The limitations section provides a balanced view and valuable guidance for future work.
    *   Code availability enhances reproducibility and future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Simplified model and data: The analysis focuses on single-layer transformers with linear self-attention and relatively simple data distributions consisting of robust and non-robust features, potentially affecting generalizability.
    *   Assumptions: Some assumptions (like small covariance between features (q_rob and q_vul)), while potentially valid in some real-world datasets, could limit applicability in other settings.  The assumption about feature separation (robust vs. non-robust) is a simplification that might not perfectly reflect real-world data. The impractical condition of universally robust single-layer transformers under certain situations, limits its usefulness.
    *   Accuracy-robustness trade-off is a known phenomenon, and its identification here, while important, isn't entirely surprising.

**Justification of Score**

The paper presents a valuable theoretical contribution to the field of robust machine learning. Its novelty lies in the investigation of universal robustness in adversarially pre-trained transformers through in-context learning. However, the simplifying assumptions regarding the model architecture, data distribution, and limitations associated with accuracy-robustness trade-offs somewhat temper its potential for immediate practical impact. The score reflects the paper's theoretical strength and potential significance tempered by its limitations in scope and the need for further validation on more complex scenarios.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Unaligned to Aligned: Scaling Multilingual LLMs with Multi-Way Parallel Corpora
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14045v1)
- **Authors**: Yingli Shen, Wen Lai, Shuo Wang, Kangyang Luo, Alexander Fraser, Maosong Sun
- **Abstract**: Continued pretraining and instruction tuning on large-scale multilingual data have proven to be effective in scaling large language models (LLMs) to low-resource languages. However, the unaligned nature of such data limits its ability to effectively capture cross-lingual semantics. In contrast, multi-way parallel data, where identical content is aligned across multiple languages, provides stronger cross-lingual consistency and offers greater potential for improving multilingual performance. In this paper, we introduce a large-scale, high-quality multi-way parallel corpus, TED2025, based on TED Talks. The corpus spans 113 languages, with up to 50 languages aligned in parallel, ensuring extensive multilingual coverage. Using this dataset, we investigate best practices for leveraging multi-way parallel data to enhance LLMs, including strategies for continued pretraining, instruction tuning, and the analysis of key influencing factors. Experiments on six multilingual benchmarks show that models trained on multiway parallel data consistently outperform those trained on unaligned multilingual data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TED2025, a new large-scale multi-way parallel corpus based on TED Talks covering 113 languages with up to 50-way parallelism. The authors investigate the use of this corpus for scaling multilingual LLMs through continued pretraining and instruction tuning. They compare training on TED2025 against unaligned multilingual data, analyzing the impact on zero-shot cross-lingual transfer, representation alignment, and cross-domain robustness. The experiments demonstrate that fine-tuning on multi-way parallel data consistently outperforms training on unaligned data, promoting stronger cross-lingual supervision and language-agnostic representations.  The paper also explores strategies for selecting parallelism, instruction tuning objectives, and the impact of English as a pivot language.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation and release of the TED2025 corpus.  While multi-way parallel corpora exist, TED2025 boasts a substantial scale, language diversity, and high degree of parallelism compared to previous resources. The application of this corpus to *scaling* multilingual LLMs, rather than just smaller cross-lingual tasks, is a notable contribution. However, the experimental setups (continued pretraining, instruction tuning) themselves are not entirely new, but the systematical study on TED2025 corpus is.

*   **Significance:** The findings demonstrate the tangible benefits of multi-way parallel data for enhancing multilingual LLMs. The results showing improved zero-shot transfer, better representation alignment, and increased robustness are significant. The analysis of factors influencing model performance (degree of parallelism, language combinations, and training objectives) provides valuable insights and practical guidelines for leveraging multi-way parallel data. The release of TED2025 itself contributes significantly, offering researchers a valuable resource for future multilingual NLP research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Large-scale and diverse corpus:** TED2025 fills a critical gap in available resources for multilingual LLM research.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper systematically investigates various aspects of training LLMs on multi-way parallel data.
    *   **Clear results and analysis:** The results are clearly presented, with thorough analysis and insightful interpretations. The identification of effective strategies and influential factors are particularly valuable.
    *   **Practical guidelines:** The paper provides practical recommendations for optimizing multilingual LLM training based on multi-way parallel data.
    *   **Release of data:**  Making the dataset publicly available significantly increases the impact and reproducibility of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Limitations:** The use of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (LoRA) instead of full fine-tuning due to resource constraints could limit the generalizability of the conclusions. While LoRA is effective, validation under full fine-tuning would strengthen the results.
    *   **Dataset size relative to unaligned data:** While large compared to other *multi-way* corpora, TED2025 is still relatively small compared to datasets used for *unaligned* multilingual pretraining.  Scaling up the dataset even further could potentially unlock greater performance gains.
    *   **Limited task types:** The evaluation benchmarks, while diverse, could be expanded to include tasks that directly assess the quality of cross-lingual transfer. Specifically, structural probing tasks to measure the similarity of parse trees across languages would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Language families study might be limited**: Studying the linguistic relatedness might be limited. The selection of language family combinations might be more rigorous.
    *   **Prompt engineering**: the prompts for several tasks are based on machine translation. These types of tasks already involve complex semantics and reasoning. Instead the prompts can include task decompositions to enhance the reasoning capabilities.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of multilingual LLM research. It highlights the importance of data alignment and provides valuable insights into the best practices for leveraging multi-way parallel data. The release of TED2025 will likely foster further research in this area. It's important to consider also that many of the conclusions are somewhat anticipated by the community, given the theoretical benefits of parallel data. The rigorous experimentation however lends more support to these claims.

*   **Overall:** The work is a solid contribution that tackles an important problem, has adequate empirical validation, and makes its key resource publicly available.

### Improved Methods for Model Pruning and Knowledge Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14052v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Jiang, Anying Fu, Youling Zhang
- **Abstract**: Model pruning is a performance optimization technique for large language models like R1 or o3-mini. However, existing pruning methods often lead to significant performance degradation or require extensive retraining and fine-tuning. This technique aims to identify and remove neurons, connections unlikely leading to the contribution during the human-computer interaction phase. Our goal is to obtain a much smaller and faster knowledge distilled model that can quickly generate content almost as good as those of the unpruned ones. We propose MAMA Pruning, short for Movement and Magnitude Analysis, an improved pruning method that effectively reduces model size and computational complexity while maintaining performance comparable to the original unpruned model even at extreme pruned levels. The improved method is based on weights, bias fixed in the pre-training phase and GRPO rewards verified during the post-training phase as our novel pruning indicators. Preliminary experimental results show that our method outperforms and be comparable to state-of-the-art methods across various pruning levels and different downstream computational linguistics tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "Improved Methods for Model Pruning and Distillation."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "MAMA Pruning," a new model pruning technique for large language models (LLMs). MAMA Pruning aims to reduce model size and computational complexity while preserving performance comparable to unpruned models, even at high pruning levels. The method identifies and removes "unimportant" neurons and connections, using a three-step process: (1) Identifying weights eligible for pruning based on magnitude and dynamic behavior during pre- and post-training; (2) Redistributing pruned weights to related neurons to preserve knowledge; and (3) Executing the pruning.  The authors compare their method against other established pruning techniques like Magnitude Pruning, SparseGPT, and WANDA. The experimental results presented compare the perplexity of models pruned with MAMA Pruning versus a "Prune by -Weights" method and show promising results. The paper concludes by discussing future research directions, including exploring other teacher models, integrating pruning and knowledge distillation techniques within a GPRO framework, and developing a wisdom graph to guide the pruning and distillation processes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of the work lies in the specific combination of techniques used in MAMA Pruning: the dual consideration of weight magnitude and dynamic behavior during both pre- and post-training, along with the redistribution of pruned weights. The use of GRPO rewards and novel pruning indicators derived from weights, biases, and activations also contribute to novelty. However, the fundamental idea of pruning based on weight importance and redistributing weights isn't entirely new in the broader field of model compression. The redistribution of weights is similar to approaches like network morphism or knowledge distillation but tailored for pruning. Therefore, the novelty is more in the specific implementation and combination of these elements rather than a completely revolutionary concept. The performance comparison to a "Prune by -Weights" method is also unclear, as the specifics of this baseline technique are not specified in the document.

**Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve the efficiency of large language models, making them more accessible and deployable in resource-constrained environments. If MAMA Pruning can effectively reduce model size without significantly sacrificing performance, it could have practical implications for various applications. However, the significance hinges on several factors that are not fully substantiated by the provided paper:

*   **Scalability:** The paper doesn't provide enough evidence to demonstrate the scalability of MAMA Pruning to extremely large models and complex datasets.
*   **Generalizability:** The experimental results are somewhat limited and don't cover a wide range of tasks and model architectures.  The tables included only show results against one pruning method, and lack sufficient information. More detailed results would be needed to assess generalizability.
*   **Comparison to SOTA:** The paper compares their method against other established pruning techniques like Magnitude Pruning, SparseGPT, and WANDA. However, the provided perplexity data does not reflect this, and only shows results against a single "Prune by -Weights" method, with no details on its implementation.
*   **Clarity and Completeness:** The evaluation lacks detail, and the methods used are not entirely clear.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem statement:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of large language models and the need for efficient pruning techniques.
*   **Well-defined approach:** The MAMA Pruning algorithm is clearly described with its three core steps.
*   **Potential for practical impact:** The technique could potentially reduce the computational cost and memory footprint of LLMs.
*   **Forward-looking future work:** The paper outlines several interesting directions for future research, including integration with knowledge distillation and wisdom graphs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited experimental results:** The experimental evaluation is not comprehensive, lacking sufficient data points and comparisons to established state-of-the-art pruning techniques. The baseline method "Prune by -Weights" is poorly described.
*   **Lack of detailed analysis:** The paper lacks an in-depth analysis of why MAMA Pruning works (or doesn't work) in certain scenarios. What specifically causes MAMA pruning to degrade at higher pruning ratios?
*   **Overly optimistic claims:** The paper makes strong claims about the performance of MAMA Pruning without sufficient evidence.
*   **Clarity issues:** Some of the descriptions could be more precise and detailed.
*   **Scalability concerns:** The paper doesn't address potential scalability issues for extremely large models.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the algorithm, it has potential applications in LLM optimization and has a clear structure. However, the weaknesses of the paper outweigh the strengths. The limited experimental results, lack of rigorous comparison to other state-of-the-art techniques, and overly optimistic claims significantly diminish the paper's significance. The novelty, while present, is incremental rather than groundbreaking. The lack of clarity in some areas also hampers a thorough evaluation of the method's effectiveness. Therefore, a score of 5 seems appropriate. It shows some potential, but needs significantly more work and thorough validation.

**Score: 5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Field Matters: A lightweight LLM-enhanced Method for CTR Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14057v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Cui, Feng Liu, Jiawei Chen, Xingyu Lou, Changwang Zhang, Jun Wang, Yuegang Sun, Xiaohu Yang, Can Wang
- **Abstract**: Click-through rate (CTR) prediction is a fundamental task in modern recommender systems. In recent years, the integration of large language models (LLMs) has been shown to effectively enhance the performance of traditional CTR methods. However, existing LLM-enhanced methods often require extensive processing of detailed textual descriptions for large-scale instances or user/item entities, leading to substantial computational overhead. To address this challenge, this work introduces LLaCTR, a novel and lightweight LLM-enhanced CTR method that employs a field-level enhancement paradigm. Specifically, LLaCTR first utilizes LLMs to distill crucial and lightweight semantic knowledge from small-scale feature fields through self-supervised field-feature fine-tuning. Subsequently, it leverages this field-level semantic knowledge to enhance both feature representation and feature interactions. In our experiments, we integrate LLaCTR with six representative CTR models across four datasets, demonstrating its superior performance in terms of both effectiveness and efficiency compared to existing LLM-enhanced methods. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/LLaCTR-EC46.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Field Matters: A Lightweight LLM-enhanced Method for CTR Prediction":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the computational inefficiency of existing LLM-enhanced Click-Through Rate (CTR) prediction methods. These methods often require processing large amounts of textual data at the instance or user/item level, leading to significant overhead. The authors propose LLaCTR, a novel lightweight method that operates at the *field level*. The key idea is to use LLMs to extract semantic knowledge from feature *fields* (categories of features), which are far fewer in number than instances or entities.  LLaCTR involves two main steps: (1) Self-supervised Field-feature Fine-tuning (SSFT), where an LLM is fine-tuned to predict the field a given feature belongs to, and (2) Field Semantic-guided Enhancement, where the distilled field semantic knowledge is used to improve both feature representation and feature interactions in existing CTR models.  Experiments across several datasets and with various CTR models demonstrate that LLaCTR achieves superior performance compared to existing LLM-enhanced methods while being significantly more efficient.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *shift from instance/entity-level to field-level LLM enhancement*.  Existing LLM-based methods apply LLMs to raw data instances, such as textual descriptions of items, or entire user profiles. LLaCTR's field-level approach is more efficient because the number of fields is orders of magnitude smaller, reducing LLM computational overhead. The self-supervised fine-tuning using domain-specific knowledge and then applying this knowledge in downstream tasks is also quite novel. The way it is applied can be related to meta learning and knowledge distillation, which is not very clear from the paper. While using LLMs for CTR prediction is not new, the specific *manner* in which LLaCTR uses them (through field-level semantics and fine-tuning) is a definite contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the *practicality* of the approach.  The paper convincingly demonstrates that LLaCTR can achieve comparable or better performance with significantly less computational cost (10-100x speedup). This makes LLM-enhanced CTR prediction more viable for real-world deployment, especially in large-scale industrial settings. The improvements in terms of AUC are modest but consistent across different datasets, showcasing the robustness of the method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitation of existing LLM-enhanced CTR methods - high computational cost.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The field-level enhancement paradigm is well-motivated by the reduced computational cost and crucial semantic information inherent in field descriptions.
    *   **Thorough Experiments:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple datasets, base CTR models, and ablation studies.  The comparison with existing LLM-enhanced methods is particularly valuable.
    *   **Efficiency Gains:** The paper convincingly demonstrates the efficiency gains of LLaCTR, which is crucial for its practical applicability.
    *   The details of the implementation and the hyperparameter settings, including the source code, improve replicability of the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Modest AUC Gains:** While statistically significant, the absolute improvement in AUC may be considered modest in some cases.
    *   **Clarity of Integration:** The approach of integrating the LLaCTR module into the architecture is not clear. In particular, the differences in the approach for explicit and implicit feature models need to be clearly articulated in the paper.
    *   **Limited Analysis:** The use case analysis could be stronger.

*   **Potential Influence:** LLaCTR has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in LLM-enhanced CTR prediction.  Its efficient field-level enhancement paradigm could become a standard approach, especially as LLMs continue to evolve. It encourages a move towards more lightweight and efficient approaches.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novel field-level approach, the significant efficiency gains, and the solid experimental results, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of CTR prediction. While the absolute performance gains might be moderate, the practicality and potential for wider adoption due to the lower computational cost warrant a high score.

### Process vs. Outcome Reward: Which is Better for Agentic RAG Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14069v1)
- **Authors**: Wenlin Zhang, Xiangyang Li, Kuicai Dong, Yichao Wang, Pengyue Jia, Xiaopeng Li, Yingyi Zhang, Derong Xu, Zhaocheng Du, Huifeng Guo, Ruiming Tang, Xiangyu Zhao
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enhances the text generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by integrating external knowledge and up-to-date information. However, traditional RAG systems are limited by static workflows and lack the adaptability required for multistep reasoning and complex task management. To address these limitations, agentic RAG systems (e.g., DeepResearch) have been proposed, enabling dynamic retrieval strategies, iterative context refinement, and adaptive workflows for handling complex search queries beyond the capabilities of conventional RAG. Recent advances, such as Search-R1, have demonstrated promising gains using outcome-based reinforcement learning, where the correctness of the final answer serves as the reward signal. Nevertheless, such outcome-supervised agentic RAG methods face challenges including low exploration efficiency, gradient conflict, and sparse reward signals. To overcome these challenges, we propose to utilize fine-grained, process-level rewards to improve training stability, reduce computational costs, and enhance efficiency. Specifically, we introduce a novel method ReasonRAG that automatically constructs RAG-ProGuide, a high-quality dataset providing process-level rewards for (i) query generation, (ii) evidence extraction, and (iii) answer generation, thereby enhancing model inherent capabilities via process-supervised reinforcement learning. With the process-level policy optimization, the proposed framework empowers LLMs to autonomously invoke search, generate queries, extract relevant evidence, and produce final answers. Compared to existing approaches such as Search-R1 and traditional RAG systems, ReasonRAG, leveraging RAG-ProGuide, achieves superior performance on five benchmark datasets using only 5k training instances, significantly fewer than the 90k training instances required by Search-R1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Process vs. Outcome Reward: Which is Better for Agentic RAG Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses limitations in traditional Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, which struggle with complex, multi-step reasoning. It focuses on Agentic RAG systems, where Large Language Models (LLMs) dynamically control retrieval strategies. The core idea is to improve reinforcement learning (RL) for these systems. Instead of just rewarding the final, correct answer (outcome-supervised RL), the authors propose using process-level rewards, giving feedback at each step of the retrieval and reasoning process. They introduce ReasonRAG, a method that uses Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to explore different reasoning paths and a Shortest Path Reward Estimation (SPRE) algorithm to assign rewards based on the quality and efficiency of each step. The resulting dataset, RAG-ProGuide, facilitates policy optimization.  The authors demonstrate ReasonRAG's superior performance and training efficiency compared to outcome-supervised methods (like Search-R1) across several datasets, achieving better results with significantly less training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using process-level rewards instead of solely outcome-based rewards for agentic RAG is incremental, but well-motivated and effectively executed. The specific techniques proposed - MCTS combined with SPRE to construct RAG-ProGuide dataset for process-supervised RL - contribute to the paper's novelty. Other RAG approaches have explored similar data collection techniques, but this paper distinguishes itself with its application in agentic RL, focusing on fine-grained supervision.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in addressing key challenges of applying RL to complex reasoning tasks. Outcome-supervised RL often suffers from sparse rewards and inefficient exploration. The process-level approach addresses these issues directly, leading to more stable and efficient training. The improvement in sample efficiency (achieving better results with less data) is particularly significant, reducing the computational burden of training agentic RAG systems. The superior out-of-domain generalization is also important.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the shortcomings of outcome-supervised RL in agentic RAG.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The use of process-level rewards is logically justified by the limitations of sparse rewards and gradient conflicts in outcome-based methods.
    *   **Sound Methodology:** The combination of MCTS and SPRE is a reasonable approach for exploring the reasoning space and assigning process-level rewards.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments are comprehensive, comparing ReasonRAG against multiple baselines on diverse datasets. The results convincingly demonstrate the benefits of the proposed approach.
    *   **Data Efficiency:**  The paper highlights the data efficiency gains, which are crucial for practical application of RL to LLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity of Implementation:** The ReasonRAG framework involves several components (MCTS, SPRE, DPO), potentially making it complex to implement and reproduce.
    *   **Reliance on LLM for Reward Estimation:** The SPRE approach relies on an LLM (GPT-40) to evaluate intermediate reasoning steps. The accuracy of this evaluation is critical, and potential biases or limitations of the evaluation LLM could affect the quality of the training data. Also, cost considerations of GPT-40 use should be a factor when evaluating practicality.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The paper could benefit from more detailed ablation studies to assess the individual contributions of MCTS and SPRE to the overall performance.
    *   **Scope for Improvement in SPRE:** The study can be further improved by elaborating how to deal with cases of overpenalization in SPRE. Certain answers are often generated with longer trajectories to better explain the answer to the end-user, so it would be interesting to explore methods to account for these situations in SPRE.
    *   **Scalability:** While the paper demonstrates improvements in efficiency, more detailed discussions on scalability for even more complex, real-world applications is needed. Can the MCTS component, for instance, scale to much larger reasoning spaces?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in agentic RAG, particularly in the design of RL training methods. It could inspire further work on process-level supervision and exploration strategies for complex reasoning tasks.  The RAG-ProGuide dataset could also serve as a valuable resource for the community. The paper's focus on efficiency could help make RL-based agentic RAG systems more practical for real-world deployment.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of agentic RAG by demonstrating the effectiveness of process-level rewards for RL. The idea is well-motivated and empirically validated. The data efficiency gains are significant. However, the approach has some limitations in terms of implementation complexity, reliance on an external LLM for reward estimation, and the need for more ablation studies and a discussion of scalability. The paper is thus a solid contribution with incremental novelty, but is unlikely to radically transform the field due to the aforementioned limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing LLMs via High-Knowledge Data Selection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14070v1)
- **Authors**: Feiyu Duan, Xuemiao Zhang, Sirui Wang, Haoran Que, Yuqi Liu, Wenge Rong, Xunliang Cai
- **Abstract**: The performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) is intrinsically linked to the quality of its training data. Although several studies have proposed methods for high-quality data selection, they do not consider the importance of knowledge richness in text corpora. In this paper, we propose a novel and gradient-free High-Knowledge Scorer (HKS) to select high-quality data from the dimension of knowledge, to alleviate the problem of knowledge scarcity in the pre-trained corpus. We propose a comprehensive multi-domain knowledge element pool and introduce knowledge density and coverage as metrics to assess the knowledge content of the text. Based on this, we propose a comprehensive knowledge scorer to select data with intensive knowledge, which can also be utilized for domain-specific high-knowledge data selection by restricting knowledge elements to the specific domain. We train models on a high-knowledge bilingual dataset, and experimental results demonstrate that our scorer improves the model's performance in knowledge-intensive and general comprehension tasks, and is effective in enhancing both the generic and domain-specific capabilities of the model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel, gradient-free High-Knowledge Scorer (HKS) for selecting high-quality data for pre-training Large Language Models (LLMs), focusing on the knowledge richness of text corpora. The HKS leverages a multi-domain knowledge element pool and quantifies knowledge using metrics like knowledge density and coverage. The paper demonstrates that models trained on data selected by the HKS outperform those trained on randomly selected data or data selected using other metrics, particularly in knowledge-intensive and general comprehension tasks. The approach also allows for domain-specific high-knowledge data selection, improving model performance in targeted areas.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its explicit focus on knowledge richness as a criterion for data selection in LLMs. While other methods exist for high-quality data selection, few address knowledge content directly and comprehensively. Simplifying knowledge into knowledge elements and creating a large multi-domain knowledge pool is a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** Selecting high-quality data is a crucial step in LLM training. By prioritizing knowledgeable data, the HKS addresses the problem of knowledge scarcity in pre-trained corpora, which directly translates to improved model performance in various tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The methodology is well-defined and comprehensive, starting from knowledge element extraction to the final scoring function.
    *   **Quantitative Metrics:** Knowledge density and coverage provide a tangible measure of knowledge content, enabling quantitative analysis.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments are thorough, with comparisons against relevant baselines and analysis of different filtering strategies. The demonstration of improvement in both knowledge-intensive and general understanding tasks is compelling.
    *   **Domain-Specific Application:** The ability to select domain-specific knowledge data is valuable for specialized LLMs.
    *   **Cost-Efficiency:** The gradient-free nature of HKS makes it computationally more efficient compared to some model-based selection techniques.
    *   **Extensive Analysis:** The paper provides a robust analysis of its performance by including perplexity evaluations and a thorough ablation of different components of the system.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on GPT4 for Knowledge Extraction:** While GPT4 simplifies knowledge extraction, it might introduce biases or inconsistencies. The quality of the knowledge element pool heavily depends on the prompts given to GPT-4 which could be improved.
    *   **Knowledge Element Definition:** The definition of a knowledge element is somewhat vague. While the paper justifies it with quicker annotations, it leaves room for interpretation and potential ambiguity.
    *   **Limited Model and Dataset Scales:** While the 1.1B model is a good starting point, the paper might be even more compelling with larger models and datasets. The experiments with larger models by continually pretraining the model provide useful insight but more information on a completely trained large scale model would further strengthen the results.
    *   **Scoring Formula Optimization:** The scoring formula selection process, while experiment-driven, lacks a rigorous theoretical justification. There is a bit of a black box approach.
    *   **Generalization:** The experiments are performed in Chinese and English datasets. The paper would benefit from an experiment in other languages to validate its language agnostic behavior.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM training by encouraging a greater focus on knowledge enrichment during data selection. It lays the groundwork for future research into more sophisticated methods of quantifying and incorporating knowledge into LLMs. It could also impact practical applications by enabling the development of more knowledgeable and reliable LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and comprehensive approach to data selection for LLMs, addressing a critical gap in existing methodologies. The HKS demonstrably improves model performance across various tasks, showcasing its efficacy and potential influence. Despite some weaknesses in the knowledge element definition, GPT-4 dependency, and limited dataset scales, the strengths of the paper outweigh the shortcomings.
