The qualitative results presented in the figures are visually appealing, but the quantitative evaluations are limited and need further context.  For example, the success score and overall score used in the video super-resolution evaluation are not clearly defined. Also, relying on game art designers for evaluation is useful but inherently subjective. More objective metrics should be included in the comparison process. Without a deeper dive, there is a risk that claimed performance improvements are merely cosmetic. Moreover, the focus on "professional" game assets raises the bar considerably - these assets must be usable without extensive manual tweaking.

*   **Data and Training:** The paper mentions a large dataset of billions of images and millions of videos. The details of the data collection and filtering pipeline are somewhat superficial.  How was data bias addressed?  What are the licensing and ethical considerations related to the data? The construction of the proprietary aesthetic scoring system is described in reasonable detail. The multi-stage training process sounds promising but lacks technical details and makes verification impossible.

*   **Comparisons:** The comparisons to existing methods (Midjourney, Kling, Wan) are useful, but a comprehensive and fair comparison is needed. Are the compared methods optimized for the specific game asset generation scenarios that Hunyuan-Game is targeting? The absence of direct comparisons with existing domain-specific models is a key weakness. The absence of ablation studies to illustrate the effect of each technical feature on the final results makes the novelty of the algorithm weaker.

*   **Community Impact:**  The authors mention promoting community-driven innovation, but the paper lacks concrete details on how they plan to achieve this. The lack of code and the limited details makes it impossible for the community to validate these claimed performances, making this project more of a press release rather than a scientific contribution.

*   **Reproducibility:** The paper suffers severely from a lack of details necessary for reproducibility. Limited architectural details, dataset specifics, and training details make it impossible to reproduce the results.

**Strengths:**

*   The ambitious scale of the project.
*   The focus on a practical application with significant industry potential.
*   The integration of multiple asset generation tasks.
*   The effort to create a high-quality, game-specific dataset.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited algorithmic novelty; relies heavily on existing generative models.
*   Lack of detailed architectural descriptions and ablation studies.
*   Insufficiently detailed quantitative evaluations.
*   Data and training processes require more transparency.
*   Limited details on the evaluation metrics and a strong dependency on subjective evaluation.
*   Vague on plans for community engagement.
*   Lack of release of any implementation or data.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the limited objective evidence of novel algorithmic contributions, the reliance on subjective evaluations, and the general lack of reproduciblity,  the paper is awarded a moderate score. The work has the potential to impact the game industry, but its scientific novelty is questionable based on the information presented.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### FlowQ: Energy-Guided Flow Policies for Offline Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14139v1)
- **Authors**: Marvin Alles, Nutan Chen, Patrick van der Smagt, Botond Cseke
- **Abstract**: The use of guidance to steer sampling toward desired outcomes has been widely explored within diffusion models, especially in applications such as image and trajectory generation. However, incorporating guidance during training remains relatively underexplored. In this work, we introduce energy-guided flow matching, a novel approach that enhances the training of flow models and eliminates the need for guidance at inference time. We learn a conditional velocity field corresponding to the flow policy by approximating an energy-guided probability path as a Gaussian path. Learning guided trajectories is appealing for tasks where the target distribution is defined by a combination of data and an energy function, as in reinforcement learning. Diffusion-based policies have recently attracted attention for their expressive power and ability to capture multi-modal action distributions. Typically, these policies are optimized using weighted objectives or by back-propagating gradients through actions sampled by the policy. As an alternative, we propose FlowQ, an offline reinforcement learning algorithm based on energy-guided flow matching. Our method achieves competitive performance while the policy training time is constant in the number of flow sampling steps.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "FlowQ: Energy-Guided Flow Policies for Offline Reinforcement Learning" introduces a new offline reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm. FlowQ leverages energy-guided flow matching to train a policy that approximates the optimal policy from a pre-collected, static dataset. Unlike many existing offline RL methods based on diffusion models, FlowQ aims to incorporate guidance during training by approximating an energy-guided probability path as a Gaussian path.  This approach eliminates the need for guidance at inference time and improves training efficiency by avoiding backpropagation through long action sampling processes. The paper proposes a conditional probability path utilizing the Q-function as an energy function. The authors evaluate FlowQ on the D4RL benchmark, demonstrating competitive performance compared to other state-of-the-art offline RL methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its application of energy-guided flow matching to offline RL.  While both flow matching and the concept of using energy functions for guidance are established, their combination specifically within the offline RL context, and the formulation of the corresponding conditional probability path, represents a novel contribution.  The idea of incorporating the Q-function as an energy function to shape the policy directly during training is a sensible and practically useful idea. Furthermore, the linear approximation of the Taylor expansion provides a way to derive the velocity field.

**Significance:** The significance of the work stems from the potential to improve the training efficiency and sample complexity of offline RL algorithms.  By avoiding the need to backpropagate gradients through actions sampled from the policy, FlowQ offers a more scalable approach, particularly as the number of flow sampling steps increases.  This is a practical concern, given that modelling multimodal action distributions often requires more intricate policies, and hence more sampling steps. The empirical results showing competitive performance on the D4RL benchmark support the potential of the approach. In particular, the scaling of training time is a critical consideration in many practical RL applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of distributional shift and the need for efficient policy training in offline RL.
*   **Novel Approach:** The combination of energy-guided flow matching with a Q-function-based energy function offers a promising new direction for offline RL.
*   **Computational Efficiency:**  The proposed method reduces the need for action sampling from the policy model in the training loop and backpropagating through the samples.
*   **Competitive Performance:** FlowQ achieves competitive results on the D4RL benchmark, demonstrating its practical applicability.
*   **Detailed Experiments and Ablations:** The paper includes a thorough experimental evaluation, including ablation studies to analyze the impact of different design choices.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Approximation Accuracy:** The linear approximation of the Taylor expansion is a significant simplification. The impact of this approximation on performance needs to be carefully considered. The paper does touch upon this in the discussion about limitations.
*   **Limited Analysis:** Although the results on the D4RL benchmark are solid, further analysis of the strengths and weaknesses of the method in particular environment types would be very valuable.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance is highly dependent on hyperparameters and the energy scaling.
*  **Baseline Comparison:** The benchmark includes DiffusionQL, which is another diffusion based approach. However, the proposed approach lacks comparisons with other similar alternatives.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the direction of offline RL research by popularizing the use of energy-guided flow matching. Its computational efficiency could make it a valuable tool for training complex policies in real-world applications. Additionally, the Q-function guided approach opens doors for integration with existing Q-learning algorithms. The paper serves as a useful example for incorporating prior knowledge or domain specific heuristics in reinforcement learning.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach to offline RL that has the potential to improve training efficiency. The experimental results are strong, and the paper is well-written and clearly articulates the key ideas. However, there is room for further investigation into the limitations and the impact of the approximations made. Moreover, broader baseline comparison would support the claims.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### RL of Thoughts: Navigating LLM Reasoning with Inference-time Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14140v1)
- **Authors**: Qianyue Hao, Sibo Li, Jian Yuan, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Despite rapid advancements in large language models (LLMs), the token-level autoregressive nature constrains their complex reasoning capabilities. To enhance LLM reasoning, inference-time techniques, including Chain/Tree/Graph-of-Thought(s), successfully improve the performance, as they are fairly cost-effective by guiding reasoning through sophisticated logical structures without modifying LLMs' parameters. However, these manually predefined, task-agnostic frameworks are applied uniformly across diverse tasks, lacking adaptability. To improve this, we propose RL-of-Thoughts (RLoT), where we train a lightweight navigator model with reinforcement learning (RL) to adaptively enhance LLM reasoning at inference time. Specifically, we design five basic logic blocks from the perspective of human cognition. During the reasoning process, the trained RL navigator dynamically selects the suitable logic blocks and combines them into task-specific logical structures according to problem characteristics. Experiments across multiple reasoning benchmarks (AIME, MATH, GPQA, etc.) with multiple LLMs (GPT, Llama, Qwen, and DeepSeek) illustrate that RLoT outperforms established inference-time techniques by up to 13.4%. Remarkably, with less than 3K parameters, our RL navigator is able to make sub-10B LLMs comparable to 100B-scale counterparts. Moreover, the RL navigator demonstrates strong transferability: a model trained on one specific LLM-task pair can effectively generalize to unseen LLMs and tasks. Our code is open-source at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/RL-LLM-Reasoning-1A30 for reproducibility.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper "RL of Thoughts: Navigating LLM Reasoning with Inference-time Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel inference-time technique called RL-of-Thoughts (RLoT) to enhance the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). RLoT uses a reinforcement learning (RL) framework to train a lightweight "navigator" model that adaptively selects and combines basic logical blocks (inspired by human cognition) during the reasoning process.  The navigator guides the LLM through task-specific logical structures without modifying the LLM's parameters, making it cost-effective. Experiments across various reasoning benchmarks and LLMs demonstrate that RLoT outperforms established inference-time techniques, allowing smaller LLMs to achieve performance comparable to much larger ones. The paper also highlights the transferability of the trained navigator across different LLMs and tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using RL to dynamically construct reasoning structures for LLMs at inference time is novel. It moves beyond static, predefined reasoning frameworks like Chain-of-Thought (CoT) and Tree-of-Thoughts (ToT), offering more flexibility and adaptability. The design of human-inspired logical blocks is interesting, but their specific design doesn't feel deeply novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the reasoning capabilities of smaller, more accessible LLMs. The efficiency of the approach, requiring minimal parameters for the navigator and no fine-tuning of the LLM, makes it a practical solution. The reported performance gains, particularly on challenging tasks like GPQA, are noteworthy. The claim of transferability further enhances its practical value. If proven robust, this could lead to wider adoption of LLMs in resource-constrained environments.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Approach:** The use of RL for adaptive reasoning structure generation is a strong point.
    *   **Efficiency:** The lightweight navigator model and avoidance of LLM fine-tuning significantly reduce computational costs.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments across diverse tasks and LLMs support the effectiveness of RLoT.
    *   **Transferability:** The demonstrated transferability of the navigator model is a valuable feature.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While the paper includes ablation studies, deeper dives into the specific contributions of each logic block and the RL reward function would have strengthened the analysis.
    *   **Navigator Training Data:**  The paper mentions extracting "hard questions" for training the navigator, but more detail on the process would enhance reproducibility. The reliance on a Process Reward Model (PRM) also raises questions, since the performance of RLoT is now linked to the quality of the PRM. The paper would have been stronger if the navigator was trained end-to-end using task-specific rewards.
    *   **Limited Novelty of Logic Blocks**: The individual "logic blocks," while inspired by human cognition, are fairly standard problem-solving strategies. The novelty is not in inventing them, but rather in adaptively orchestrating them.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM reasoning, particularly in the development of more adaptive and efficient inference-time techniques.  The focus on smaller LLMs is also a valuable contribution, potentially broadening the accessibility of advanced reasoning capabilities.

*   **Rigor:** Experiments are well designed. Statistical significance analysis of the results might have added more weight.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and efficient approach to enhance LLM reasoning using RL, which holds significant potential for enabling smaller LLMs to perform competitively. However, the novelty of the logical blocks and the reliance on an external PRM, combined with the limited ablation studies, somewhat limit the overall impact. The potential for practical application and the demonstrated transferability justify a solid score, but further work would be needed to strengthen the claims and address the limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### s3: You Don't Need That Much Data to Train a Search Agent via RL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14146v1)
- **Authors**: Pengcheng Jiang, Xueqiang Xu, Jiacheng Lin, Jinfeng Xiao, Zifeng Wang, Jimeng Sun, Jiawei Han
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems empower large language models (LLMs) to access external knowledge during inference. Recent advances have enabled LLMs to act as search agents via reinforcement learning (RL), improving information acquisition through multi-turn interactions with retrieval engines. However, existing approaches either optimize retrieval using search-only metrics (e.g., NDCG) that ignore downstream utility or fine-tune the entire LLM to jointly reason and retrieve-entangling retrieval with generation and limiting the real search utility and compatibility with frozen or proprietary models. In this work, we propose s3, a lightweight, model-agnostic framework that decouples the searcher from the generator and trains the searcher using a Gain Beyond RAG reward: the improvement in generation accuracy over naive RAG. s3 requires only 2.4k training samples to outperform baselines trained on over 70x more data, consistently delivering stronger downstream performance across six general QA and five medical QA benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "s3: You Don't Need That Much Data to Train a Search Agent via RL":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "s3," a lightweight and model-agnostic framework for training search agents using reinforcement learning (RL).  The key innovation is a novel reward signal called "Gain Beyond RAG" (GBR), which measures the improvement in generation accuracy when using the agent's retrieved context compared to naive top-k retrieval. This focuses the training on improving *retrieval quality* specifically, decoupled from the generation component.  s3 requires significantly less training data (2.4k samples) than existing approaches (up to 170k samples) and demonstrates strong downstream performance across several QA benchmarks, outperforming baselines in both general and medical domains. The core idea is modularity, isolating the searcher and generator, and focusing RL training on the search component.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   *Strengths:* The primary novelty lies in the GBR reward signal and the framework's modular design. The GBR reward *directly* optimizes for generation accuracy improvements resulting from better retrieval, which is more aligned with the ultimate goal than optimizing for isolated search metrics like NDCG or recall. The decoupling of search and generation allows for greater flexibility and compatibility with proprietary LLMs, an increasingly important practical consideration. The extreme data efficiency is also noteworthy.
    *   *Weaknesses:* While the GBR reward is clever, it still relies on a potentially expensive LLM call for reward computation. The paper claims the model-agnostic nature of s3, however, it might need careful adjustment when applying to new scenarios, e.g., the hyperparameters (number of retrievals, turns). The use of Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) is a standard RL technique; the innovation is primarily in *how* RL is applied, not the RL algorithm itself.

*   **Significance:**
    *   *Strengths:* The data efficiency of s3 is a significant advantage, making RL-based search agents more accessible to researchers and practitioners with limited computational resources. The model-agnostic nature opens the door for using powerful, pre-trained, and potentially closed-source LLMs for generation, which addresses a practical constraint in many real-world settings. The improved performance across various QA datasets suggests the approach is generalizable and not simply overfitting to a specific benchmark. The focus on searcher-centric optimization provides a valuable alternative perspective to end-to-end training, making the retrieval component reusable across different models and tasks.
    *   *Weaknesses:* The paper doesn't fully explore the limitations of relying on a frozen generator. The performance of s3 is inherently limited by the capabilities of the generator LLM. The claim on data efficiency seems quite strong and there can be concerns on generalization. It would be nice to see the framework applied to more different scenarios.
    *   *Impact:* The paper contributes a practical and efficient method for training search agents, which could have a substantial impact on the development of RAG systems. The core principle of optimizing retrieval quality directly for downstream generation accuracy is an important insight that can influence future research in this area.

*   **Score Justification:**

    The paper presents a well-executed idea with demonstrable empirical results. The GBR reward and the modular design of s3 offer a practical and data-efficient approach to training search agents for RAG. The results across general and medical QA datasets are compelling. However, the reliance on a frozen generator and potential overhead of LLM calls for reward computation limit the novelty and impact. The paper could also benefit from a more thorough analysis of the limitations and failure cases. Overall, the paper offers a valuable contribution, however, still needs more evaluation in broader settings.
