- **Score**: 7/10

### SHARP: Synthesizing High-quality Aligned Reasoning Problems for Large Reasoning Models Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14147v1)
- **Authors**: Xiong Jun Wu, Zhenduo Zhang, ZuJie Wen, Zhiqiang Zhang, Wang Ren, Lei Shi, Cai Chen, Deng Zhao, Dingnan Jin, Qing Cui, Jun Zhou
- **Abstract**: Training large reasoning models (LRMs) with reinforcement learning in STEM domains is hindered by the scarcity of high-quality, diverse, and verifiable problem sets. Existing synthesis methods, such as Chain-of-Thought prompting, often generate oversimplified or uncheckable data, limiting model advancement on complex tasks. To address these challenges, we introduce SHARP, a unified approach to Synthesizing High-quality Aligned Reasoning Problems for LRMs reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). SHARP encompasses a strategic set of self-alignment principles -- targeting graduate and Olympiad-level difficulty, rigorous logical consistency, and unambiguous, verifiable answers -- and a structured three-phase framework (Alignment, Instantiation, Inference) that ensures thematic diversity and fine-grained control over problem generation. We implement SHARP by leveraging a state-of-the-art LRM to infer and verify challenging STEM questions, then employ a reinforcement learning loop to refine the model's reasoning through verifiable reward signals. Experiments on benchmarks such as GPQA demonstrate that SHARP-augmented training substantially outperforms existing methods, markedly improving complex reasoning accuracy and pushing LRM performance closer to expert-level proficiency. Our contributions include the SHARP strategy, framework design, end-to-end implementation, and experimental evaluation of its effectiveness in elevating LRM reasoning capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces SHARP, a novel approach to synthesizing high-quality, aligned reasoning problems for training large reasoning models (LRMs) in STEM domains using reinforcement learning.  SHARP addresses the scarcity of such data by using self-alignment principles and a three-phase framework (Alignment, Instantiation, Inference) to generate diverse, verifiable, and challenging questions. Key aspects include graduate/Olympiad-level difficulty, logical consistency, unambiguous answers, and structured control over problem generation.  The authors implement SHARP with a state-of-the-art LRM to infer and verify problems and refine the LRM's reasoning via reinforcement learning. Experiments on GPQA demonstrate significant performance improvements compared to existing methods. The paper contributes the SHARP strategy, the framework design, an end-to-end implementation, and experimental validation demonstrating its effectiveness in improving LRM reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Significant Problem:** The paper tackles a critical bottleneck in LRM development for STEM: the lack of high-quality training data.  Existing approaches often generate simplistic or unverifiable data, hindering model progress.
    *   **Novel Approach:** SHARP presents a structured and principled methodology for synthesizing reasoning problems.  The self-alignment principles and three-phase framework are a significant departure from more ad-hoc data generation methods.  The three-tier categorization in the Instantiation phase is clever for enabling diverse problem selection.
    *   **Emphasis on Verifiability:**  The rigorous focus on answer verifiability is crucial for reliable reinforcement learning. This helps ensure that the model learns genuine reasoning skills rather than simply memorizing patterns in noisy or incorrect data.
    *   **Experimental Validation:**  The performance gains on the challenging GPQA dataset provide compelling evidence for SHARP's effectiveness.  The comparison to existing methods (CoT prompting, and other distillation methods) strengthens the argument for its superiority.
    *   **End-to-end system**: A practical implementation is described, which includes SHARP problem generation, self-verification, and reinforcement learning stages.

* **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a Baseline LRM:** The framework relies heavily on the quality of the initial LRM used for problem generation and verification.  The generated data is inherently biased by the capabilities and limitations of this base model.  The degree to which the *synthesized* data truly surpasses the base model's reasoning capabilities needs more nuanced discussion. This concern is somewhat mitigated by the verification stage with general verifiers.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While GPQA is a challenging benchmark, the experiments are still confined to a single dataset. Further validation on other STEM reasoning tasks would bolster the claims of generalizability.
    *   **Complexity:** The SHARP framework is relatively complex, involving several components and parameters. The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the sensitivity of performance to different parameter settings, and a discussion on the cost/benefit tradeoff for each of its constituent elements.

* **Novelty:** The SHARP approach is novel in its structured, self-aligned method for generating verifiable reasoning problems. While self-alignment is gaining traction, the specific principles and the three-phase framework tailored for STEM are a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:** SHARP's potential impact is significant, especially if it can be shown to generalize across a broader range of STEM domains and can be adapted to generate training data that exceeds the base LRM's capacity.  It offers a pathway to overcome the data scarcity bottleneck, enabling more effective training of LRMs for complex reasoning. It has broad impact because the core issues with training LLMs in complex domains such as STEM are generic.

* **Specific Concerns:** It is unclear what mechanisms are in place to avoid a "feedback loop" where the system becomes increasingly specialized to the particular biases of its base model and verification process. There may be a need for explicit diversity-promoting mechanisms to prevent over-optimization on specific question types or answer formats. Although verifiers obtain general feedback, more analysis in the paper to demonstrate diversity is desired.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a compelling solution to a vital problem in LRM development for STEM. The SHARP strategy and framework are well-designed and show promising experimental results on GPQA. While the framework's dependence on the initial LRM and limited scope are weaknesses, the novelty and potential impact warrant a score of 7. The approach represents a substantial step forward in data synthesis for reasoning, and its emphasis on verifiability is particularly valuable. Future work should focus on addressing the limitations, exploring generalizability, and analyzing the parameter sensitivities.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### MM-Agent: LLM as Agents for Real-world Mathematical Modeling Problem
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14148v1)
- **Authors**: Fan Liu, Zherui Yang, Cancheng Liu, Tianrui Song, Xiaofeng Gao, Hao Liu
- **Abstract**: Mathematical modeling is a cornerstone of scientific discovery and engineering practice, enabling the translation of real-world problems into formal systems across domains such as physics, biology, and economics. Unlike mathematical reasoning, which assumes a predefined formulation, modeling requires open-ended problem analysis, abstraction, and principled formalization. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong reasoning capabilities, they fall short in rigorous model construction, limiting their utility in real-world problem-solving. To this end, we formalize the task of LLM-powered real-world mathematical modeling, where agents must analyze problems, construct domain-appropriate formulations, and generate complete end-to-end solutions. We introduce MM-Bench, a curated benchmark of 111 problems from the Mathematical Contest in Modeling (MCM/ICM), spanning the years 2000 to 2025 and across ten diverse domains such as physics, biology, and economics. To tackle this task, we propose MM-Agent, an expert-inspired framework that decomposes mathematical modeling into four stages: open-ended problem analysis, structured model formulation, computational problem solving, and report generation. Experiments on MM-Bench show that MM-Agent significantly outperforms baseline agents, achieving an 11.88\% improvement over human expert solutions while requiring only 15 minutes and \$0.88 per task using GPT-4o. Furthermore, under official MCM/ICM protocols, MM-Agent assisted two undergraduate teams in winning the Finalist Award (\textbf{top 2.0\% among 27,456 teams}) in MCM/ICM 2025, demonstrating its practical effectiveness as a modeling copilot. Our code is available at https://github.com/usail-hkust/LLM-MM-Agent
- **Summary**: Okay, I understand. Here's a concise summary and a rigorous, critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score with justification:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "MM-Agent," a novel framework designed to tackle real-world mathematical modeling problems using Large Language Models (LLMs). It formalizes the task of LLM-powered real-world mathematical modeling and presents MM-Bench, a curated benchmark of 111 problems from the Mathematical Contest in Modeling (MCM/ICM), spanning various domains and years.  The MM-Agent framework decomposes the mathematical modeling process into four stages: open-ended problem analysis, structured model formulation, computational problem solving, and report generation.  Experiments on MM-Bench demonstrate MM-Agent's significant outperformance of baseline agents, achieving an improvement even over human expert solutions. The paper also highlights the successful application of MM-Agent as a copilot in the MCM/ICM competition.

**Critical Evaluation (Novelty, Significance, and Impact):**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty of Problem Formulation:** The paper tackles a crucial gap in LLM research by formalizing the task of real-world mathematical modeling, which goes beyond mathematical reasoning by requiring open-ended problem analysis and model formulation. This is a significant departure from existing LLM benchmarks that primarily focus on well-defined mathematical problems.
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark (MM-Bench):** The creation of MM-Bench is a major contribution.  A real-world dataset, large enough to show practical significance and covering multiple problems types.
    *   **Expert-Inspired Framework (MM-Agent):**  The design of MM-Agent, inspired by expert workflows and incorporating a Hierarchical Mathematical Modeling Library (HMML), addresses a key limitation of existing LLM agents: their inability to capture essential modeling principles like abstraction and assumption design. The integration of an actor-critic mechanism and MLE-Solver further enhances the framework's robustness and efficiency.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results on MM-Bench provide strong evidence of MM-Agent's effectiveness, demonstrating significant improvements over baseline agents and even exceeding human expert performance. The real-world validation through the MCM/ICM competition provides practical proof of concept.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:**  The framework is computationally intensive. While the paper highlights that it's more efficient than some other "research agent" approaches, the barrier to adoption may be substantial in practice.
    *   **Generalization Potential:** Real world modeling is a wide, diverse field. This data comes primarily from a single source, MCM/ICM. The agent's abilities may be overfitted to this specific environment.

*   **Significance and Potential Impact:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of AI-assisted scientific discovery and engineering. By providing a framework for automating mathematical modeling, it can empower researchers and practitioners to tackle complex real-world problems more efficiently and effectively.
    *   The MM-Bench benchmark can serve as a valuable resource for evaluating and comparing different LLM-based modeling agents, driving further research and development in this area.
    *   The successful application of MM-Agent as a modeling copilot suggests that LLMs can play a crucial role in enhancing human expertise and accelerating scientific innovation.

*   **Critical Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **8** out of 10. The paper makes a substantial contribution to the field by formalizing a crucial task, creating a comprehensive benchmark, and developing an effective framework for real-world mathematical modeling.  The strong empirical validation and real-world application further solidify its significance. While the computational cost and reliance on a specific dataset are limitations, the potential impact of this work on AI-assisted scientific discovery and engineering is substantial. MM-Agent provides a solid framework and clear opportunities for improving in the future.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 0/10

### Unify Graph Learning with Text: Unleashing LLM Potentials for Session Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14156v1)
- **Authors**: Songhao Wu, Quan Tu, Hong Liu, Jia Xu, Zhongyi Liu, Guannan Zhang, Ran Wang, Xiuying Chen, Rui Yan
- **Abstract**: Session search involves a series of interactive queries and actions to fulfill user's complex information need. Current strategies typically prioritize sequential modeling for deep semantic understanding, overlooking the graph structure in interactions. While some approaches focus on capturing structural information, they use a generalized representation for documents, neglecting the word-level semantic modeling. In this paper, we propose Symbolic Graph Ranker (SGR), which aims to take advantage of both text-based and graph-based approaches by leveraging the power of recent Large Language Models (LLMs). Concretely, we first introduce a set of symbolic grammar rules to convert session graph into text. This allows integrating session history, interaction process, and task instruction seamlessly as inputs for the LLM. Moreover, given the natural discrepancy between LLMs pre-trained on textual corpora, and the symbolic language we produce using our graph-to-text grammar, our objective is to enhance LLMs' ability to capture graph structures within a textual format. To achieve this, we introduce a set of self-supervised symbolic learning tasks including link prediction, node content generation, and generative contrastive learning, to enable LLMs to capture the topological information from coarse-grained to fine-grained. Experiment results and comprehensive analysis on two benchmark datasets, AOL and Tiangong-ST, confirm the superiority of our approach. Our paradigm also offers a novel and effective methodology that bridges the gap between traditional search strategies and modern LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Unify Graph Learning with Text: Unleashing LLM Potentials for Session Search" proposes a novel approach, Symbolic Graph Ranker (SGR), for session search that leverages the power of Large Language Models (LLMs). SGR combines the benefits of sequential modeling (for semantic understanding) and graph modeling (for capturing user behavior structure).  It converts the session graph into a text-based representation using symbolic grammar rules, allowing LLMs to process both semantic and structural information. To address the discrepancy between LLMs (pre-trained on plain text) and the symbolic representation of graphs, the authors introduce three self-supervised learning tasks: link prediction, node content generation, and generative contrastive learning. Experimental results on AOL and Tiangong-ST datasets demonstrate that SGR outperforms existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the fusion of graph-based and text-based approaches for session search via LLMs.  Prior work typically focuses on one or the other. Converting graph structures to a textual format is a relatively new approach, particularly combined with tailored self-supervised learning tasks to enhance LLM's understanding of these symbolic graphs. Prior works also leverage LLMs with graph data but most times these are treated as separate modules or are not specifically tuned to understand the graph topological information. The specific grammar rules for conversion and the designed pre-training tasks are a significant contribution.

* **Significance:** Session search is a crucial area for improving search engine performance. This work could have a good impact, because it attempts to bridge the gap between structured user interaction data and LLM-driven semantic understanding. The demonstrated performance gains suggest the proposed method has practical value. Additionally, this paper opens avenues for future research towards better incorporating graph information within LLMs. The approach, of converting other modalities into text, is also a promising approach to many multimodal problems.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents convincing experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness of SGR compared to strong baselines across multiple evaluation metrics and datasets.
    * **Well-Designed Symbolic Learning Tasks:** The self-supervised learning tasks seem well-motivated and effectively address the challenge of adapting LLMs to understand graph structures presented in symbolic form.
    * **Comprehensive Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are essential for understanding the contribution of individual components of SGR, and clearly shows the importance of using the symbolic representation and pre-training tasks.
    * **Addresses a key issue:** The paper tackles the critical challenge of bridging between traditional search strategies and state-of-the-art language models.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity of Symbolic Grammar:** The design and implementation of the symbolic grammar rules are not trivial. This could potentially limit the method's applicability to other domains if significant effort is needed to tailor the grammar rules for different types of graphs.
    * **Generalizability:** The experiments are limited to session search. It is not immediately clear how this approach would generalize to other tasks that involve graph-structured data.
    * **Dependency on LLM Scale:** The paper relies on the power of large LLMs. If LLMs become more difficult to access or more computationally expensive, the practicality of this approach could be affected.
    * **Limited Analysis:** More detailed analysis of the limitations and failure cases of the proposed method would be helpful. It would be beneficial to understand scenarios where SGR struggles or where other methods perform better.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper introduces a potentially new paradigm for session search that combines graph and text-based approaches.  It could influence future research to explore similar approaches for adapting LLMs to other structured data formats and tasks. The symbolic learning approach might also inspire new pre-training strategies for LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has some limitations, the core idea of converting graph data into a symbolic textual representation for consumption by LLMs, coupled with targeted self-supervised pre-training tasks, shows significant promise. The experiments demonstrate that this approach yields strong performance improvements over existing session search methods. The well-designed ablation studies provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of the symbolic representation and the pre-training strategies. The work successfully bridges different modalities in a manner that can potentially have impacts outside session search. These factors warrant a high rating.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Temporal Alignment of Time Sensitive Facts with Activation Engineering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14158v1)
- **Authors**: Sanjay Govindan, Maurice Pagnucco, Yang Song
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are trained on diverse and often conflicting knowledge spanning multiple domains and time periods. Some of this knowledge is only valid within specific temporal contexts, such as answering the question, "Who is the President of the United States in 2022?" Ensuring LLMs generate time appropriate responses is crucial for maintaining relevance and accuracy. In this work we explore activation engineering as a method for temporally aligning LLMs to improve factual recall without any training or dataset creation. In this research we explore an activation engineering technique to ground three versions of LLaMA 2 to specific points in time and examine the effects of varying injection layers and prompting strategies. Our experiments demonstrate up to a 44% and 16% improvement in relative and explicit prompting respectively, achieving comparable performance to the fine-tuning method proposed by Zhao et al. (2024) . Notably, our approach achieves similar results to the fine-tuning baseline while being significantly more computationally efficient and requiring no pre-aligned datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores activation engineering (AE) as a method for temporally aligning Large Language Models (LLMs), aiming to improve factual recall for time-sensitive queries.  The authors apply AE to LLaMA2 models, injecting vectors into the residual stream to represent specific time periods. They experiment with different injection layers and prompting strategies and compare the results against relative prompting, explicit prompting, and fine-tuning baselines using the Head of Governments (HOG) and Temporal Alignment Question Answer (Taqa) datasets. The paper argues that AE offers a computationally efficient alternative to fine-tuning, requiring less training data while achieving comparable performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using activation engineering for *temporal alignment* of LLMs is relatively novel.  While AE has been used for other tasks like toxicity reduction or topic fixation, its application to aligning model knowledge to specific time periods, particularly for improving factual recall, is a fresh perspective. The comparison with fine-tuning further strengthens this point. Prior work, as acknowledged by the authors, focuses mainly on knowledge editing or continual learning for overwriting facts.

* **Significance:**  The paper's significance lies in its potential to provide a lightweight and efficient approach for dealing with the temporal drift of knowledge in LLMs. Fine-tuning is computationally expensive and requires substantial data. AE offers a potentially more scalable way to influence model behavior without retraining the entire model.  The results, showing comparable performance to fine-tuning but with reduced computational overhead, are encouraging and could open up new avenues for managing time-sensitive information in LLMs.  The finding that multi-layer AE can be more stable than single-layer AE is also a valuable contribution.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and motivation.
    * Rigorous experimental setup with multiple datasets (HOG and Taqa) and LLaMA2 model sizes.
    * Comprehensive comparison against relevant baselines.
    * Detailed ablation studies exploring the impact of different layers and prompting strategies.
    * Explicit discussion of limitations, such as the limited investigation of the effect on model confidence.
    * Focus on computational efficiency.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * While the results are promising, the gains are sometimes incremental compared to explicit prompting. The authors should delve deeper into the scenarios where AE provides the most significant advantage over simpler techniques.
    * The paper could benefit from more analysis of the *types* of questions and facts where AE is most effective. Are there specific temporal relationships (e.g., identifying a start date vs. an end date) where AE excels?
    * The explanation of the mechanism by which AE achieves temporal alignment could be strengthened. The authors hypothesize that AE nudges the model towards specific time periods, but a more detailed analysis of the changes in activation patterns would be beneficial.
    * The comparison to fine-tuning only used PEFT LORA for LLaMA-70b due to computational constraints, potentially skewing the comparison. Ideally, a full parameter fine-tuning experiment would have been done.

* **Potential Influence:**  If the claims of comparable performance with lower computational overhead hold up in future studies and larger-scale applications, this work could significantly influence how LLMs are deployed in scenarios where time-sensitive information is critical (e.g., chatbots, information retrieval systems). It has the potential to stimulate further research into AE techniques for temporal alignment and knowledge management in LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating the potential of activation engineering for temporal alignment of LLMs. While the performance gains are not always dramatic and further investigation is needed to fully understand the mechanism and optimal application scenarios, the computational efficiency of the proposed approach, combined with the comprehensive experimental evaluation and explicit acknowledgement of limitations, warrants a positive assessment. While the significance is notable, several points of improvement, such as the lack of full fine-tuning for all models, prevents this from receiving a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LMP: Leveraging Motion Prior in Zero-Shot Video Generation with Diffusion Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14167v1)
- **Authors**: Changgu Chen, Xiaoyan Yang, Junwei Shu, Changbo Wang, Yang Li
- **Abstract**: In recent years, large-scale pre-trained diffusion transformer models have made significant progress in video generation. While current DiT models can produce high-definition, high-frame-rate, and highly diverse videos, there is a lack of fine-grained control over the video content. Controlling the motion of subjects in videos using only prompts is challenging, especially when it comes to describing complex movements. Further, existing methods fail to control the motion in image-to-video generation, as the subject in the reference image often differs from the subject in the reference video in terms of initial position, size, and shape. To address this, we propose the Leveraging Motion Prior (LMP) framework for zero-shot video generation. Our framework harnesses the powerful generative capabilities of pre-trained diffusion transformers to enable motion in the generated videos to reference user-provided motion videos in both text-to-video and image-to-video generation. To this end, we first introduce a foreground-background disentangle module to distinguish between moving subjects and backgrounds in the reference video, preventing interference in the target video generation. A reweighted motion transfer module is designed to allow the target video to reference the motion from the reference video. To avoid interference from the subject in the reference video, we propose an appearance separation module to suppress the appearance of the reference subject in the target video. We annotate the DAVIS dataset with detailed prompts for our experiments and design evaluation metrics to validate the effectiveness of our method. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance in generation quality, prompt-video consistency, and control capability. Our homepage is available at https://vpx-ecnu.github.io/LMP-Website/
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LMP: Leveraging Motion Prior in Zero-Shot Video Generation with Diffusion Transformer."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LMP (Leveraging Motion Prior), a zero-shot framework that allows DiT (Diffusion Transformer)-based video generation models to control the motion of generated videos using a reference video. The core idea is to transfer the motion from a reference video to a newly generated video, even when the subject or background in the reference video differs from what's intended in the generated video.  The LMP framework consists of three main modules:

1.  **Foreground-Background Disentangle Module (FBDM):** Separates the moving subject (foreground) from the background in the reference video using the attention maps present in the DiT architecture. This prevents the background of the reference video from influencing the generated video.

2.  **Reweighted Motion Transfer Module (RMTM):**  Injects tokens representing the foreground motion from the reference video into the generation process of the target video. It uses a reweighting mechanism to balance the motion transfer with the prompt information to not overly constrain the generated output to follow the reference video's style or structure.

3.  **Appearance Separation Module (ASM):** Removes subject appearance information from the reference video tokens to prevent the subject's appearance in the reference video from affecting the subject's appearance in the generated video. This is particularly important when the subjects in the reference and target videos are different. Gradient descent is used to minimize attention losses based on the subjects in the respective prompts.

The authors annotate the DAVIS dataset with detailed prompts to facilitate evaluation and design specific evaluation metrics. Experiments demonstrate that LMP can generate high-quality videos that are consistent with both the text prompts and the motion in the reference video.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in applying a zero-shot motion transfer approach specifically to DiT architectures, which have recently become dominant in video generation. While previous works have explored motion control, they often rely on UNet architectures or require training. The adaptation to the DiT architecture is a key contribution. The modular approach is also novel - combining foreground disentanglement, reweighted motion transfer, and appearance suppression.

*   **Significance:** The ability to control motion in generated videos is a significant advancement, as it allows for more precise and creative control over the output. A zero-shot approach is valuable as it avoids the need for task-specific training data and allows for greater flexibility. By leveraging the attention mechanism present within the DiT architecture, they have created a model that is plug-and-play, so to speak.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The modular design is a significant strength.  Each module addresses a specific challenge in motion transfer, making the framework more robust and interpretable.
    *   The paper provides a clear explanation of the method, including the rationale behind each module and the implementation details.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating that LMP can generate high-quality videos with accurate motion transfer and subject/background control.
    *   Annotation of the DAVIS dataset contributes to a more rigorous evaluation of motion transfer methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The quantitative evaluation relies heavily on CLIP-based metrics and a motion fidelity score. These metrics, while useful, might not fully capture the nuanced aspects of motion quality or the aesthetic appeal of the generated videos. More user studies or comparisons with additional metrics could strengthen the evaluation.
    *   While LMP achieves impressive results, it's still a zero-shot approach. The quality of the motion transfer may be limited by the capabilities of the pre-trained DiT model and the accuracy of the foreground-background disentanglement. There might be scenarios where fine-tuning or training could yield even better results.
    *   The reliance on LLMs to obtain prompts could introduce biases from the LLM. While effective for dataset annotation, it is unclear how it would affect performance in different domains/prompts.
    *  The paper mentions limitations with generating videos with 24 frames for comparison purposes, but no details are provided for why this is a hard limit.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of video generation. The zero-shot nature of LMP makes it a practical tool for controlling motion in generated videos. The paper's clear explanation and modular design could inspire other researchers to develop more advanced motion control techniques for diffusion models.  The annotated DAVIS dataset can also serve as a valuable resource for future research. It paves the way for greater control and creativity in video creation.

*   **Limitations in the paper:**
    * The prompts need to be relevant to the reference video to prevent the FBDM from removing key elements from the image.
    * The method is limited to rigid motion and may struggle with significant pose changes.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

LMP is a well-executed and novel approach to zero-shot motion transfer in DiT-based video generation. The modular design, clear explanation, and compelling experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness. The method addresses a significant challenge in video generation, offering a flexible and practical solution. However, the evaluation metrics could be more comprehensive, and the zero-shot nature of the approach limits the extent to which motion can be controlled. Furthermore, the reliance on LLM prompts introduce some concerns. Although a strong contribution, the limitations described above keep the score from being higher.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Strawberry Problem: Emergence of Character-level Understanding in Tokenized Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14172v1)
- **Authors**: Adrian Cosma, Stefan Ruseti, Emilian Radoi, Mihai Dascalu
- **Abstract**: Despite their remarkable progress across diverse domains, Large Language Models (LLMs) consistently fail at simple character-level tasks, such as counting letters in words, due to a fundamental limitation: tokenization. In this work, we frame this limitation as a problem of low mutual information and analyze it in terms of concept emergence. Using a suite of 19 synthetic tasks that isolate character-level reasoning in a controlled setting, we show that such capabilities emerge slowly, suddenly, and only late in training. We further show that percolation-based models of concept emergence explain these patterns, suggesting that learning character composition is not fundamentally different from learning commonsense knowledge. To address this bottleneck, we propose a lightweight architectural modification that significantly improves character-level reasoning while preserving the inductive advantages of subword models. Together, our results bridge low-level perceptual gaps in tokenized LMs and provide a principled framework for understanding and mitigating their structural blind spots. We make our code publicly available.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper. Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper, "The Strawberry Problem: Emergence of Character-level Understanding in Tokenized Language Models," investigates why Large Language Models (LLMs) struggle with seemingly simple character-level tasks (like counting letters in a word), despite their impressive capabilities in other domains. The authors attribute this to tokenization, a preprocessing step that breaks text into subword units, effectively severing the connection between words and their constituent characters. They frame this limitation as a problem of low mutual information and analyze it through the lens of concept emergence.

The authors create a suite of 19 synthetic tasks designed to isolate and test character-level reasoning in a controlled environment. They show that these capabilities emerge slowly and late in training, are influenced by vocabulary size and composition, and align with predictions from percolation-based models of emergence. To address this, they propose a lightweight architectural modification: a block cross-attention mechanism that exposes character-level information to the model alongside token embeddings.  They demonstrate that this significantly improves character-level reasoning without sacrificing the inductive biases of subword tokenization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the "Strawberry Problem" and provides a compelling rationale for why it exists in tokenized language models. Framing it as a low mutual information problem and connecting it to concept emergence provides a useful perspective.
*   **Controlled Experiments:** The use of synthetic tasks allows for a rigorous and systematic investigation of character-level reasoning. The controlled setting reduces confounding factors and allows for more precise analysis.
*   **Interesting Findings:** The observation that character-level capabilities emerge slowly, late in training, and are influenced by vocabulary size is significant and provides insights into the learning dynamics of LLMs.
*   **Percolation Theory Connection:** Linking the emergence of character-level understanding to percolation theory and concept learning is a novel and thought-provoking contribution. It suggests that these capabilities are not fundamentally different from learning other abstract concepts.
*   **Practical Solution:** The proposed architectural modification (block cross-attention) is a practical and relatively simple solution that improves character-level reasoning without sacrificing the benefits of tokenization.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors emphasize reproducibility and make their code publicly available.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Real-World Validation:** While the synthetic experiments are rigorous, the extent to which the findings generalize to more complex, real-world scenarios could be strengthened.  The paper does include some experiments on Wikipedia data using pretrained tokenizers, but more extensive validation would be beneficial.
*   **Architectural Novelty:** While effective, the proposed architectural modification (block cross-attention) isn't groundbreaking in itself. Cross-attention and hierarchical architectures are well-established techniques. The novelty lies more in *how* it's applied to address this specific problem.
*   **Oversimplification:** The idealized synthetic setting, while beneficial for control, may oversimplify the complexities of character-level reasoning in natural language. The tasks are relatively simple manipulations.
*   **"Inverse Scaling" Claim:** The statement that character understanding tasks are a form of "inverse scaling" needs further investigation. The link between tokenizer vocabulary size and character understanding performance is more likely due to larger tokenizer vocabularies over-splitting words which reduces context provided to the LLM. The authors would benefit from further validation of this claim.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by:

*   **Framing:** Clearly framing and analyzing the "Strawberry Problem" as a low mutual information issue within the context of concept emergence.
*   **Experiments:** Conducting a systematic empirical evaluation of character-level reasoning capabilities in LLMs.
*   **Connection:** Suggesting the novel approach of relating percolation theory to the lack of character-level understanding in language models.
*   **Addressing:** Proposing and evaluating a straightforward yet effective architectural intervention.

The work provides valuable insights into the limitations of tokenized language models and offers a practical solution to improve their character-level reasoning abilities. This is increasingly important as LLMs are deployed in applications where understanding and manipulating text at a granular level is crucial.

**Score:**

Score: 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a rigorous and insightful analysis of a known problem (LLMs struggling with character-level tasks). The use of synthetic tasks and the connection to percolation theory are novel and contribute to our understanding of concept emergence in LLMs. The proposed architectural modification is a practical and effective solution, although it's not fundamentally groundbreaking. The main limitation is the need for more extensive validation on real-world data and tasks, as well as further investigation into the counter-intuitive "inverse scaling" conclusion. Despite these limitations, the paper provides valuable insights and a practical approach to addressing a relevant problem in the field. Therefore, it warrants a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cheaper, Better, Faster, Stronger: Robust Text-to-SQL without Chain-of-Thought or Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14174v1)
- **Authors**: Yusuf Denizay Dönder, Derek Hommel, Andrea W Wen-Yi, David Mimno, Unso Eun Seo Jo
- **Abstract**: LLMs are effective at code generation tasks like text-to-SQL, but is it worth the cost? Many state-of-the-art approaches use non-task-specific LLM techniques including Chain-of-Thought (CoT), self-consistency, and fine-tuning. These methods can be costly at inference time, sometimes requiring over a hundred LLM calls with reasoning, incurring average costs of up to \$0.46 per query, while fine-tuning models can cost thousands of dollars. We introduce "N-rep" consistency, a more cost-efficient text-to-SQL approach that achieves similar BIRD benchmark scores as other more expensive methods, at only \$0.039 per query. N-rep leverages multiple representations of the same schema input to mitigate weaknesses in any single representation, making the solution more robust and allowing the use of smaller and cheaper models without any reasoning or fine-tuning. To our knowledge, N-rep is the best-performing text-to-SQL approach in its cost range.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "N-rep" consistency, a novel text-to-SQL approach designed to be more cost-effective than existing methods. N-rep achieves comparable BIRD benchmark scores to state-of-the-art approaches, but at a significantly lower inference cost by leveraging multiple text representations of the database schema and intelligent filtering. It avoids expensive techniques like Chain-of-Thought (CoT) and fine-tuning by using smaller models in conjunction with N-rep, achieving an order of magnitude cost reduction. The method leverages n different text representations of the database schema, filtering these with schema linking, which results in a more robust solution to input sensitivity and schema linking errors than other consistency-based approaches. The authors claim that N-rep is the best-performing text-to-SQL approach in its cost range.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using multiple representations of the database schema for text-to-SQL is novel. While other approaches have explored variations in schema formatting, the systematic exploitation of different representations as a mechanism to improve robustness *without* expensive LLM reasoning or fine-tuning, is a distinctive contribution. The confidence-aware candidate selection strategy also represents a practical improvement over simple voting or relying solely on LLM-based selection, as it adaptively applies LLM selection only when necessary.

*   **Significance:**  The paper's primary significance lies in its demonstration of how to achieve competitive text-to-SQL performance with drastically reduced computational costs.  This is crucial for democratizing access to LLM-based solutions, particularly for real-world applications where cost is a major barrier. The focus on cost-effectiveness aligns well with the growing concern about the environmental and economic impacts of large language models. Also, demonstrating a robust approach that doesn't depend on specific LLM architectures or excessive fine-tuning is important, as these architectures are rapidly changing.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Cost-Effectiveness:** The most compelling strength is the substantial cost reduction compared to CoT and fine-tuning methods, supported by empirical evidence on the BIRD benchmark.
    *   **Robustness:** The use of multiple representations and schema linking filtering enhance robustness to input variations and schema linking errors.
    *   **Practicality:** The method avoids the need for extensive data labeling or model training, making it easier to deploy and adapt to new databases.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Manual Selection:** The requirement of manually selecting "best" schema representations is a potential limitation, as the optimal representations may depend on the specific database or query distribution. The authors acknowledge this limitation but propose it as an area of future work.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:**  While the paper provides cost comparisons and overall performance results, deeper ablation studies on the contribution of each representation and the influence of different numbers of representations would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Benchmarks:** While BIRD is a complex dataset, it's still limited in scope. The paper could be strengthened by including evaluation on other datasets, especially those focusing on specific domains.
    *   **Candidate Diversity:** The paper's motivation suggests that varying schema representations creates candidate diversity. A more explicit measure of candidate diversity would enhance the argument.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by highlighting the importance of cost-effectiveness in text-to-SQL research and development. It presents a viable alternative to resource-intensive methods, encouraging researchers to explore more efficient approaches that leverage domain-specific knowledge rather than relying solely on model scale.

**Justification for Score:**

While the idea of multiple schema representations is simple, its effective exploitation for cost reduction and robustness makes a notable contribution. The authors demonstrate significant practical benefits on a challenging benchmark.  However, the limitations regarding manual representation selection and the need for further ablation studies temper the overall impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Tokenization Constraints in LLMs: A Study of Symbolic and Arithmetic Reasoning Limits
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14178v1)
- **Authors**: Xiang Zhang, Juntai Cao, Jiaqi Wei, Yiwei Xu, Chenyu You
- **Abstract**: Tokenization is the first - and often underappreciated - layer of computation in language models. While Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting enables transformer models to approximate recurrent computation by externalizing intermediate steps, we show that the success of such reasoning is fundamentally bounded by the structure of tokenized inputs. This work presents a theoretical and empirical investigation into how tokenization schemes, particularly subword-based methods like byte-pair encoding (BPE), impede symbolic computation by merging or obscuring atomic reasoning units. We introduce the notion of Token Awareness to formalize how poor token granularity disrupts logical alignment and prevents models from generalizing symbolic procedures. Through systematic evaluation on arithmetic and symbolic tasks, we demonstrate that token structure dramatically affect reasoning performance, causing failure even with CoT, while atomically-aligned formats unlock strong generalization, allowing small models (e.g., GPT-4o-mini) to outperform larger systems (e.g., o1) in structured reasoning. Our findings reveal that symbolic reasoning ability in LLMs is not purely architectural, but deeply conditioned on token-level representations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Tokenization Constraints in LLMs: A Study of Symbolic and Arithmetic Reasoning Limits":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of tokenization schemes, particularly subword-based methods like Byte Pair Encoding (BPE), on the symbolic and arithmetic reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors argue that tokenization, often an underappreciated aspect of LLM design, can significantly hinder reasoning performance by merging or obscuring atomic reasoning units. They introduce the concept of "Token Awareness" to formalize how poor token granularity disrupts logical alignment and prevents models from generalizing symbolic procedures. Through experiments on arithmetic and symbolic tasks, they demonstrate that token structure heavily influences reasoning, causing failure even with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, while atomically aligned formats unlock strong generalization.  The paper's central finding is that symbolic reasoning ability in LLMs is deeply conditioned on token-level representations, not solely architectural.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the connection between tokenization and LLM performance isn't entirely new, the paper provides a more systematic and in-depth analysis, particularly focusing on symbolic and arithmetic reasoning. The introduction of "Token Awareness" offers a useful framework for understanding the problem. The detailed empirical analysis comparing various tokenization strategies is valuable. However, some prior work has also pointed out the role of tokenization, especially with BPE, impacting performance, so the novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have important implications for LLM design and application. It highlights a critical limitation of existing models and suggests that improvements in tokenization could lead to substantial gains in symbolic reasoning, even without architectural changes or massive scaling. The paper's emphasis on the interplay between tokenization and CoT prompting is also significant, demonstrating that CoT's effectiveness is contingent on the quality of token representations. The paper's model-agnostic approach also makes it very useful for practical application.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a strong theoretical argument about the limits of fixed-depth Transformers for tasks requiring sequential computation.
    *   The empirical evaluation is thorough and well-controlled, systematically comparing different tokenization schemes and LLMs.
    *   The authors provide a clear definition of "Token Awareness" and relate it to reasoning performance.
    *   The focus on arithmetic and symbolic tasks provides a concrete and measurable way to assess the impact of tokenization.
    *   The model-agnostic methodology also makes the work more impactful, as it can be applied to closed-source models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper's scope is somewhat limited to symbolic and arithmetic reasoning. While important, it doesn't address other potential areas where tokenization could impact LLM performance.
    *   The study primarily uses relatively simple arithmetic and symbolic tasks. While this allows for controlled analysis, it's unclear how well the findings generalize to more complex, real-world scenarios.
    *   The paper's suggestions for improving tokenization are somewhat vague. While it identifies the problem, it doesn't offer concrete solutions or algorithms for designing better tokenization schemes. More details on what a "better" tokenization is would have been beneficial.
    *   The paper could have explored the relationship between tokenization and training data in more detail. Does the training data reflect the tokenization format used during inference, and how does this affect generalization?

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence future research in several ways:
    *   It may encourage more researchers to consider tokenization as a critical design factor in LLMs.
    *   It could spur the development of new tokenization schemes that are better suited for symbolic reasoning.
    *   It may lead to the development of techniques for mitigating the negative effects of existing tokenization schemes.
    *   It will provide impetus for more theoretical investigations on the effect of tokenization in modern LLMs, in particular those that are very large and complex, so that a deeper understanding of tokenization may lead to more insights on design and usage.

*   **Score Justification:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a valuable and well-supported contribution to our understanding of LLMs. The systematic analysis of tokenization constraints on symbolic reasoning is novel and significant, highlighting an often overlooked aspect of LLM design. While the paper's scope is somewhat limited, the findings have broad implications for improving LLM performance. The weaknesses are that the paper mostly focuses on the effect, but lack the details of the cause and potential remedies. The proposed idea of token awareness, even though intriguing, is a bit high level, and would require more formalization. As such, the paper is a step towards the right direction, but there is still a distance to go to arrive at a more conclusive result.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SlangDIT: Benchmarking LLMs in Interpretative Slang Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14181v1)
- **Authors**: Yunlong Liang, Fandong Meng, Jiaan Wang, Jie Zhou
- **Abstract**: The challenge of slang translation lies in capturing context-dependent semantic extensions, as slang terms often convey meanings beyond their literal interpretation. While slang detection, explanation, and translation have been studied as isolated tasks in the era of large language models (LLMs), their intrinsic interdependence remains underexplored. The main reason is lacking of a benchmark where the two tasks can be a prerequisite for the third one, which can facilitate idiomatic translation. In this paper, we introduce the interpretative slang translation task (named SlangDIT) consisting of three sub-tasks: slang detection, cross-lingual slang explanation, and slang translation within the current context, aiming to generate more accurate translation with the help of slang detection and slang explanation. To this end, we construct a SlangDIT dataset, containing over 25k English-Chinese sentence pairs. Each source sentence mentions at least one slang term and is labeled with corresponding cross-lingual slang explanation. Based on the benchmark, we propose a deep thinking model, named SlangOWL. It firstly identifies whether the sentence contains a slang, and then judges whether the slang is polysemous and analyze its possible meaning. Further, the SlangOWL provides the best explanation of the slang term targeting on the current context. Finally, according to the whole thought, the SlangOWL offers a suitable translation. Our experiments on LLMs (\emph{e.g.}, Qwen2.5 and LLama-3.1), show that our deep thinking approach indeed enhances the performance of LLMs where the proposed SLangOWL significantly surpasses the vanilla models and supervised fine-tuned models without thinking.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SlangDIT: Benchmarking LLMs in Interpretative Slang Translation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new task called Interpretative Slang Translation (SlangDIT) to address the challenge of translating slang, which often relies on context-dependent semantic extensions. The task is designed to evaluate LLMs on three sub-tasks: slang detection, cross-lingual slang explanation, and slang translation within a given context. To facilitate research, the authors construct a SlangDIT dataset containing over 25,000 English-Chinese sentence pairs with slang terms and corresponding cross-lingual explanations. They also propose a deep thinking model named SlangOWL, which first identifies slang, judges its polysemy, analyzes its meaning, provides a cross-lingual explanation, and finally, translates the sentence. Experimental results on LLMs such as Qwen2.5 and Llama-3 show that the SlangOWL model significantly outperforms vanilla and supervised fine-tuned models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The paper's novelty lies primarily in introducing the SlangDIT task and associated dataset. While slang detection, explanation, and translation have been studied before, the integrated task and the focus on polysemous slang terms are relatively novel. This addresses a gap in the literature where the interdependence of these tasks is often overlooked.

    *   The SlangOWL model, while leveraging existing LLM backbones, introduces a deep thinking approach that emulates human cognitive processes in understanding and translating slang. This aspect also contributes to the novelty.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The SlangDIT dataset is a valuable resource for the community, providing a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on a challenging aspect of natural language understanding and translation. The dataset's size and the inclusion of polysemous slang terms enhance its utility.

    *   The SlangOWL model demonstrates the potential of incorporating deep thinking approaches into LLMs for improving the accuracy of slang translation. This has implications for developing more robust and context-aware machine translation systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly defines the problem and motivates the need for a dedicated benchmark for interpretative slang translation.

    *   The dataset creation process is well-described, and the authors take steps to ensure data quality through multiple rounds of annotation and validation.

    *   The SlangOWL model is well-designed and demonstrates significant performance improvements over baseline models.

    *   The experiments are comprehensive, and the results are presented clearly.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The dataset is limited to English-Chinese language pairs. Expanding the dataset to include other languages would increase its broader impact.
    *   The study primarily focuses on leveraging existing LLM backbones. Exploring novel architectures or training techniques specifically tailored for slang translation could further enhance the research.
    *   The SlangOWL model, while effective, may be computationally expensive due to the deep thinking approach. Further optimization could improve its efficiency.
    *   The evaluation, while using standard metrics, still relies heavily on reference-based metrics. Exploring human evaluation or task-specific metrics could provide a more nuanced assessment of translation quality.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The SlangDIT task and dataset are likely to stimulate further research in the area of slang understanding and translation, encouraging the development of more sophisticated models.

    *   The SlangOWL model's deep thinking approach could inspire other researchers to explore similar techniques for addressing other challenging aspects of natural language processing.

    *   The work could contribute to the development of more robust and culturally sensitive machine translation systems that are better equipped to handle the nuances of slang.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a novel task (SlangDIT) and a well-constructed dataset that addresses a real-world challenge in machine translation. The SlangOWL model demonstrates the effectiveness of deep thinking approaches in improving slang translation accuracy. While there are some limitations (dataset language scope, computational cost), the work is well-executed, clearly presented, and has the potential to influence future research in the field. The score of 8 reflects the paper's novelty, significance, and potential influence, balanced against the limitations and areas for future improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### ThinkSwitcher: When to Think Hard, When to Think Fast
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14183v1)
- **Authors**: Guosheng Liang, Longguang Zhong, Ziyi Yang, Xiaojun Quan
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) excel at solving complex tasks by leveraging long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. However, this often leads to overthinking on simple tasks, resulting in unnecessary computational overhead. We observe that LRMs inherently possess the capability for efficient short CoT reasoning, which can be reliably elicited through prompt design. To leverage this capability, we propose ThinkSwitcher, a framework that enables a single LRM to dynamically switch between short and long CoT modes based on task complexity. ThinkSwitcher introduces a lightweight switching module trained with supervision signals derived from the relative performance of each reasoning mode across tasks. Experiments on multiple reasoning benchmarks show that ThinkSwitcher reduces computational cost by 20-30% while maintaining high accuracy on complex tasks. This demonstrates the effectiveness of ThinkSwitcher as a scalable and efficient solution for unified LRM deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ThinkSwitcher: When to Think Hard, When to Think Fast":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ThinkSwitcher, a framework designed to enable large reasoning models (LRMs) to dynamically switch between long and short chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning modes based on the complexity of the task. The core idea is that LRMs often "overthink" simple problems, leading to unnecessary computational overhead. ThinkSwitcher employs a lightweight switching module, trained with self-supervised signals derived from the LRM's own performance in both reasoning modes, to predict which mode is most appropriate for a given query. Experiments across several reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ThinkSwitcher can reduce computational costs (measured by token usage) by 20-30% while maintaining high accuracy on complex tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of adaptively controlling the reasoning depth of LRMs is not entirely new. Dual-mode systems and techniques to reduce CoT length have been explored before. The novelty lies in the *implementation* and *training strategy*. The use of a lightweight, self-supervised switching module that doesn't require extensive post-training or backbone modifications is a practical contribution. The observation that simply adding an empty `<think></think>` block encourages short CoT reasoning is a valuable empirical finding and makes their prompt design efficient. Further, their claim of theoretical justification of short CoT is a strong aspect of their paper.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant practical challenge: deploying LRMs efficiently in scenarios where both complex and simple tasks are common. Reducing the computational burden of LRMs is crucial for wider adoption, particularly in high-throughput applications. The demonstrated results (20-30% token reduction) are substantial and practically meaningful. This framework also contributes towards better utilization of resources and improved scalability of such models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Simplicity and Efficiency:** The framework is lightweight and doesn't require extensive retraining of the underlying LRM.
    *   **Self-Supervision:**  The use of self-supervision eliminates the need for labeled data for training the switching module.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling experimental results across multiple datasets and LRM sizes.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper provides detailed analyses of decision dynamics, training objective impact, and computational efficiency.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Task Scope:** The paper primarily focuses on mathematical reasoning tasks. While these tasks are relevant, the generalizability of ThinkSwitcher to other reasoning domains (e.g., code generation, natural language inference) is not fully explored.
    *   **Model Size Constraint:** Experiments are limited to models up to 14B parameters. Evaluation on larger models would strengthen the claims of scalability.
    *   **Threshold Tuning:** The method requires tuning of the threshold `τ`, which might need to be adjusted based on the specific dataset and LRM being used. The impact of this parameter on robustness could be explored further.
    *   **Limited Explanation:** There is need to investigate further why their observation, to reliably elicit short CoT using an empty thinking block, holds true.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design and deployment of LRMs, particularly in resource-constrained environments. The self-supervised switching mechanism could be adapted for other LRM architectures and tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-engineered and empirically validated solution to a practical problem. It leverages a clever self-supervised approach to adaptively control reasoning depth, leading to significant computational savings without sacrificing accuracy. While the core idea of adaptive reasoning is not entirely new, the specific implementation (lightweight switcher, self-supervision, prompt design) and thorough experimental validation make this paper a valuable contribution. The limitations regarding task scope and model size prevent a higher score, the rigorous rationale and significant reduction of computation costs for LLMs merits a high rating.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safety Subspaces are Not Distinct: A Fine-Tuning Case Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14185v1)
- **Authors**: Kaustubh Ponkshe, Shaan Shah, Raghav Singhal, Praneeth Vepakomma
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) rely on safety alignment to produce socially acceptable responses. This is typically achieved through instruction tuning and reinforcement learning from human feedback. However, this alignment is known to be brittle: further fine-tuning, even on benign or lightly contaminated data, can degrade safety and reintroduce harmful behaviors. A growing body of work suggests that alignment may correspond to identifiable geometric directions in weight space, forming subspaces that could, in principle, be isolated or preserved to defend against misalignment. In this work, we conduct a comprehensive empirical study of this geometric perspective. We examine whether safety-relevant behavior is concentrated in specific subspaces, whether it can be separated from general-purpose learning, and whether harmfulness arises from distinguishable patterns in internal representations. Across both parameter and activation space, our findings are consistent: subspaces that amplify safe behaviors also amplify unsafe ones, and prompts with different safety implications activate overlapping representations. We find no evidence of a subspace that selectively governs safety. These results challenge the assumption that alignment is geometrically localized. Rather than residing in distinct directions, safety appears to emerge from entangled, high-impact components of the model's broader learning dynamics. This suggests that subspace-based defenses may face fundamental limitations and underscores the need for alternative strategies to preserve alignment under continued training. We corroborate these findings through multiple experiments on five open-source LLMs. Our code is publicly available at: https://github.com/CERT-Lab/safety-subspaces.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether safety alignment in Large Language Models (LLMs) is encoded in distinct geometric subspaces, which could potentially be isolated or preserved to prevent misalignment during fine-tuning. Through a series of experiments on parameter updates and internal representations of five open-source LLMs, the authors find that safety-relevant behavior isn't concentrated in specific subspaces. Instead, subspaces that amplify safe behaviors also amplify unsafe ones, and harmful prompts activate overlapping representations with helpful prompts. The authors argue this challenges the assumption that alignment is geometrically localized and suggests safety emerges from entangled components of the model's broader learning dynamics.  The paper concludes that subspace-based defenses may have fundamental limitations and alternative strategies are needed to preserve alignment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper directly challenges a growing belief in the field that safety alignment is localized within identifiable geometric structures in LLM weight space. While previous works hinted at the fragility of safety alignments, this paper provides a rigorous empirical analysis disproving that the safety subspaces can be separated with linear methods. This is an important negative result.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for defense strategies against model misalignment. If safety cannot be easily isolated geometrically, subspace-based defenses (which involve projections, filtering, etc.) will likely be limited in their effectiveness. The work directs attention towards more sophisticated techniques that account for the entangled nature of safety within the model's broader learning dynamics. This could influence future research in safety alignment and robustness.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Analysis:**  The paper conducts a thorough set of experiments, exploring both parameter space (weight updates) and activation space (internal representations).
    *   **Multiple LLMs:** Testing on five different open-source LLMs increases the generalizability of the findings and mitigates the risk of model-specific artifacts.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The experimental setup is well-defined, and the metrics used (energy-kept ratio, utility, harmfulness score, MSO) are appropriate for the research question.
    *   **Well-Supported Claims:** The conclusions are supported by consistent patterns observed across multiple experiments and models.
    *   **Clear articulation of limitations:** The paper clearly identifies its limitations, focusing on linear subspaces and mid-sized LLMs which leaves room for future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Linear Subspace Focus:**  The study primarily focuses on *linear* subspaces.  It is possible that more complex, non-linear relationships could exist that separate safe and unsafe behaviors, though these are inherently more difficult to identify and work with.
    *   **Model Size:** The LLMs used are relatively small (1B to 7B parameters).  While they offer a more controllable experimental setting, it's not guaranteed that the same patterns will hold for much larger, state-of-the-art models.
    *   **Specific Tasks and Datasets:** The analysis depends on particular tasks (math problem-solving) and datasets (MetaMathQA, BeaverTails, ToxiGen). While these are reasonable choices, the results may not generalize perfectly to all possible scenarios.
*   **Impact:** This paper will likely influence the direction of research on safety alignment and defenses against misalignment. It's a significant negative result that pushes the field to look beyond simple geometric solutions and consider more complex approaches. The analysis of activation spaces is also an interesting angle, and more research could be done in this space.

**Overall:**

Despite the inherent limitations of focusing on linear subspaces and smaller models, the paper presents a strong empirical case against the existence of easily separable "safety subspaces." This finding is significant for the field and motivates a shift in research towards more sophisticated alignment strategies. The paper is well-written, the methodology is sound, and the conclusions are well-supported.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unraveling Interwoven Roles of Large Language Models in Authorship Privacy: Obfuscation, Mimicking, and Verification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14195v1)
- **Authors**: Tuc Nguyen, Yifan Hu, Thai Le
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have been fueled by large scale training corpora drawn from diverse sources such as websites, news articles, and books. These datasets often contain explicit user information, such as person names and addresses, that LLMs may unintentionally reproduce in their generated outputs. Beyond such explicit content, LLMs can also leak identity revealing cues through implicit signals such as distinctive writing styles, raising significant concerns about authorship privacy. There are three major automated tasks in authorship privacy, namely authorship obfuscation (AO), authorship mimicking (AM), and authorship verification (AV). Prior research has studied AO, AM, and AV independently. However, their interplays remain under explored, which leaves a major research gap, especially in the era of LLMs, where they are profoundly shaping how we curate and share user generated content, and the distinction between machine generated and human authored text is also increasingly blurred. This work then presents the first unified framework for analyzing the dynamic relationships among LLM enabled AO, AM, and AV in the context of authorship privacy. We quantify how they interact with each other to transform human authored text, examining effects at a single point in time and iteratively over time. We also examine the role of demographic metadata, such as gender, academic background, in modulating their performances, inter-task dynamics, and privacy risks. All source code will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a unified framework for analyzing the dynamic relationships between Large Language Model (LLM)-enabled authorship obfuscation (AO), authorship mimicking (AM), and authorship verification (AV) in the context of authorship privacy. It addresses the current research gap where these tasks are typically studied independently. The authors quantify how these tasks interact to transform human-authored text, both at a single point in time and iteratively over time. The study also examines the role of demographic metadata in modulating their performances, inter-task dynamics, and privacy risks. The experiments utilize several commercial LLMs and three diverse datasets. Key findings reveal that obfuscation tends to outperform mimicking in interactive settings, but mimicking can partially reverse obfuscation over successive cycles.  Models with stronger reasoning abilities excel at verification and concealing authorial traits.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main strength of this paper is its novel and unified framework for analyzing the interplay between AO, AM, and AV, something previous work has largely neglected. The explicit focus on how these processes interact, especially in the context of LLMs, represents a significant contribution. The iterative analysis adds another layer of novelty by exploring how these processes evolve over time, mirroring real-world scenarios.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the increasing importance of authorship privacy in the age of LLMs.  The paper highlights the privacy risks associated with LLMs unintentionally reproducing user information or leaking identity-revealing cues through writing style. The research provides insights into how users can leverage LLMs to protect their privacy and how adversaries can potentially exploit LLMs to de-anonymize authors or spread misinformation.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The paper defines clear and well-motivated research questions (RQ1, RQ2, RQ3) that guide the investigation.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The proposed framework provides a structured approach to analyzing the complex interactions between AO, AM, and AV.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes empirical evaluations with multiple LLMs and datasets, providing quantitative evidence to support the claims.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The analysis is detailed, examining the effects of demographic metadata, iterative cycles, and the strengths and weaknesses of different LLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Human Evaluation:** The paper acknowledges the absence of human-centered evaluation. The reliance on automated metrics might not fully capture the perceived naturalness and effectiveness of obfuscation and mimicking from a human perspective. This is a significant limitation.
    *   **Dataset Scope:** While the datasets are diverse, they might not fully represent all types of user-generated content or authorship scenarios. The generalizability of the findings to other domains could be further explored.
    *   **LLM Selection:** The paper focuses on commercial LLMs, which might limit the reproducibility of the experiments and analysis since these models often change. It would be beneficial to include some open-source LLMs, if available for comparison.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in authorship privacy, LLM security, and responsible AI. The framework can be used as a foundation for developing more robust privacy-preserving techniques and detection mechanisms for AI-generated impersonations. The findings can also inform the design of LLM-powered tools that protect user privacy. The iterative analysis and interdependency loop could be pivotal in modeling multi-turn dialogue scenarios involving automated content creation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is valuable for its novelty in framing the interaction between AO, AM, and AV in the current LLM landscape, and provides a useful starting point for further research on authorship privacy with complex interplay. However, limitations in human-centric evaluation and dataset scope, reduce potential influence.
