- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ABBA: Highly Expressive Hadamard Product Adaptation for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14238v1)
- **Authors**: Raghav Singhal, Kaustubh Ponkshe, Rohit Vartak, Praneeth Vepakomma
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models have demonstrated strong performance across a wide range of tasks, but adapting them efficiently to new domains remains a key challenge. Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) methods address this by introducing lightweight, trainable modules while keeping most pre-trained weights fixed. The prevailing approach, LoRA, models updates using a low-rank decomposition, but its expressivity is inherently constrained by the rank. Recent methods like HiRA aim to increase expressivity by incorporating a Hadamard product with the frozen weights, but still rely on the structure of the pre-trained model. We introduce ABBA, a new PEFT architecture that reparameterizes the update as a Hadamard product of two independently learnable low-rank matrices. In contrast to prior work, ABBA fully decouples the update from the pre-trained weights, enabling both components to be optimized freely. This leads to significantly higher expressivity under the same parameter budget. We formally analyze ABBA's expressive capacity and validate its advantages through matrix reconstruction experiments. Empirically, ABBA achieves state-of-the-art results on arithmetic and commonsense reasoning benchmarks, consistently outperforming existing PEFT methods by a significant margin across multiple models. Our code is publicly available at: https://github.com/CERT-Lab/abba.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ABBA, a new parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) architecture for large language models (LLMs). ABBA reparameterizes the weight update as a Hadamard product of two independently learnable low-rank matrices. This contrasts with methods like LoRA which uses a single low-rank decomposition and HiRA which modulates pre-trained weights with a low-rank update.  ABBA aims to improve expressivity under a similar parameter budget by decoupling the update from the pre-trained weights. The authors provide theoretical and empirical analyses to support their approach, including a matrix reconstruction task, toy MNIST experiment, and benchmarking on arithmetic and commonsense reasoning tasks, showing superior performance compared to existing PEFT methods. They also provide an efficient implementation using Khatri-Rao factorization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using a Hadamard product of *two independently learnable* low-rank matrices for weight updates is a significant departure from existing PEFT approaches, particularly LoRA and HiRA. While HiRA uses a Hadamard product, its expressivity is still constrained by its dependence on the pre-trained weights. ABBA's complete decoupling allows for more flexible optimization. The Khatri-Rao factorization for efficient implementation is also a notable contribution.

*   **Significance:** Parameter-efficient fine-tuning is a crucial area for making LLMs more accessible and adaptable. ABBA's improved expressivity and demonstrated performance gains across a variety of tasks, with results rivaling full fine-tuning in some instances, suggest it could become a prominent method in the PEFT landscape. The rigorous theoretical and empirical validation strengthens the paper's significance. The exact analytical expression derived is valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing low-rank methods like LoRA in terms of expressivity.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:**  The ABBA architecture is logically derived from the limitations of LoRA and HiRA.
    *   **Theoretical Analysis:**  The formal analysis of expressivity, and the proofs regarding scaling factors and rank-stability, provide strong theoretical grounding for the approach.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  Extensive experiments on multiple models and datasets demonstrate consistent and significant performance gains over existing PEFT methods. The inclusion of matrix reconstruction and MNIST task helps illuminate the mechanisms underlying ABBA's performance.
    *   **Efficient Implementation:** The Khatri-Rao factorization provides a practical and scalable implementation of ABBA.
    *   **Ablation Studies and Hyperparameter Analysis:** Strong experimentation to assess all the components of ABBA (such as the initialization and choice of scaling factor).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The Hadamard product and Khatri-Rao factorization might add a bit of complexity to the implementation and understanding compared to LoRA's simplicity. However, their approach is simple enough to be implementable, and there are plenty of available and accessible resources to implement Hadamard products/Khatri-Rao factorizations.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Adapter Composition:** The adapter chaining experiment yielded negative results, and further exploration of this direction may be needed.

*   **Potential Impact:**  ABBA has the potential to influence the future of PEFT research by demonstrating the benefits of decoupling weight updates and leveraging Hadamard products. The improved performance and efficient implementation could lead to wider adoption of ABBA in practical applications.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**  ABBA presents a novel and well-justified approach to parameter-efficient fine-tuning that overcomes limitations of existing methods. The theoretical analysis and extensive empirical validation provides strong support for its efficacy. However, while ABBA introduces architectural complexity, the performance and ablation studies prove that the additional complexity is well worth it. The potential for impact in the PEFT field is substantial. Future work could explore more deeply how ABBA’s performance varies across different tasks/datasets or consider adapter chaining and other techniques with ABBA.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### TransBench: Benchmarking Machine Translation for Industrial-Scale Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14244v1)
- **Authors**: Haijun Li, Tianqi Shi, Zifu Shang, Yuxuan Han, Xueyu Zhao, Hao Wang, Yu Qian, Zhiqiang Qian, Linlong Xu, Minghao Wu, Chenyang Lyu, Longyue Wang, Gongbo Tang, Weihua Luo, Zhao Xu, Kaifu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Machine translation (MT) has become indispensable for cross-border communication in globalized industries like e-commerce, finance, and legal services, with recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) significantly enhancing translation quality. However, applying general-purpose MT models to industrial scenarios reveals critical limitations due to domain-specific terminology, cultural nuances, and stylistic conventions absent in generic benchmarks. Existing evaluation frameworks inadequately assess performance in specialized contexts, creating a gap between academic benchmarks and real-world efficacy. To address this, we propose a three-level translation capability framework: (1) Basic Linguistic Competence, (2) Domain-Specific Proficiency, and (3) Cultural Adaptation, emphasizing the need for holistic evaluation across these dimensions. We introduce TransBench, a benchmark tailored for industrial MT, initially targeting international e-commerce with 17,000 professionally translated sentences spanning 4 main scenarios and 33 language pairs. TransBench integrates traditional metrics (BLEU, TER) with Marco-MOS, a domain-specific evaluation model, and provides guidelines for reproducible benchmark construction. Our contributions include: (1) a structured framework for industrial MT evaluation, (2) the first publicly available benchmark for e-commerce translation, (3) novel metrics probing multi-level translation quality, and (4) open-sourced evaluation tools. This work bridges the evaluation gap, enabling researchers and practitioners to systematically assess and enhance MT systems for industry-specific needs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TransBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate machine translation (MT) systems for industrial-scale applications, specifically focusing on international e-commerce. It identifies limitations of existing general-purpose MT models and benchmarks when applied to specialized industrial scenarios, citing the importance of domain-specific terminology, cultural nuances, and stylistic conventions. The authors propose a three-level translation capability framework: Basic Linguistic Competence, Domain-Specific Proficiency, and Cultural Adaptation. TransBench is constructed to address these levels, including 17,000 professionally translated sentences across 4 e-commerce scenarios and 33 language pairs. The benchmark integrates traditional metrics (BLEU, TER) with a domain-specific evaluation model (Marco-MOS) and provides guidelines for benchmark construction. The paper claims contributions in the form of a structured evaluation framework, a publicly available e-commerce translation benchmark, novel metrics, and open-sourced evaluation tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the creation of a benchmark specifically tailored to the industrial MT landscape, particularly e-commerce. While existing MT benchmarks exist, they often lack the domain specificity and focus on cultural adaptation that TransBench emphasizes.  The three-level framework itself, although built upon existing translation theories, provides a more structured approach to assess MT in the context of industrial needs. The creation of Marco-MOS, a domain-specific evaluation model, is a valuable contribution towards achieving more reliable evaluation in specialized contexts. The open-sourcing of the benchmark and evaluation tools is also a commendable move.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a real and growing problem: the gap between academic MT research and the practical requirements of industrial applications.  The limitations of applying general-purpose models to specialized domains are well-articulated. The proposed framework provides a clearer roadmap for evaluating and improving MT systems for industry needs, potentially leading to more effective and culturally sensitive translations. The significance is also amplified by the growing globalization and the increasing reliance on MT in cross-border commerce.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the limitations of existing MT benchmarks for industrial applications.
*   **Framework:** The three-level framework offers a structured and intuitive way to think about MT capabilities in an industrial context.
*   **Benchmark Construction:** The paper details the methodology behind building TransBench, providing some information about the data processing and annotation steps. This helps with the reproducibility of the benchmark.
*   **Domain-Specific Evaluation:** The integration of Marco-MOS showcases a commitment to developing more contextually relevant evaluation metrics.
*   **Open Source:** The availability of the benchmark and evaluation tools will encourage further research and development in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Detail on Marco-MOS:** While the paper mentions Marco-MOS, it lacks significant detail about its architecture, training data, and performance compared to other QE models. A more thorough description would strengthen the contribution.
*   **Limited Experimental Validation:** The paper introduces the benchmark and metrics but provides minimal experimental results showing how the benchmark can differentiate the performance of various MT systems. It would be valuable to show performance comparisons between different models/architectures using TransBench.
*   **Scope Limitation:** The benchmark is currently limited to e-commerce and financial domains. It would be more impactful to also cover areas such as legal or medical areas.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper.

*   The paper introduces a valuable and timely resource for the MT community, addressing a significant gap in the evaluation of industrial-scale MT applications.
*   The three-level framework and the creation of Marco-MOS contribute to a more nuanced understanding of MT capabilities beyond general-purpose translation.
*   The open-sourcing of the benchmark and tools will facilitate further research and development.
*   However, the lack of detailed information on Marco-MOS, limited experimental validation, and narrow application scope slightly detract from its overall impact.

The paper demonstrates solid novelty and significant potential to influence the direction of MT research and development in industrial settings. Further work in providing detailed experimentation, addressing limitations and extending the benchmark to other high-impact domains would greatly enhance its impact and contribution to the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Instructing Text-to-Image Diffusion Models via Classifier-Guided Semantic Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14254v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanyuan Chang, Yinghua Yao, Tao Qin, Mengmeng Wang, Ivor Tsang, Guang Dai
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image diffusion models have emerged as powerful tools for high-quality image generation and editing. Many existing approaches rely on text prompts as editing guidance. However, these methods are constrained by the need for manual prompt crafting, which can be time-consuming, introduce irrelevant details, and significantly limit editing performance. In this work, we propose optimizing semantic embeddings guided by attribute classifiers to steer text-to-image models toward desired edits, without relying on text prompts or requiring any training or fine-tuning of the diffusion model. We utilize classifiers to learn precise semantic embeddings at the dataset level. The learned embeddings are theoretically justified as the optimal representation of attribute semantics, enabling disentangled and accurate edits. Experiments further demonstrate that our method achieves high levels of disentanglement and strong generalization across different domains of data.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach called Classifier-Guided Semantic Optimization (CASO) for editing images using text-to-image diffusion models. Unlike existing methods that rely heavily on crafting text prompts, CASO optimizes semantic embeddings guided by attribute classifiers to steer the diffusion model towards desired edits. This eliminates the need for manual prompt engineering and, according to the authors, results in more disentangled and accurate edits. The method utilizes classifiers to learn precise semantic embeddings at the dataset level and demonstrates strong generalization across different data domains. The core idea is to learn a continuous semantic embedding for a specific attribute (e.g., "smile") and then use this embedding to guide the Stable Diffusion model in generating images with that attribute. The method does not require training or fine-tuning the underlying diffusion model, making it computationally efficient.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using attribute classifiers to guide semantic optimization within the latent space of diffusion models is reasonably novel. While previous works explored latent space editing and text prompt optimization, CASO directly leverages classifier gradients for a more targeted and controllable approach. The theoretical justification linking the learned embeddings to attribute class means adds another layer of novelty. The work innovates in eliminating text prompt engineering as a required step in the process, reducing manual effort.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in improving the controllability and accuracy of image editing with diffusion models. By decoupling the editing process from the complexities of text prompts, CASO could make image editing more accessible and intuitive. The reported improvements in disentanglement and generalization are also significant if they hold true across a broad range of scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The method is computationally efficient, as it doesn't require training or fine-tuning the diffusion model.
    *   It offers improved controllability and accuracy compared to prompt-based methods, according to the authors.
    *   The theoretical justification based on neural collapse provides a solid foundation for the approach.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate strong generalization across different data domains.
    *   CASO enables fine-grained attribute editing and reverse editing, providing flexibility to the user.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method relies on pre-trained attribute classifiers, which may limit its applicability to scenarios where suitable classifiers are not readily available. The reliance on a strong classifier to work well is a major limitation.
    *   The approach is dependent on the quality and capabilities of the underlying diffusion model (Stable Diffusion).
    *   The experiments, while promising, might not cover all possible image editing scenarios, and a more comprehensive evaluation would be beneficial.
    *   The dependence on a pre-trained classifier is a major limitation to this work
    *   While the paper claims to solve the disentanglement issue, the method relies heavily on semantic extraction by external classifiers.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the reported results can be consistently replicated and extended, CASO could influence the development of more user-friendly and controllable image editing tools. It could also inspire further research into leveraging external knowledge sources (like classifiers) for guiding generative models.

**Justification for Score:**

While the CASO approach presents a novel way to guide semantic edits within diffusion models, several aspects temper its overall impact. The reliance on external, well-trained classifiers significantly restricts its general applicability. The extent of improvement in disentanglement relies greatly on classifiers and the disentanglement capability of diffusion models themselves. The results, while promising, need to be validated more extensively across different data types, diffusion models, and editing tasks. The experimental section lacks detail in terms of hyperparameter choices and justification for the metrics utilized. Given the limitations, while the core concept has merit, the reliance on external resources restricts impact.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### FuxiMT: Sparsifying Large Language Models for Chinese-Centric Multilingual Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14256v1)
- **Authors**: Shaolin Zhu, Tianyu Dong, Bo Li, Deyi Xiong
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we present FuxiMT, a novel Chinese-centric multilingual machine translation model powered by a sparsified large language model (LLM). We adopt a two-stage strategy to train FuxiMT. We first pre-train the model on a massive Chinese corpus and then conduct multilingual fine-tuning on a large parallel dataset encompassing 65 languages. FuxiMT incorporates Mixture-of-Experts (MoEs) and employs a curriculum learning strategy for robust performance across various resource levels. Experimental results demonstrate that FuxiMT significantly outperforms strong baselines, including state-of-the-art LLMs and machine translation models, particularly under low-resource scenarios. Furthermore, FuxiMT exhibits remarkable zero-shot translation capabilities for unseen language pairs, indicating its potential to bridge communication gaps where parallel data are scarce or unavailable.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "FuxiMT: Sparsifying Large Language Models for Chinese-Centric Multilingual Machine Translation":

**Summary:**

The paper presents FuxiMT, a Chinese-centric multilingual machine translation model built on a sparsified large language model (LLM). FuxiMT leverages a two-stage training strategy: first, pre-training on a massive Chinese corpus (5B tokens) to instill deep Chinese understanding, followed by multilingual fine-tuning on 100B+ parallel sentences across 65 languages. It incorporates a Mixture-of-Experts (MoEs) architecture for efficient computation and scalability.  Experimental results demonstrate that FuxiMT outperforms strong baselines, including LLMs like BLOOM and even commercial systems like GPT-3.5, particularly in low-resource scenarios, and exhibits remarkable zero-shot translation capabilities. The paper emphasizes the Chinese-centric design to address the underrepresentation of Chinese in existing LLMs and claims its potential for bridging communication gaps in areas with limited parallel data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the individual components of FuxiMT – the BLOOMz backbone, MoEs, two-stage training, curriculum learning, and back-translation augmentation – are not entirely novel, the *combination* and careful application, especially with the deliberate Chinese-centric focus, do represent a genuine contribution. The paper highlights the importance of specialized pre-training for underrepresented languages like Chinese, a valuable insight. The MoE integration seems to provide an edge in low-resource translation, offering a path to handle diverse languages efficiently. However, the novelty isn't groundbreaking but rather an intelligent orchestration of existing techniques towards a specific objective.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its attempt to address the imbalance in multilingual LLMs, which are often heavily English-centric.  Creating a Chinese-first model like FuxiMT, that demonstrably improves performance on Chinese-involved translation pairs, is a step towards a more equitable distribution of language technologies. The strong performance in low-resource scenarios and the zero-shot capabilities are promising and could have practical implications for communication in regions where parallel data is scarce. The performance surpasses NLLB in some scenarios which is quite good. However, the impact is somewhat limited by the relatively small scale of the language models (7B parameters) compared to more recent and much larger LLMs. The comparison to GPT-3.5 is somewhat misleading, as the GPT-3.5 results are not directly comparable, as they are not open source and can not be easily fine-tuned.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured presentation.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with comparisons to strong baselines.
    *   Emphasis on addressing the limitations of existing LLMs regarding Chinese-centric translation.
    *   Promising results in low-resource scenarios and zero-shot translation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental novelty (combination of existing techniques).
    *   Limited model scale (7B parameters), especially considering the rapid advancements in LLM sizes.
    *   The improvements in high-resource scenarios seem marginal compared to other, more recent models (Qwen, and InternLM).
    *   The GPT-3.5 comparison seems misleading, and might inflate the perceived novelty of the work.
    *   Limited discussion of ethical considerations, particularly regarding potential biases or misuse of the translation capabilities.

*   **Potential Influence:** FuxiMT could influence future research by:
    *   Demonstrating the effectiveness of Chinese-centric pre-training for improving performance on Chinese-involved translation pairs.
    *   Highlighting the benefits of MoEs for handling diverse languages efficiently in MT models.
    *   Inspiring further development of LLMs tailored for specific language families or regional contexts.

**Justification of Score:**

While FuxiMT offers a valuable contribution by addressing the imbalance in multilingual LLMs and demonstrates promising results, its incremental novelty and somewhat limited scale restrict its potential impact. The paper does show significant benefits over the BLOOMz-7B and NLLB models, but does not strongly outperform contemporary models. Given that Chinese centric pre-training is not novel, FuxiMT is mostly incremental compared to recent advances. The claims about outperforming GPT-3.5 are misleading and are not directly comparable. Therefore, a score of 6 is appropriate.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Speculative Decoding Reimagined for Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14260v1)
- **Authors**: Luxi Lin, Zhihang Lin, Zhanpeng Zeng, Rongrong Ji
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces Multimodal Speculative Decoding (MSD) to accelerate Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) inference. Speculative decoding has been shown to accelerate Large Language Models (LLMs) without sacrificing accuracy. However, current speculative decoding methods for MLLMs fail to achieve the same speedup as they do for LLMs. To address this, we reimagine speculative decoding specifically for MLLMs. Our analysis of MLLM characteristics reveals two key design principles for MSD: (1) Text and visual tokens have fundamentally different characteristics and need to be processed separately during drafting. (2) Both language modeling ability and visual perception capability are crucial for the draft model. For the first principle, MSD decouples text and visual tokens in the draft model, allowing each to be handled based on its own characteristics. For the second principle, MSD uses a two-stage training strategy: In stage one, the draft model is trained on text-only instruction-tuning datasets to improve its language modeling ability. In stage two, MSD gradually introduces multimodal data to enhance the visual perception capability of the draft model. Experiments show that MSD boosts inference speed by up to $2.29\times$ for LLaVA-1.5-7B and up to $2.46\times$ for LLaVA-1.5-13B on multimodal benchmarks, demonstrating its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/Lyn-Lucy/MSD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Speculative Decoding Reimagined for Multimodal Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multimodal Speculative Decoding (MSD), a novel approach to accelerate the inference speed of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The core idea is to reimagine speculative decoding specifically for MLLMs, addressing the limitations of applying existing LLM-focused methods directly. The authors identify two key design principles: 1) decoupling the processing of text and visual tokens due to their differing characteristics, and 2) emphasizing both language modeling and visual perception capabilities in the draft model.  They propose a two-stage training strategy for the draft model: first, training on text-only data to improve language modeling, then gradually introducing multimodal data to enhance visual perception.  Experiments on various multimodal tasks demonstrate significant inference speedups for LLaVA models without sacrificing accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its tailored approach to speculative decoding for MLLMs. While speculative decoding itself is not new, its application to MLLMs, with specific architectural and training modifications to address the unique characteristics of multimodal data, constitutes a significant contribution.  The insight that text and visual tokens require different processing is well-reasoned and empirically supported. The two-stage training strategy is also a practical and effective innovation. Gagrani et al. [18] already took the initial step of exploring SD for MLLMs, so the current paper builds upon that initial exploration. However, the specific design choices and the performance boost reported elevate this work beyond a mere incremental improvement.

*   **Significance:** Accelerating MLLM inference is a crucial area for practical deployment. MLLMs are computationally expensive, and any method that can reduce latency without compromising accuracy is valuable.  The reported speedups are substantial and impactful. The insights into the different roles of language modeling and visual perception in draft model training provide useful guidance for future research. However, the significance is slightly diminished by the evaluation being limited to LLaVA models. While LLaVA is a popular architecture, exploring the generalizability of MSD across other MLLM architectures would strengthen the paper's impact. Also, the gap between average acceptance length of MLLMs and LLMs still exists, which requires future exploration.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly identifies the shortcomings of directly applying existing SD methods to MLLMs and provides a compelling rationale for its design choices.
    *   **Empirically Supported:** Extensive experiments on various multimodal tasks validate the effectiveness of MSD. Ablation studies provide further insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Practical:** The proposed method is relatively easy to implement and can be directly integrated into existing MLLM frameworks.
    *   **Clear and well-organized:** The writing is easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Evaluation:** The evaluation focuses primarily on LLaVA models.  Expanding the evaluation to other MLLM architectures would enhance the paper's generality.
    *   **Relatively Small Training Dataset Size:** The size of the text-only and visual instruction-tuning datasets (68,000 samples) seems limited, especially compared to typical LLM training regimes. Demonstrating scalability to larger datasets would be valuable.
    *   **Speedup Ratio gap:** Although MSD can match LLMs in average acceptance length, it still shows a lower overall speedup ratio because of longer input length of MLLMs benchmarks.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research on efficient MLLM inference. The insights into multimodal-specific speculative decoding can guide the development of more sophisticated and effective acceleration techniques. The two-stage training strategy may also be adopted in other MLLM training paradigms.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field, it's not without limitations. The focused evaluation, the training dataset size, and speedup ratio gap slightly temper its significance. The paper clearly improves upon previous speculative decoding methods applied to MLLMs and provides new insights. Therefore, the final score reflects a solid, impactful contribution that warrants further exploration.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### AAPO: Enhance the Reasoning Capabilities of LLMs with Advantage Momentum
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14264v1)
- **Authors**: Jian Xiong, Jingbo Zhou, Jingyong Ye, Dejing Dou
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as an effective approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), especially in scenarios where supervised fine-tuning (SFT) falls short due to limited chain-of-thought (CoT) data. Among RL-based post-training methods, group relative advantage estimation, as exemplified by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has attracted considerable attention for eliminating the dependency on the value model, thereby simplifying training compared to traditional approaches like Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). However, we observe that exsiting group relative advantage estimation method still suffers from training inefficiencies, particularly when the estimated advantage approaches zero. To address this limitation, we propose Advantage-Augmented Policy Optimization (AAPO), a novel RL algorithm that optimizes the cross-entropy (CE) loss using advantages enhanced through a momentum-based estimation scheme. This approach effectively mitigates the inefficiencies associated with group relative advantage estimation. Experimental results on multiple mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate the superior performance of AAPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Advantage-Augmented Policy Optimization (AAPO), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm designed to enhance the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). AAPO addresses limitations in existing group relative advantage estimation methods, particularly their inefficiency when the estimated advantage approaches zero.  AAPO enhances the cross-entropy loss using advantages estimated through a momentum-based scheme.  This approach aims to provide a more stable and informative training signal, leading to improved performance on mathematical reasoning tasks.  The paper presents experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness of AAPO on several benchmarks, outperforming existing RL methods like GRPO and GPG.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the introduction of "advantage momentum."  While group relative advantage estimation is not new (GRPO, GPG), the observation that it can become ineffective when advantages become small or have low variance is a valid and practical concern. Augmenting the advantage estimation with a momentum term derived from a comparison to the reference model is a reasonably novel attempt to address this problem. This approach injects a signal even when the within-group reward variance is low, maintaining a more consistent learning gradient.
* **Significance:** The problem of effectively training LLMs for complex reasoning tasks is a significant one. Methods that improve the sample efficiency and stability of RL-based training are valuable contributions. The results presented in the paper demonstrate a consistent improvement of AAPO over existing baselines on several mathematical reasoning benchmarks. This is a good indication that the method has practical value.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies a weakness in existing group relative advantage estimation methods.
    * **Well-Motivated Solution:** The proposed AAPO algorithm is logically motivated by the identified problem.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents experimental results across several benchmarks to support the effectiveness of AAPO.  The comparison with other methods like GRPO and GPG under the same training data is a good practice. The ablation study on clipping adds further insight.
    * **Analysis:** The paper provides a theoretical analysis of the method and its impact on gradient updates.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The experiments are mainly focused on mathematical reasoning. While this is a relevant domain, it would be valuable to see how AAPO generalizes to other reasoning tasks or even other RL tasks beyond LLMs.
    * **Computational Cost:** The paper mentions that the method is computationally intensive and requires long training times, but doesn't offer extensive insights into this and how it compares to alternatives. While improvements in sample efficiency are mentioned, a more detailed breakdown of the computational overheads of calculating the advantage momentum would be helpful.
    * **Rule-Based Reward Systems:**  The RL setup still depends on a rule-based reward system, which can be brittle and prone to reward hacking. The experiments would be stronger if the reward function were more sophisticated or learned from human preference data, as is often the case in RLHF setups.
    * **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The theoretical analysis is somewhat limited. A deeper theoretical understanding of the convergence properties and stability of AAPO would strengthen the paper.
    * **Presentation:** There are minor notational and formatting issues that slightly detract from the paper's clarity.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on RL-based training of LLMs. The concept of injecting momentum into advantage estimation could be a useful direction for further exploration. The method may become a practical technique for improving the stability and sample efficiency of RL for reasoning tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper.

* **Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-motivated algorithm (AAPO) that addresses a practical limitation in existing methods for training LLMs for reasoning tasks. The empirical results show consistent improvements over existing baselines on relevant benchmarks. The analysis provides some theoretical justification for the method's effectiveness. While the scope of experiments is somewhat limited, and the computational cost could be discussed further, the overall contribution is valuable and has the potential to influence future research in this area. The paper presents a specific solution to a clearly identified problem within a growing area of research. There is some novelty and good validation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Think-J: Learning to Think for Generative LLM-as-a-Judge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14268v1)
- **Authors**: Hui Huang, Yancheng He, Hongli Zhou, Rui Zhang, Wei Liu, Weixun Wang, Wenbo Su, Bo Zheng, Jiaheng Liu
- **Abstract**: LLM-as-a-Judge refers to the automatic modeling of preferences for responses generated by Large Language Models (LLMs), which is of significant importance for both LLM evaluation and reward modeling. Although generative LLMs have made substantial progress in various tasks, their performance as LLM-Judge still falls short of expectations. In this work, we propose Think-J, which improves generative LLM-as-a-Judge by learning how to think. We first utilized a small amount of curated data to develop the model with initial judgment thinking capabilities. Subsequently, we optimize the judgment thinking traces based on reinforcement learning (RL). We propose two methods for judgment thinking optimization, based on offline and online RL, respectively. The offline RL requires training a critic model to construct positive and negative examples for learning. The online method defines rule-based reward as feedback for optimization. Experimental results showed that our approach can significantly enhance the evaluation capability of generative LLM-Judge, surpassing both generative and classifier-based LLM-Judge without requiring extra human annotations.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Think-J: Learning to Think for Generative LLM-as-a-Judge":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Think-J," a method to improve the performance of generative Large Language Models (LLMs) when used as judges for evaluating other LLM responses.  The approach involves two main steps: 1) initializing the judge LLM with judgment thinking capabilities by using a curated dataset and annotating thinking traces with a proprietary model, and 2) optimizing the judge's thinking process using reinforcement learning (RL).  Two RL strategies are proposed: critic-guided offline learning (using a critic model to generate positive and negative examples) and rule-based online learning (defining rule-based rewards based on the correctness of judgment results). The authors demonstrate that Think-J outperforms existing LLM-judge approaches (both generative and classifier-based) on several benchmarks with limited training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of improving LLM-as-a-Judge performance by explicitly focusing on and optimizing the "thinking" or reasoning process is relatively novel. While chain-of-thought prompting and similar techniques are common, the specific focus on judgment thinking optimization is a valuable contribution.  The two RL-based optimization methods offer interesting approaches to improving the judge's reasoning capabilities without requiring extensive human annotations of thinking traces. However, the idea of using critic model to generate samples for reinforcement learning has been demonstrated in many other domains, which lowers the novelty of this work.

*   **Significance:**  Accurate and reliable LLM evaluation is a crucial bottleneck in the development and deployment of LLMs, especially in RLHF settings.  Think-J addresses this by aiming to create a more effective and interpretable judge. The demonstrated performance improvements over existing approaches are significant and could have a practical impact on LLM evaluation pipelines. In addition, the rule-based online RL is also significant, as it reduces the dependence on external critic models. The experiments conducted on different datasets validate the robustness and the effectiveness of this work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-defined problem statement.
    *   A novel approach to improving LLM-as-a-Judge performance.
    *   Effective use of reinforcement learning to optimize judgment thinking.
    *   Strong experimental results demonstrating the superiority of Think-J.
    *   The method does not require additional human annotations beyond initial training.
    *   Detailed experimental setup and analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The use of a proprietary thinking model for the initial annotation of thinking traces introduces a dependency and potential bias. The impact of this choice is not fully explored.
    *   The curated dataset (LIMJ707) is relatively small (707 samples) and might limit the generalizability of the learned judgment thinking capabilities. While this dataset is well-curated, it is a significant limiting factor in the scope and impact of this work.
    *   The exploration of different reward function designs in online learning could be more detailed.
    *   While experiments compare with various existing methods, a more thorough ablation study on the different components of Think-J (e.g., the specific RL methods, trace clipping, etc.) would further strengthen the analysis.
    *   Ethical considerations: Given that LLMs are known to have biased and unfair results, the authors should have explored how the proposed LLM-as-a-Judge performs on different demographic groups.

*   **Potential Influence:**  Think-J has the potential to influence the development of more accurate and reliable LLM evaluation methods. The focus on judgment thinking optimization could inspire further research in this area. The results suggest that optimizing the *process* of judgment is as important as, or even more important than, simply training on more data.
*   **Rigour:** The paper has demonstrated sufficient experimental settings and rigorous evaluation methodology to validate the effectiveness of the proposed model. However, the lack of extensive ablation studies and potential bias in initial annotations limit the reliability of the results.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. The Think-J approach is novel, effective, and well-motivated. While there are some weaknesses, the strengths outweigh them.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### YESciEval: Robust LLM-as-a-Judge for Scientific Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14279v1)
- **Authors**: Jennifer D'Souza, Hamed Babaei Giglou, Quentin Münch
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) drive scientific question-answering on modern search engines, yet their evaluation robustness remains underexplored. We introduce YESciEval, an open-source framework that combines fine-grained rubric-based assessment with reinforcement learning to mitigate optimism bias in LLM evaluators. We release multidisciplinary scienceQ&A datasets, including adversarial variants, with evaluation scores from multiple LLMs. Independent of proprietary models and human feedback, our approach enables scalable, cost-free evaluation. By advancing reliable LLM-as-a-judge models, this work supports AI alignment and fosters robust, transparent evaluation essential for scientific inquiry and artificial general intelligence.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "YESciEval: Robust LLM-as-a-Judge for Scientific Question Answering":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces YESciEval, an open-source framework for evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of scientific question answering (scienceQ&A).  It addresses the limitations of traditional evaluation metrics (n-grams) and the potential biases and cost associated with human or proprietary LLM evaluations.  YESciEval combines fine-grained rubric-based assessment with reinforcement learning (RL) to mitigate optimism bias in LLM evaluators. The framework includes a nine-rubric multifaceted assessment scheme covering linguistic quality, logical integrity, and content accuracy. The authors also release a multidisciplinary scienceQ&A dataset, including adversarial variants designed to test the robustness of LLM evaluators. They experiment with open-source LLMs (LLAMA3.1 and Mistral-Large) for both generating and evaluating scienceQ&A content, demonstrating a cost-effective and transparent evaluation paradigm. The method is independent of proprietary models and human feedback, while still working to align LLMs to real-world critical feedback expectations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper has a moderate degree of novelty. While the idea of using LLMs as judges isn't new, the systematic application of fine-grained rubrics *specifically designed* for scienceQ&A, coupled with a reinforcement learning strategy targeting optimism bias, represents a valuable contribution. The adversarial dataset is a significant addition, providing a more rigorous way to assess LLM evaluator performance compared to relying solely on benign datasets. The explicit focus on open-source models also increases the accessibility and reproducibility of the research, differentiating it from studies that rely solely on proprietary models. The framing of the work around AGI safety and transparent scientific inquiry helps to elevate the long-term importance of the work. The design of the adversarial attacks is relatively simple (heuristic based), but appropriate as a first pass approach.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of this work lies in several areas:
    *   **Addressing a Real Problem:** The lack of robust and transparent evaluation methods hinders progress in scienceQ&A. YESciEval provides a more reliable and scalable alternative to human evaluation, facilitating iterative development and user confidence.
    *   **Promoting Transparency:** By using open-source models and releasing the dataset and code, the authors contribute to more transparent and reproducible research. This is crucial for scientific integrity and AI alignment.
    *   **Reducing Reliance on Proprietary Models:** The framework reduces the financial and ethical issues related to relying solely on closed-source, proprietary LLMs for evaluation.
    *   **Advancing AI Alignment:** The work directly addresses the issue of AI alignment by designing the system in such a way that it can work well for alignment of general intelligence systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined and structured evaluation framework.
    *   Release of a valuable dataset with adversarial examples.
    *   Focus on open-source LLMs.
    *   Systematic approach to mitigating optimism bias.
    *   Comprehensive experiments, including qualitative analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The adversarial attack heuristics are somewhat basic (e.g., sentence swapping, inserting unrelated news) and may not capture all types of evaluation errors. More sophisticated, learning-based adversarial techniques could be explored in the future.
    *   While the framework is cost-free, it still requires computational resources for running open-source LLMs. This could be a barrier for researchers with limited resources.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the limitations of the chosen LLMs and how these limitations may affect the evaluation results.
    *   The generalization of the approach to other NLG tasks should be demonstrated experimentally, beyond just assertions.

*   **Potential Influence:** YESciEval has the potential to become a standard evaluation framework in the scienceQ&A community. The dataset can be used to benchmark new LLMs and evaluation methods, and the code can be adapted to other domains. The emphasis on transparent and reproducible research is likely to inspire more work in this area. If future work can develop more intelligent adversarial attacks, and a deeper integration with alignment techniques is made, this work could become a cornerstone of research in the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** While not revolutionary, the paper makes a solid and well-executed contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. The focus on scienceQ&A, the use of open-source models, the adversarial dataset, and the mitigation of optimism bias are all valuable contributions. The paper's weaknesses are primarily limitations of scope and avenues for future work, rather than fundamental flaws. The potential influence of YESciEval is significant, especially in promoting transparent, reproducible, and ethically sound research in the area of scientific question answering and AI alignment.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Universal Acoustic Adversarial Attacks for Flexible Control of Speech-LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14286v1)
- **Authors**: Rao Ma, Mengjie Qian, Vyas Raina, Mark Gales, Kate Knill
- **Abstract**: The combination of pre-trained speech encoders with large language models has enabled the development of speech LLMs that can handle a wide range of spoken language processing tasks. While these models are powerful and flexible, this very flexibility may make them more vulnerable to adversarial attacks. To examine the extent of this problem, in this work we investigate universal acoustic adversarial attacks on speech LLMs. Here a fixed, universal, adversarial audio segment is prepended to the original input audio. We initially investigate attacks that cause the model to either produce no output or to perform a modified task overriding the original prompt. We then extend the nature of the attack to be selective so that it activates only when specific input attributes, such as a speaker gender or spoken language, are present. Inputs without the targeted attribute should be unaffected, allowing fine-grained control over the model outputs. Our findings reveal critical vulnerabilities in Qwen2-Audio and Granite-Speech and suggest that similar speech LLMs may be susceptible to universal adversarial attacks. This highlights the need for more robust training strategies and improved resistance to adversarial attacks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates universal acoustic adversarial attacks on speech-language models (LLMs). It explores the susceptibility of models like Qwen2-Audio and Granite-Speech to fixed, adversarial audio segments prepended to the input, focusing on two attack forms: muting outputs and controlling tasks.  A novel selective attack is also introduced, which conditionally alters outputs based on input attributes (speaker gender/language).  The results show significant vulnerabilities in the tested models, highlighting the need for robust training and adversarial defense strategies. It demonstrates the feasibility of both general and attribute-specific manipulations of these powerful models through crafted audio perturbations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the introduction and exploration of _selective_ universal acoustic adversarial attacks on speech LLMs. While universal attacks on other speech recognition systems (like Whisper) have been explored, the extension to more complex instruction-following speech LLMs and the introduction of attribute-conditional attacks are novel. The concept of manipulating responses based on speaker characteristics (gender, language) is significant and hadn't been addressed before.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical vulnerability of rapidly developing speech LLMs. As these models become integrated into real-world applications (e.g., voice assistants, automated transcription), their robustness to adversarial attacks becomes paramount. The demonstration that relatively simple audio manipulations can either disable the model or steer it towards unintended behavior poses a serious security and ethical risk. Selective attacks particularly highlight the danger of malicious actors biasing or suppressing model outputs based on user attributes. The experiments provide compelling evidence of these vulnerabilities in prominent models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the potential risk and vulnerability of speech LLMs to adversarial attacks.
    *   **Novel Attack Methodology:** The concept of a selective attack is a valuable contribution and highlights a more subtle and potentially damaging type of adversarial manipulation.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims, including a rigorous evaluation of the effectiveness of the attacks and analysis of transferability across datasets and prompts.
    *   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow, with a clear description of the methodology and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Scope:** While Qwen2-Audio and Granite-Speech are representative models, the evaluation could benefit from testing a more diverse range of speech LLM architectures and sizes.
    *   **White-Box Assumption:** The attack methodology relies on white-box access (gradient information from the model).  Black-box transferability could be improved.
    *   **Defense Considerations:** While the paper highlights the need for robust training, it doesn't explore or propose any specific defense mechanisms. Including an initial exploration of potential mitigation strategies could strengthen the paper. The authors acknowledge this and encourage focus on robust training methods and adversarial resilience.
    *   **Imperceptibility:** While the inclusion of an amplitude constraint is a step toward addressing imperceptibility, psychoacoustic metrics could provide a more rigorous assessment of how detectable the attacks are to human listeners. The imperceptibility section shows an increasing trade-off in terms of model muting between the addition of the Lo norm constraint and the strength of attack success.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research in adversarial attacks on speech LLMs. It serves as a starting point for developing more robust training strategies and defense mechanisms specifically tailored to these models. The introduction of selective attacks also opens up new research directions focused on ensuring fairness and preventing bias in AI systems.
* **Future work** The authors could explore model specific training and mitigation to further combat the discussed concerns. This should also include the implementation of defenses against the presented adversarial attacks. Additionally, black-box settings should be considered in future research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by highlighting a significant vulnerability in speech LLMs. The introduction of selective attacks is a novel and important contribution. The results are well-supported by empirical evidence, and the paper is well-written. While the paper has some limitations (model scope, white-box assumption, defense considerations, and a better definition of imperceptibility), it provides a solid foundation for future research in this area. A score of 7.5 reflects the paper's novelty, significance, and overall quality, balanced against its acknowledged limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Generating Realistic Underwater Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14296v1)
- **Authors**: Abdul-Kazeem Shamba
- **Abstract**: This paper explores the use of contrastive learning and generative adversarial networks for generating realistic underwater images from synthetic images with uniform lighting. We investigate the performance of image translation models for generating realistic underwater images using the VAROS dataset. Two key evaluation metrics, Fr\'echet Inception Distance (FID) and Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM), provide insights into the trade-offs between perceptual quality and structural preservation. For paired image translation, pix2pix achieves the best FID scores due to its paired supervision and PatchGAN discriminator, while the autoencoder model attains the highest SSIM, suggesting better structural fidelity despite producing blurrier outputs. Among unpaired methods, CycleGAN achieves a competitive FID score by leveraging cycle-consistency loss, whereas CUT, which replaces cycle-consistency with contrastive learning, attains higher SSIM, indicating improved spatial similarity retention. Notably, incorporating depth information into CUT results in the lowest overall FID score, demonstrating that depth cues enhance realism. However, the slight decrease in SSIM suggests that depth-aware learning may introduce structural variations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Generating Realistic Underwater Images":

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of contrastive learning and Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) for generating realistic underwater images from synthetic images with uniform lighting. It evaluates image translation models using the VAROS dataset, focusing on Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) and Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM) metrics.  The study compares paired image translation (pix2pix) with unpaired methods like CycleGAN and CUT, investigating the impact of incorporating depth information into the CUT architecture. The key findings suggest that pix2pix achieves the best FID scores in paired translation, autoencoders produce higher SSIM, CycleGAN gives competitive FID scores, CUT improves spatial similarity, and incorporating depth into CUT further lowers the FID.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the application of contrastive learning (CUT) and the incorporation of depth information within a GAN-based framework for underwater image generation. While GANs and image translation are not new, the specific combination of these elements, targeted towards the underwater domain, presents a reasonable, though not groundbreaking, contribution. The novelty of incorporating depth information is somewhat incremental since other works have used depth, but the use within the CUT framework is unique.

*   **Significance:** The generation of realistic underwater imagery has practical applications in robotics, marine biology, and archaeology, as the paper correctly points out. The paper's potential significance rests on improving the realism of synthetic underwater images, which could reduce the need for expensive real-world data and improve the performance of algorithms trained on synthetic data. However, the gains demonstrated in the paper, while measurable, are relatively modest. The drop in FID with depth integration in CUT, for example, is impactful, but not transformative.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a thorough comparative analysis of different GAN architectures for underwater image generation.
    *   The use of established metrics (FID and SSIM) allows for objective evaluation of the generated images.
    *   The experimental setup is clearly described, facilitating reproducibility.
    *   The incorporation of depth information is a valuable addition, reflecting the physical properties of underwater environments.
    *   The discussion of failure cases is a strength, providing valuable insights into the limitations of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The improvements achieved by the proposed method (CUT + Depth) over existing methods (e.g., CycleGAN) are not dramatically significant. The FID score improvement is modest and the SSIM decreases slightly.
    *   The paper lacks a robust subjective human evaluation. Although the authors state that the FID metric is preferred over SSIM, a perceptual study would strengthen the claims of realism.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the learned feature representations.
    *   The transferability of the generated images to real-world tasks is not evaluated.
    *   The paper focuses primarily on quantitative metrics and could have benefited from more qualitative discussion, particularly regarding the visual differences introduced by the use of depth information.
    *   The dataset is relatively small, which can limit the generalization capability of the model.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can serve as a useful benchmark for future research on underwater image generation using GANs and contrastive learning. However, the incremental nature of the improvement limits its widespread influence. The exploration of depth integration is a useful contribution.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid and well-executed application of GANs and contrastive learning to the problem of underwater image generation. The incorporation of depth information is a valuable and logical step. However, the gains in realism are incremental rather than revolutionary. The lack of robust human evaluation and the limited evaluation of real-world task performance further constrain its impact. Therefore, I'm assigning a score that recognizes its solid contributions and useful experimentation while acknowledging its limitations in terms of novelty and practical impact.

**Score: 6**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Cross-Lingual Optimization for Language Transfer in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14297v1)
- **Authors**: Jungseob Lee, Seongtae Hong, Hyeonseok Moon, Heuiseok Lim
- **Abstract**: Adapting large language models to other languages typically employs supervised fine-tuning (SFT) as a standard approach. However, it often suffers from an overemphasis on English performance, a phenomenon that is especially pronounced in data-constrained environments. To overcome these challenges, we propose \textbf{Cross-Lingual Optimization (CLO)} that efficiently transfers an English-centric LLM to a target language while preserving its English capabilities. CLO utilizes publicly available English SFT data and a translation model to enable cross-lingual transfer. We conduct experiments using five models on six languages, each possessing varying levels of resource. Our results show that CLO consistently outperforms SFT in both acquiring target language proficiency and maintaining English performance. Remarkably, in low-resource languages, CLO with only 3,200 samples surpasses SFT with 6,400 samples, demonstrating that CLO can achieve better performance with less data. Furthermore, we find that SFT is particularly sensitive to data quantity in medium and low-resource languages, whereas CLO remains robust. Our comprehensive analysis emphasizes the limitations of SFT and incorporates additional training strategies in CLO to enhance efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Cross-Lingual Optimization (CLO), a method for transferring the knowledge of English-centric Large Language Models (LLMs) to other languages while preserving English performance. CLO uses publicly available English Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) data and a translation model to create parallel data in the target language. It then modifies the Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) loss to encourage the model to respond in the same language as the input query.  Experiments across six languages (Chinese, German, Korean, Indonesian, Swahili, and Yoruba) with varying resource availability demonstrate that CLO outperforms standard SFT in both acquiring target language proficiency and maintaining English performance, especially in low-resource settings. The paper explores the impact of data size, different layers of fine-tuning, and the effect of different loss functions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using a translated dataset and a modified DPO loss to explicitly encourage responses in the target language is a valuable contribution. While prior work has explored cross-lingual transfer and instruction tuning, CLO presents a focused, efficient, and seemingly effective method to address the common issue of diminished English capabilities and poor performance in low-resource languages after SFT. The explicit language correspondence within a batch through the modified DPO objective is a novel and promising aspect.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a practical problem: adapting LLMs to more languages without sacrificing performance in the language they were primarily trained on. The findings demonstrate that CLO can achieve better results with less data compared to standard SFT, which is particularly important for low-resource languages where data scarcity is a major bottleneck. The ablation studies provide further insights into the effectiveness of various components of the CLO method. The consistent performance gains observed across several models and languages strengthen the impact of the results.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges in cross-lingual transfer and motivates the need for a method like CLO.
    *   **Well-Designed Method:** CLO is well-explained and relatively simple to implement, making it accessible to other researchers.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough, covering multiple models, languages, and evaluation metrics.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies help to understand the importance of different aspects of CLO.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Translation Models:** The method relies on the quality of the translation model. While the paper acknowledges this, a more in-depth analysis of the impact of translation quality on CLO's performance would be beneficial. There may be scenarios where the translated data is significantly noisy/biased, affecting performance.
    *   **Scope of Languages:** Although six languages is a reasonable starting point, the paper acknowledges this to be a limitation. Further study across a broader and more diverse set of languages would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Limited exploration beyond DPO**: While applying CLO to DPO is valid, the authors mention that it potentially extends to different preference optimization algorithms. Verifying this, with at least one more popular algorithm, would considerably boost the confidence in CLO's applicability.
    *   **Limited evaluation on cultural relevance**: The paper acknowledges in a brief discussion on one of its limitations the lack of cultural awareness in the test data. Expanding on how CLO impacts language specific nuances and cultural relevance would strengthen the overall arguments.

*   **Potential Influence:** The CLO method can potentially influence how LLMs are adapted to new languages, particularly in resource-constrained scenarios. The findings encourage further research into methods that leverage existing language knowledge for efficient transfer. It could also lead to the development of more robust and versatile LLMs that can perform well across a wider range of languages.

**Justification of Score:**

CLO is a well-designed and empirically validated method for cross-lingual transfer. It directly addresses the problem of adapting LLMs to other languages efficiently and effectively. While it suffers from potential reliance on translation quality, the experimental results across several languages and models show consistent gains. It opens promising new avenues for future research in cross-lingual transfer. Given the practical significance of the problem and the solid contributions of the method, the findings of this paper deserve recognition.
