- **Abstract**: While humans naturally learn and adapt from past experiences, large language models (LLMs) and their agentic counterparts struggle to retain reasoning from previous tasks and apply them in future contexts. To address this limitation, we propose a novel framework, log-augmented generation (LAG) that directly reuses prior computation and reasoning from past logs at test time to enhance model's ability to learn from previous tasks and perform better on new, unseen challenges, all while keeping the system efficient and scalable. Specifically, our system represents task logs using key-value (KV) caches, encoding the full reasoning context of prior tasks while storing KV caches for only a selected subset of tokens. When a new task arises, LAG retrieves the KV values from relevant logs to augment generation. Our approach differs from reflection-based memory mechanisms by directly reusing prior reasoning and computations without requiring additional steps for knowledge extraction or distillation. Our method also goes beyond existing KV caching techniques, which primarily target efficiency gains rather than improving accuracy. Experiments on knowledge- and reasoning-intensive datasets demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms standard agentic systems that do not utilize logs, as well as existing solutions based on reflection and KV cache techniques.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Log-Augmented Generation (LAG), a framework designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by enabling them to reuse past computations and reasoning steps. LAG achieves this by storing task logs in a key-value (KV) cache format, representing the full reasoning context while storing only a subset of tokens.  When a new task arises, LAG retrieves relevant KV values from the logs and augments the LLM's generation process. The approach differs from reflection-based methods by directly reusing prior reasoning and from existing KV caching techniques which mainly target efficiency. Experiments on knowledge- and reasoning-intensive datasets demonstrate that LAG outperforms standard agentic systems, reflection methods, and KV caching baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of reusing reasoning steps is not entirely new, as prior work in reflection and case-based reasoning has explored similar concepts. However, LAG's use of KV caches to represent and reuse reasoning traces *is* a novel and practical implementation.  The insight that KV values encapsulate the entire surrounding context is well leveraged. The paper also clearly distinguishes LAG from existing KV caching techniques, which primarily focus on efficiency gains, whereas LAG prioritizes accuracy improvements via contextual information retention. The positional embedding manipulation for log integration is also a significant technical detail.

*   **Significance:**  The paper provides empirical evidence demonstrating that LAG can significantly improve both the accuracy and efficiency of LLMs on complex reasoning tasks. The gains over strong baselines, including ReAct and reflection methods, suggest that LAG has the potential to be a valuable tool for enhancing the problem-solving capabilities of LLMs. The fact that the improvement is seen across different datasets (knowledge and reasoning intensive) strengthens the argument. Also, making it easier to use and integrate with existing LLM workflows is a practical benefit. It contributes meaningfully to the field by showing a practical way to improve the ability of LLMs to remember and reuse reasoning from prior tasks, which is a major limitation in LLMs today.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of LLMs' inability to reuse past reasoning.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The KV cache implementation for representing and reusing reasoning traces is well-motivated and technically sound.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on diverse datasets, demonstrating significant improvements over strong baselines in both accuracy and efficiency.
    *   **Ablation Studies and Analysis:**  The paper includes ablation studies to analyze the impact of different design choices, such as the number of logs retrieved and the type of tokens used for KV caching.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The code and data availability enhance the reproducibility and adoption of the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Log Selection:** The log retrieval process relies on semantic similarity.  While this is reasonable, it could be vulnerable to retrieving noisy or irrelevant logs. A more sophisticated retrieval mechanism may further improve performance. There is also limited description on how to optimize the static log store (what tasks should be run in advance to generate logs).
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** While the paper addresses storage efficiency, the retrieval of KV caches and the re-application of positional embeddings might introduce computational overhead. A more detailed analysis of the runtime performance would be beneficial.
    *   **Generalizability:**  The experiments are limited to specific datasets. Further validation on a wider range of tasks and domains would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Static Log Store:** The use of a fixed offline constructed log store restricts the potential of adapting to ongoing changes during online utilization.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's findings could influence future research on memory mechanisms for LLMs, particularly in the development of more effective and efficient methods for storing and retrieving past reasoning experiences.  The KV cache approach could be integrated into other LLM frameworks to enhance their problem-solving abilities. It also provides insights into the importance of retaining contextual information for effective reasoning.

**Justification for Score:**

While the idea of reusing reasoning steps isn't groundbreaking, LAG presents a novel *implementation* using KV caches that achieves impressive results. The clear demonstration of improved accuracy and efficiency across multiple datasets justifies a high score. The weaknesses (scalability, static logs, and dataset limitations) temper the enthusiasm slightly, but the overall contribution is significant and promising.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### ViC-Bench: Benchmarking Visual-Interleaved Chain-of-Thought Capability in MLLMs with Free-Style Intermediate State Representations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14404v1)
- **Authors**: Xuecheng Wu, Jiaxing Liu, Danlei Huang, Xiaoyu Li, Yifan Wang, Chen Chen, Liya Ma, Xuezhi Cao, Junxiao Xue
- **Abstract**: Visual-Interleaved Chain-of-Thought (VI-CoT) enables MLLMs to continually update their understanding and decisions based on step-wise intermediate visual states (IVS), much like a human would, which demonstrates impressive success in various tasks, thereby leading to emerged advancements in related benchmarks. Despite promising progress, current benchmarks provide models with relatively fixed IVS, rather than free-style IVS, whch might forcibly distort the original thinking trajectories, failing to evaluate their intrinsic reasoning capabilities. More importantly, existing benchmarks neglect to systematically explore the impact factors that IVS would impart to untamed reasoning performance. To tackle above gaps, we introduce a specialized benchmark termed ViC-Bench, consisting of four representive tasks: maze navigation, jigsaw puzzle, embodied long-horizon planning, and complex counting, where each task has dedicated free-style IVS generation pipeline supporting function calls. To systematically examine VI-CoT capability, we propose a thorough evaluation suite incorporating a progressive three-stage strategy with targeted new metrics. Besides, we establish Incremental Prompting Information Injection (IPII) strategy to ablatively explore the prompting factors for VI-CoT. We extensively conduct evaluations for 18 advanced MLLMs, revealing key insights into their VI-CoT capability. Our proposed benchmark is publicly open at Huggingface.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and a rigorous evaluation of the paper "ViC-Bench: Benchmarking Visual-Interleaved Chain-of-Thought Capability in MLLMs with Free-Style Intermediate State Representations."

**Summary:**

This paper introduces ViC-Bench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the Visual-Interleaved Chain-of-Thought (VI-CoT) reasoning capabilities of Multi-Modal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  ViC-Bench consists of four diverse tasks: maze navigation, jigsaw puzzle, embodied long-horizon planning, and complex counting. A key feature of the benchmark is the support for free-style Intermediate Visual States (IVS), allowing for more natural and flexible reasoning processes. The paper also proposes a comprehensive three-stage evaluation strategy with targeted metrics and an Incremental Prompting Information Injection (IPII) strategy for ablative analysis of prompting factors.  The authors extensively evaluate 18 state-of-the-art MLLMs on ViC-Bench and present key insights into their VI-CoT capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The primary novelty lies in the *creation of ViC-Bench itself*.  The benchmark distinguishes itself from existing benchmarks by focusing on free-style IVS representations, which are argued to be more representative of human-like reasoning. This is a valid point, as previous benchmarks often provide fixed or constrained IVS, potentially skewing the evaluation.

    *   The *three-stage evaluation strategy* is another novel aspect, designed to progressively assess VI-CoT capabilities, starting with simple tasks and gradually increasing complexity. The introduction of metrics like Recall and ThinkGain, specifically tailored for VI-CoT, represents a further contribution to the evaluation methodology.

    *   The *IPII strategy* for ablative prompting factor analysis is also valuable, allowing the authors to systematically investigate the influence of different prompting levels on VI-CoT performance.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The development of ViC-Bench is *highly significant* for the field of MLLMs.  The availability of a standardized benchmark specifically designed to assess VI-CoT capabilities fills a gap in the existing evaluation landscape. This should facilitate more focused research and development in this area.

    *   The *extensive evaluation of 18 SOTA MLLMs* provides valuable insights into the current state-of-the-art. The comparative analysis helps to identify strengths and weaknesses of different models and provides a roadmap for future improvements.  The authors point out performance gaps between different MLLMs.

    *   The *insights gained from the experiments and analysis* contribute to a better understanding of the factors that influence VI-CoT performance.  The finding that free-style IVS can enhance performance for MLLMs with strong reasoning priors, but might confuse weaker models, is particularly relevant.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and provides sufficient details about the benchmark construction, evaluation methodology, and experimental setup.

    *   The authors address a critical gap in the MLLM evaluation landscape by focusing on free-style IVS, which is more aligned with human cognition.

    *   The comprehensive evaluation of a large number of MLLMs and the ablative analysis of prompting factors provide valuable insights for the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The IVS generation is not completely automated and relies on function calls and potentially human assistance for some tasks (e.g., embodied long-horizon planning). While this allows for free-style IVS, it raises concerns about scalability and potential bias.

    *   While the authors introduce new metrics like ThinkGain and Legality, further explanation and justification of their mathematical formulations and their implications could be beneficial.

    *   The results of the IPII strategy are not clearly displayed and are not implemented across all benchmarks.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   ViC-Bench has the potential to become a widely adopted benchmark for evaluating VI-CoT capabilities in MLLMs, driving progress in the field.

    *   The proposed evaluation methodology and the identified insights can guide the development of more effective VI-CoT techniques and improved training strategies for MLLMs.

    *   The benchmark can also serve as a foundation for developing more sophisticated multi-modal agents and embodied AI systems.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant contribution by introducing a novel and well-designed benchmark (ViC-Bench) for evaluating VI-CoT capabilities in MLLMs with free-style IVS. The extensive evaluation of SOTA models and the ablative prompting study provide valuable insights. Although there are some limitations regarding the automation of IVS generation and clarity of proposed metrics, the strengths of the paper, particularly its novelty, significance, and potential impact on the field, outweigh the weaknesses. This is a well done, highly valuable paper deserving a score of 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Pierce the Mists, Greet the Sky: Decipher Knowledge Overshadowing via Knowledge Circuit Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14406v1)
- **Authors**: Haoming Huang, Yibo Yan, Jiahao Huo, Xin Zou, Xinfeng Li, Kun Wang, Xuming Hu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs), despite their remarkable capabilities, are hampered by hallucinations. A particularly challenging variant, knowledge overshadowing, occurs when one piece of activated knowledge inadvertently masks another relevant piece, leading to erroneous outputs even with high-quality training data. Current understanding of overshadowing is largely confined to inference-time observations, lacking deep insights into its origins and internal mechanisms during model training. Therefore, we introduce PhantomCircuit, a novel framework designed to comprehensively analyze and detect knowledge overshadowing. By innovatively employing knowledge circuit analysis, PhantomCircuit dissects the internal workings of attention heads, tracing how competing knowledge pathways contribute to the overshadowing phenomenon and its evolution throughout the training process. Extensive experiments demonstrate PhantomCircuit's effectiveness in identifying such instances, offering novel insights into this elusive hallucination and providing the research community with a new methodological lens for its potential mitigation.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PHANTOMCIRCUIT, a novel framework for analyzing and detecting knowledge overshadowing, a specific type of hallucination in Large Language Models (LLMs). Knowledge overshadowing occurs when a dominant piece of knowledge masks a relevant but less frequent piece, leading to incorrect outputs. PHANTOMCIRCUIT utilizes knowledge circuit analysis to dissect the internal workings of attention heads, tracing how competing knowledge pathways contribute to overshadowing. The framework analyzes the evolution of overshadowing throughout training, correlating it with factors like knowledge popularity, model size, and dataset size. Finally, the paper proposes a circuit-based method to mitigate overshadowing by optimizing the circuit structure. Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of PHANTOMCIRCUIT in identifying and addressing knowledge overshadowing, offering new insights and a methodological lens for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper introduces a genuinely novel framework, PHANTOMCIRCUIT, for analyzing knowledge overshadowing. While the concept of knowledge overshadowing isn't entirely new, the paper takes a significant step forward by proposing a way to dissect the phenomenon's internal mechanisms using knowledge circuit analysis.  The use of circuit analysis, adapted from other interpretability work, specifically in the context of understanding *why* overshadowing happens *during training* rather than just observing it at inference, is a key innovation.  The automated process for recovering from overshadowing via circuit optimization, even if simplified, is another valuable contribution.

**Significance:**  Hallucination, especially knowledge-related errors, is a critical barrier to the reliable deployment of LLMs. Knowledge overshadowing is a particularly insidious form because it occurs even with high-quality training data.  By offering a framework to understand and potentially mitigate this issue, PHANTOMCIRCUIT has the potential to improve the factuality and trustworthiness of LLMs.  The dynamic analysis correlating overshadowing with training parameters is also important, as it gives insights for developing better training strategies. The circuit-based recovery method, although preliminary, points towards practical solutions.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Methodology:** The core strength lies in the application of knowledge circuit analysis to understand knowledge overshadowing's internal mechanisms.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The framework addresses both the training dynamics and the internal workings of the models, providing a holistic view.
*   **Potential for Mitigation:** The circuit-based recovery method, even in its initial form, offers a path towards practical solutions.
*   **Extensive Experiments:** The paper includes a thorough experimental evaluation on various model sizes and datasets.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology and experimental results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Dataset Limitations:**  The heavy reliance on synthetic datasets is a concern. While allowing for controlled experiments, it raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to real-world, complex datasets. The finetuning experiment addresses it partially, but more real-world validation is necessary.
*   **Simplifications in Recovery:** The circuit optimization method, while promising, seems somewhat simplified. The automated detection and edge pruning process might benefit from more sophisticated approaches. Also, the simplified R-PMI could potentially miss relevant features or connections compared to a more complex extraction strategy.
*   **Computational Intensity:** The circuit analysis, as the authors acknowledge, is computationally demanding, which could limit its scalability to extremely large models.
*   **Scope:** While the paper addresses a critical problem, it is still focused on a specific type of hallucination.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by offering a new approach to understanding and addressing a significant problem in LLMs. The novelty of applying knowledge circuit analysis to knowledge overshadowing, combined with the comprehensive analysis and potential for mitigation, justifies a relatively high score. However, the limitations related to synthetic datasets, simplifications in the recovery method, and computational intensity prevent it from reaching the highest levels. The generalizability to real-world scenarios, and the scalability of the approach to larger, more complex models, needs further investigation.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Non-Euclidean Foundation Models: Advancing AI Beyond Euclidean Frameworks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14417v1)
- **Authors**: Menglin Yang, Yifei Zhang, Jialin Chen, Melanie Weber, Rex Ying
- **Abstract**: In the era of foundation models and Large Language Models (LLMs), Euclidean space is the de facto geometric setting of our machine learning architectures. However, recent literature has demonstrated that this choice comes with fundamental limitations. To that end, non-Euclidean learning is quickly gaining traction, particularly in web-related applications where complex relationships and structures are prevalent. Non-Euclidean spaces, such as hyperbolic, spherical, and mixed-curvature spaces, have been shown to provide more efficient and effective representations for data with intrinsic geometric properties, including web-related data like social network topology, query-document relationships, and user-item interactions. Integrating foundation models with non-Euclidean geometries has great potential to enhance their ability to capture and model the underlying structures, leading to better performance in search, recommendations, and content understanding. This workshop focuses on the intersection of Non-Euclidean Foundation Models and Geometric Learning (NEGEL), exploring its potential benefits, including the potential benefits for advancing web-related technologies, challenges, and future directions. Workshop page: [https://hyperboliclearning.github.io/events/www2025workshop](https://hyperboliclearning.github.io/events/www2025workshop)
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided text. Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation of the paper's novelty and significance:

**Summary**

The paper proposes a workshop focusing on "Non-Euclidean Foundation Models," aiming to explore the integration of non-Euclidean geometries (hyperbolic, spherical, etc.) with foundation models in machine learning. The motivation stems from the limitations of Euclidean space in capturing complex relationships within web-related data (social networks, query-document relationships, user-item interactions). The workshop seeks to bring together researchers from various backgrounds to discuss advancements, challenges, and future directions in this emerging area. It will include invited talks, poster sessions, panel discussions, and contributed talks. The organizers highlight the potential benefits for web applications like search, recommendations, and content understanding, emphasizing the importance of trustworthiness and robustness in these models.  The workshop aims to bridge the gap between existing work on geometric deep learning and the recent surge in research and applications of large foundation models, specifically in the context of web technologies.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies not in presenting a novel *method* or *architecture*, but in *proposing a dedicated forum* (the workshop) to explore a relatively nascent but promising intersection of research areas: non-Euclidean geometry and Foundation Models, specifically with an emphasis on web applications. While geometric deep learning, particularly on hyperbolic and spherical spaces, is a established field, its integration with the relatively new area of very large foundation models is less mature. Specifically, the paper emphasizes the *web application* domain which brings new application specific requirements that merit distinct research.

The workshop's explicit focus on web-related applications differentiates it from other geometric deep learning workshops (like DiffGeo4DL, mentioned in the text) that might have broader scopes. This specialization represents a slight but important increment of novelty.

**Significance:** The significance of the proposed workshop can be assessed based on its potential to catalyze progress in the field. The arguments for using non-Euclidean spaces in web applications are well-articulated: these spaces naturally capture hierarchical relationships (e.g., in ontologies), complex topologies (e.g., social networks), and user-item interactions (recommendation systems). Foundation models provide a powerful paradigm for representation learning, and combining them with the geometric advantages of non-Euclidean spaces could unlock significant improvements in various web-related tasks.

The mention of "trustworthiness and robustness" is also a valuable point, indicating awareness of the ethical and practical considerations when deploying these models in real-world web applications.

The main weakness is the lack of concrete details about how the workshop will specifically foster innovation. While the themes and structure are sound, the actual success hinges on the quality of submissions, the engagement of participants, and the generation of new ideas during the discussions. The inclusion of diverse speakers and committee members is a positive sign, but it is not sufficient to guarantee significant impact.

Also, while it aims to address the limitations of Euclidean space in foundation models, the specific challenges and potential drawbacks of using non-Euclidean spaces at scale in foundation models are not explicitly addressed. This could be an area for further investigation and discussion during the workshop.

**Overall:** The paper presents a worthwhile initiative. While not groundbreaking in isolation, the *workshop* holds potential to facilitate important cross-pollination of ideas and accelerate research in a promising area. The significance lies in *bringing together* researchers from related fields to focus on a specific problem domain (web applications) and to address theoretical and practical concerns.
Score: 6

**Rationale:** The score reflects the moderate novelty and significant *potential* impact of the workshop. It is not a higher score because the paper describes a *planned event* and the *actual impact* will depend on the quality of participation and subsequent research. A score above 5 indicates that it is expected that this workshop will be meaningful in catalyzing important research for the field, but is not a fundamental new discovery in itself.

- **Classification**: cs.CG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Hidden Ghost Hand: Unveiling Backdoor Vulnerabilities in MLLM-Powered Mobile GUI Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14418v1)
- **Authors**: Pengzhou Cheng, Haowen Hu, Zheng Wu, Zongru Wu, Tianjie Ju, Daizong Ding, Zhuosheng Zhang, Gongshen Liu
- **Abstract**: Graphical user interface (GUI) agents powered by multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have shown greater promise for human-interaction. However, due to the high fine-tuning cost, users often rely on open-source GUI agents or APIs offered by AI providers, which introduces a critical but underexplored supply chain threat: backdoor attacks. In this work, we first unveil that MLLM-powered GUI agents naturally expose multiple interaction-level triggers, such as historical steps, environment states, and task progress. Based on this observation, we introduce AgentGhost, an effective and stealthy framework for red-teaming backdoor attacks. Specifically, we first construct composite triggers by combining goal and interaction levels, allowing GUI agents to unintentionally activate backdoors while ensuring task utility. Then, we formulate backdoor injection as a Min-Max optimization problem that uses supervised contrastive learning to maximize the feature difference across sample classes at the representation space, improving flexibility of the backdoor. Meanwhile, it adopts supervised fine-tuning to minimize the discrepancy between backdoor and clean behavior generation, enhancing effectiveness and utility. Extensive evaluations of various agent models in two established mobile benchmarks show that AgentGhost is effective and generic, with attack accuracy that reaches 99.7\% on three attack objectives, and shows stealthiness with only 1\% utility degradation. Furthermore, we tailor a defense method against AgentGhost that reduces the attack accuracy to 22.1\%. Our code is available at \texttt{anonymous}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Hidden Ghost Hand: Unveiling Backdoor Vulnerabilities in MLLM-Powered Mobile GUI Agents":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces AgentGhost, a novel framework for injecting backdoor vulnerabilities into multimodal large language model (MLLM)-powered GUI agents. It exploits multiple interaction-level triggers (historical steps, environment states, task progress) to construct composite triggers at both the goal and interaction levels, allowing GUI agents to activate backdoors stealthily while maintaining task utility. The backdoor injection is formulated as a Min-Max optimization problem using supervised contrastive learning to maximize feature differences between clean and poisoned samples. The framework is evaluated across various agent models and mobile benchmarks, demonstrating high attack accuracy and minimal utility degradation. A tailored defense method is also presented.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper tackles a relatively underexplored area: backdoor attacks on MLLM-powered GUI agents. The identification of multiple interaction-level triggers and their use in constructing composite triggers is a valuable contribution. The Min-Max optimization approach with supervised contrastive learning adds another layer of sophistication. However, backdoor attacks on LLMs are not entirely new, so while the specific application to GUI agents and the focus on episode-level stealth is novel, the underlying techniques build upon existing work.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a critical security vulnerability in a rapidly developing field. As MLLM-powered GUI agents become more prevalent, the risk of supply chain attacks increases, making this research timely and relevant. The demonstration of high attack accuracy and minimal utility degradation underscores the severity of the threat. The introduction of a defense method provides a starting point for mitigation strategies. The potential impact on the security and trust of GUI agents is significant. The identified vulnerabilities make GUI agents potential targets for exploitation, and understanding and mitigating these vulnerabilities is crucial for the responsible deployment of such systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed and comprehensive framework (AgentGhost).
    *   Extensive experimental evaluation across diverse models and benchmarks.
    *   Demonstration of high attack accuracy and stealthiness.
    *   Introduction of a defense method.
    *   Focus on episode-level scenarios of GUI agents.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper introduces the use of stealthy interaction-level conditions to activate a trigger, the underlying backdoor attack methodology relies on existing contrastive learning and supervised fine-tuning techniques. Thus, the novelty of the individual components could be questioned.
    *   The ethical considerations, while mentioned, could be more thoroughly discussed, especially regarding the potential misuse of the identified vulnerabilities.
    *   The defense method, while a valuable addition, only achieves limited success in reducing attack accuracy, suggesting that further research is needed to develop more effective mitigation strategies.
    *   The limitations of using *ToolUsing* as the primary attack action across all defined threats might limit the generalizability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research directions in several ways:
    *   It can spur the development of more robust defense mechanisms against backdoor attacks in GUI agents.
    *   It can motivate further exploration of the ethical implications of MLLM-powered agent technology.
    *   It can inspire research into more sophisticated attack strategies that are even more difficult to detect and mitigate.
    *   It contributes to a broader understanding of security vulnerabilities in MLLM-based systems.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper provides a concrete contribution to the study of MLLM security, revealing vulnerabilities in GUI agents. The work is well-executed with a focus on maintaining task utility, while successfully implanting backdoors. However, there's some reliance on existing techniques for backdoor attacks. Though this is a valuable finding in MLLMs for GUI agents, the contribution is somewhat incremental in nature.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MindVote: How LLMs Predict Human Decision-Making in Social Media Polls
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14422v1)
- **Authors**: Xutao Mao, Ezra Xuanru Tao
- **Abstract**: The increasing complexity of Large Language Models (LLMs) necessitates new benchmarks to assess their ability to predict human decision-making in dynamic social contexts. We introduce MindVote, the first benchmark for evaluating LLMs as "virtual respondents" in social media polling. MindVote comprises 276 poll instances with 1,142 data entry points from three platforms (Weibo, Reddit, Fizz), features bilingual content (Chinese/English), and covers five domains. Our evaluation of 18 LLMs demonstrates that top-performing models achieve an overall score of 0.74, an 80% relative improvement over traditional baselines, and then we analyze LLM world model bias with human preferences across societal bias dimensions. MindVote also uncovers significant disparities related to platform, language, and domain. We present strategies to optimize LLM performance and use LLM-as-a-Judge to assess reasoning in societal contexts. Furthermore, we show that temperature controls can reflect a way of human thinking diversity and opinion shifts in polling. In summary, MindVote offers a scalable framework for evaluating LLMs' social intelligence, with implications for understanding behavioral decision-making. Code and data will be available soon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MindVote, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to understand and predict human decision-making within the complex and dynamic context of social media polls. Unlike traditional survey settings, social media polls present unique challenges such as platform biases, peer influence, and informal language. The MindVote benchmark consists of 276 poll instances with 1,142 data entry points from three platforms (Weibo, Reddit, Fizz), bilingual content (Chinese/English), and covers five domains (College, Entertainment, Sport, Technology, Lifestyle). The authors evaluate 18 LLMs using MindVote and analyze performance disparities related to platform, language, and domain. The paper also explores strategies to optimize LLM performance, uses LLM-as-a-Judge to assess reasoning in societal contexts, and demonstrates how temperature controls can reflect human thinking diversity in polling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The creation of MindVote is a significant and novel contribution. While LLMs have been benchmarked on various tasks, this paper fills a gap by focusing on the specific challenges of social media polling, which is a very dynamic and relevant space. Conceptualizing the LLM as a "virtual respondent" within the MDC framework is also innovative and provides a solid theoretical basis for analysis. The use of real-world poll data from diverse platforms and in multiple languages adds significant complexity and ecological validity. The emphasis on analyzing how LLMs diverge from actual human preferences when considering factors like platform, language, and user profile highlights a crucial area often overlooked in standard LLM evaluations.

*   **Significance:**  The paper's findings are significant because they highlight the limitations of current LLMs in understanding nuanced human decision-making in complex social contexts. The discovery of performance hierarchies across different LLM architectures and the observation of domain, platform, and language biases are valuable insights for future LLM development. The strategies for optimizing LLM performance (e.g., 2-shot prompting, token budget) provide practical guidance for researchers and practitioners. Furthermore, exploring temperature control as a proxy for human thinking diversity opens up new avenues for investigating and modeling collective intelligence.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined research questions.
    *   A comprehensive benchmark (MindVote) with diverse datasets.
    *   Rigorous evaluation methodology with multiple metrics (Agreement, KL Divergence, Overall Score).
    *   Clear identification of performance hierarchies and biases.
    *   Valuable insights for optimizing LLM performance and capturing human thinking diversity.
    *   Use of the MDC framework grounding.
    *   Focus on societal impact and potential risks of misaligned LLM preferences.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The benchmark's scope is limited in terms of the range of social media platforms and types of polls. While the chosen platforms are representative, they don't encompass every possible variation.
    *   GPT-4.5 could not be included due to API limits which is a major limitation.
    *   The LLM-as-a-Judge approach, while insightful, introduces some level of subjectivity in reasoning evaluation.
    *   The world model bias analysis has the limitation of the analysis still being at a very high level. For instance, it's not clear *how* "factual knowledge" manifests and leads to inaccurate predictions.
    *   The reliance on Zero-shot prompting. Zero shot is valuable for understanding inherent limitations, but it would be very beneficial to understand how to design even a single-digit few-shot examples.

*   **Potential Influence:**  MindVote has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating LLMs' social intelligence and their ability to model human decision-making in online environments. The paper's findings can guide future research in developing more socially aware and ethically aligned LLMs. It can also inform the development of AI agents that participate in or influence societal feedback mechanisms like social media voting.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning this paper a score of **8/10**.

*   The paper makes a novel and significant contribution by introducing MindVote. It tackles a very timely and important problem: evaluating LLMs in the complex context of social media decision-making.
*   The research is well-executed, rigorously evaluated, and clearly presented. The findings offer valuable insights for the field.
*   The paper could have been improved by providing more in-depth analysis of LLM world model bias and exploring other advanced prompting strategies to more specifically solve the Zero-Shot limitations.
*   The focus on the ethical implications and societal impact of LLM-driven decision-making further enhances the paper's value.

Even considering the limitations, the development of this new benchmark and the systematic study of different factors make it a very high-quality contribution. Therefore, an 8/10 rating is a reasonable, defensible, and rigorous assessment.

- **Classification**: cs.SI
- **Score**: 0/10

### From Templates to Natural Language: Generalization Challenges in Instruction-Tuned LLMs for Spatial Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14425v1)
- **Authors**: Chalamalasetti Kranti, Sherzod Hakimov, David Schlangen
- **Abstract**: Instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on a variety of tasks; however, generalizing from synthetic to human-authored instructions in grounded environments remains a challenge for them. In this work, we study generalization challenges in spatial grounding tasks where models interpret and translate instructions for building object arrangements on a $2.5$D grid. We fine-tune LLMs using only synthetic instructions and evaluate their performance on a benchmark dataset containing both synthetic and human-written instructions. Our results reveal that while models generalize well on simple tasks, their performance degrades significantly on more complex tasks. We present a detailed error analysis of the gaps in instruction generalization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the challenges of instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs) in generalizing from synthetic to human-authored instructions in spatial reasoning tasks. Specifically, it focuses on a structure-building task where models must interpret and translate instructions to arrange objects on a 2.5D grid. The authors fine-tune LLMs using only synthetic, template-based instructions and then evaluate their performance on a dataset containing both synthetic and human-written instructions. The key finding is that while the models perform well on simpler tasks, their performance significantly degrades on more complex tasks involving object repetition. The authors perform a detailed error analysis, identifying referential ambiguity as a key factor hindering generalization and conclude that fine-tuning solely on synthetic datasets is insufficient for robust transfer to complex spatial concepts expressed in natural language.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** While the observation that LLMs struggle to generalize from synthetic to natural language is not entirely new, this paper makes a contribution by specifically examining this issue within the context of *spatial reasoning* and *structure building*. Previous work has touched upon this issue in other domains, such as robotics or general instruction following. The paper's focus on the compositional and structural aspects of the task adds some novelty. The error analysis providing qualitative insights into the types of failures is also a positive aspect.

**Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they highlight the limitations of relying solely on synthetic data for training LLMs, particularly when dealing with tasks that require grounding and spatial understanding. Many real-world applications of LLMs will involve interaction with physical environments, and this work underscores the need for more sophisticated training strategies that can bridge the gap between synthetic and natural language representations of spatial concepts. The analysis of referential ambiguity and its impact on performance also offers valuable insights for future research in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Question:** The paper clearly defines the problem being addressed and provides a well-defined experimental setup.
*   **Controlled Experimentation:** The use of both synthetic and human-authored instructions allows for a direct comparison of model performance under different conditions.
*   **Detailed Error Analysis:** The qualitative analysis of errors provides valuable insights into the types of mistakes that the models are making, and suggests potential avenues for improvement.
*   **Relevant Domain:** The study focuses on spatial reasoning, a crucial area for real-world applications of LLMs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Synthetic Data:** The paper relies on a very specific, template-based approach to generating synthetic instructions. This raises the question of whether the results generalize to other types of synthetic data or to scenarios where the synthetic data is more diverse and less structured.
*   **Task Complexity:** While the regular board task is complex relative to the simple board, the overall task (arranging colored shapes on an 8x8 grid) is somewhat artificial and limited. The complexity metric could have been refined to better categorize different degrees of complexity, as well as other spatial relation types.
*   **Model Selection**: The results rely on models fine-tuned via low-resource methods using QLoRA. Better performance could be realized with full fine-tuning and increased compute, so it may be premature to call the generalization limited without more rigorous ablation study.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6** to this paper. While the paper makes a worthwhile contribution to the field by examining the generalization capabilities of instruction-tuned LLMs in spatial reasoning, it suffers from certain limitations. The error analysis, while insightful, could be more exhaustive. The relatively limited dataset, the artificial nature of the task and the limitation of focusing on one spatial task (assembly of objects in a 2.5D grid) somewhat limit the impact of the paper. The work would benefit from exploring different types of synthetic data generation strategies and evaluating the models on more complex and diverse real-world spatial reasoning tasks. While the conclusions are valid and the findings are interesting, the novelty and broader implications are somewhat constrained by these limitations. The error analysis of the task provides value, and it has the potential to open directions for future work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Rank-K: Test-Time Reasoning for Listwise Reranking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14432v1)
- **Authors**: Eugene Yang, Andrew Yates, Kathryn Ricci, Orion Weller, Vivek Chari, Benjamin Van Durme, Dawn Lawrie
- **Abstract**: Retrieve-and-rerank is a popular retrieval pipeline because of its ability to make slow but effective rerankers efficient enough at query time by reducing the number of comparisons. Recent works in neural rerankers take advantage of large language models for their capability in reasoning between queries and passages and have achieved state-of-the-art retrieval effectiveness. However, such rerankers are resource-intensive, even after heavy optimization. In this work, we introduce Rank-K, a listwise passage reranking model that leverages the reasoning capability of the reasoning language model at query time that provides test time scalability to serve hard queries. We show that Rank-K improves retrieval effectiveness by 23\% over the RankZephyr, the state-of-the-art listwise reranker, when reranking a BM25 initial ranked list and 19\% when reranking strong retrieval results by SPLADE-v3. Since Rank-K is inherently a multilingual model, we found that it ranks passages based on queries in different languages as effectively as it does in monolingual retrieval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Rank-K: Test-Time Reasoning for Listwise Reranking":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Rank-K, a novel listwise passage reranking model that leverages the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) at query time to improve retrieval effectiveness. Rank-K is fine-tuned from QwQ-32B, distilling reasoning traces produced by DeepSeek R1. The key innovation is the integration of test-time reasoning into a listwise reranking framework. The authors demonstrate that Rank-K outperforms the state-of-the-art listwise reranker (RankZephyr) and shows strong cross-language transfer capabilities. The paper includes details on the training data generation, model architecture, and experimental evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating a reasoning trace at test time into a listwise reranking model is reasonably novel. Previous work has explored listwise reranking with LLMs (RankZephyr, RankGPT), but they largely focus on generating the final ranking directly without the explicit reasoning step. The distillation of reasoning traces from a larger model to a smaller one is also not entirely new, but its application in the context of listwise reranking is a worthwhile contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the effectiveness of retrieval systems, particularly for complex queries where reasoning is essential. The reported improvements over existing state-of-the-art listwise rerankers (RankZephyr) are substantial. Also, the demonstration of cross-lingual transferability is valuable, which means the model trained on English data can be readily applied to other languages without additional training. The release of the training data, model, and scripts is also a significant contribution that will facilitate future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The idea of test-time reasoning is compelling and well-motivated, showing strong potential.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements, especially on more challenging datasets (NeuCLIR).
    *   The cross-lingual evaluation adds to the significance and applicability of the work.
    *   The release of the dataset and code is an important contribution to the community.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The model relies on large and computationally expensive LLMs (DeepSeek R1, QwQ 32B), which limits its practical applicability in resource-constrained settings. While knowledge distillation helps, the distilled model is still fairly large (QwQ-32B). The distillation process may also lead to some loss of reasoning ability compared to the teacher model.
    *   The reported improvements on TREC DL 2019 and 2020 are smaller than on NeuCLIR, suggesting that the benefit of Rank-K may be more pronounced for certain types of queries or datasets. More detailed analysis of query types that benefit the most from Rank-K would be useful.
    *   Although the authors report nDCG@10, it would be great to see results on other information retrieval metrics, like MAP, MRR or recall.
    *   The hyperparameter tuning on TREC DL 2019 and 2020 benchmarks might result in overfitting which should be addressed.
    *   The scaling capacity is achieved with high computational cost. The computational cost of incremental reranking over reasoning is not investigated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in several ways:
    *   It highlights the importance of reasoning in retrieval systems.
    *   It provides a framework for incorporating reasoning into listwise reranking models.
    *   It encourages the development of more efficient reasoning algorithms and architectures.
    *   It inspires further exploration of cross-lingual transfer learning in retrieval.
    *   The released dataset and code could be used as a benchmark for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, the paper represents a solid contribution to the field of information retrieval. The integration of test-time reasoning into listwise reranking is a valuable approach, and the experimental results are compelling. However, the reliance on large LLMs and the limited analysis of query types somewhat limit its immediate impact. The dataset released by the authors is quite important and worth some points.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Choosing a Model, Shaping a Future: Comparing LLM Perspectives on Sustainability and its Relationship with AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14435v1)
- **Authors**: Annika Bush, Meltem Aksoy, Markus Pauly, Greta Ontrup
- **Abstract**: As organizations increasingly rely on AI systems for decision support in sustainability contexts, it becomes critical to understand the inherent biases and perspectives embedded in Large Language Models (LLMs). This study systematically investigates how five state-of-the-art LLMs -- Claude, DeepSeek, GPT, LLaMA, and Mistral - conceptualize sustainability and its relationship with AI. We administered validated, psychometric sustainability-related questionnaires - each 100 times per model -- to capture response patterns and variability. Our findings revealed significant inter-model differences: For example, GPT exhibited skepticism about the compatibility of AI and sustainability, whereas LLaMA demonstrated extreme techno-optimism with perfect scores for several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Models also diverged in attributing institutional responsibility for AI and sustainability integration, a results that holds implications for technology governance approaches. Our results demonstrate that model selection could substantially influence organizational sustainability strategies, highlighting the need for awareness of model-specific biases when deploying LLMs for sustainability-related decision-making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the perspectives of five large language models (LLMs) – Claude, DeepSeek, GPT, LLaMA, and Mistral – on sustainability and its relationship with AI. It uses validated psychometric questionnaires to capture response patterns and variability. The study reveals significant inter-model differences, such as GPT exhibiting skepticism towards AI and sustainability's compatibility, while LLaMA demonstrates techno-optimism.  The models also differ in their attribution of institutional responsibility for integrating AI and sustainability, leading to implications for technology governance. The paper argues that model selection can substantially influence organizational sustainability strategies, highlighting the need to be aware of model-specific biases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a crucial and timely topic: the inherent biases and perspectives of LLMs in sustainability-related decision-making. As organizations increasingly rely on AI for sustainability initiatives, understanding how these models conceptualize sustainability becomes paramount. The methodology is sound, using validated psychometric instruments and rigorous statistical analysis (nonparametric multiple contrast testing procedures). The use of multiple LLMs, including both closed- and open-source models, strengthens the study's comprehensiveness.

*Strengths:*

*   **Relevance:**  The topic is highly relevant to current trends and challenges in AI and sustainability.
*   **Methodology:**  The quantitative approach using validated questionnaires and statistical analysis provides a structured and replicable way to assess LLM perspectives. The approach of framing sustainability through validated psychometric scales is a strength.
*   **Comparative Analysis:** The study directly compares different LLMs, highlighting the significant inter-model differences. This is valuable for practitioners choosing which model to use.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper emphasizes the practical implications of model selection on organizational sustainability strategies, urging caution and awareness of biases.
*   **Clear findings:** The research uncovers distinct 'sustainability personalities' among LLMs, extending the understanding of AI bias beyond demographic or political dimensions.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Human-Designed Questionnaires for LLMs:** Adapting psychometric instruments designed for humans to AI systems introduces potential limitations. The internal experiences of humans aren't reflected in the behavior of LLMs. The "attitudes" described here can only be described as statistical patterns, not necessarily attitudes.
*   **Static Snapshot:** The rapid evolution of LLMs means the findings may become quickly outdated. The results are reflective of a specific point in time and may not generalize to future versions of the models.
*   **English Language Bias:**  The monolingual approach (using English questionnaires) could introduce linguistic biases, limiting the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Prompting Limitations:** While standardized prompts were used, architectural and operational differences between models might not have been fully addressed, potentially affecting accuracy. However, the paper acknowledges this and suggests further study on the differences.

*Novelty and Significance:*

The novelty lies in the systematic quantitative comparison of multiple LLMs on sustainability-related topics using validated psychometric scales. While previous studies have examined LLM perspectives on sustainability, few have used this approach to directly compare different models and uncover their distinct biases. The significance stems from its direct implications for organizations deploying AI in sustainability contexts. It highlights that LLMs are not neutral tools and that model selection requires careful consideration of potential biases. It also serves as a reminder that LLM output is statistical pattern recognition of prior human input, not necessarily representative of human perceptions.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence:

*   **AI ethics and governance:** By highlighting the need for bias awareness in sustainability applications.
*   **Organizational decision-making:** By informing the selection of LLMs for sustainability strategies.
*   **Future research:** By providing a framework for evaluating LLM perspectives and developing bias mitigation techniques.

Overall, the paper provides valuable insights into LLM perspectives on sustainability and its relationship with AI. The methodology is sound, and the findings have practical implications for organizations deploying AI in sustainability contexts. Despite some limitations (primarily related to adapting human-designed instruments to LLMs and the rapidly evolving nature of the field), the study makes a significant contribution to the field.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Neural Incompatibility: The Unbridgeable Gap of Cross-Scale Parametric Knowledge Transfer in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14436v1)
- **Authors**: Yuqiao Tan, Shizhu He, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a transparent brain with accessible parameters that encode extensive knowledge, which can be analyzed, located and transferred. Consequently, a key research challenge is to transcend traditional knowledge transfer paradigms rooted in symbolic language and achieve genuine Parametric Knowledge Transfer (PKT). Significantly, exploring effective methods for transferring knowledge across LLMs of different scales through parameters presents an intriguing and valuable research direction. In this paper, we first demonstrate $\textbf{Alignment}$ in parametric space is the fundamental prerequisite to achieve successful cross-scale PKT. We redefine the previously explored knowledge transfer as Post-Align PKT (PostPKT), which utilizes extracted parameters for LoRA initialization and requires subsequent fine-tune for alignment. Hence, to reduce cost for further fine-tuning, we introduce a novel Pre-Align PKT (PrePKT) paradigm and propose a solution called $\textbf{LaTen}$ ($\textbf{L}$oc$\textbf{a}$te-$\textbf{T}$h$\textbf{e}$n-Alig$\textbf{n}$) that aligns the parametric spaces of LLMs across scales only using several training steps without following training. Comprehensive experiments on four benchmarks demonstrate that both PostPKT and PrePKT face challenges in achieving consistently stable transfer. Through in-depth analysis, we identify $\textbf{Neural Incompatibility}$ as the ethological and parametric structural differences between LLMs of varying scales, presenting fundamental challenges to achieving effective PKT. These findings provide fresh insights into the parametric architectures of LLMs and highlight promising directions for future research on efficient PKT. Our code is available at https://github.com/Trae1ounG/Neural_Incompatibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Neural Incompatibility: The Unbridgeable Gap of Cross-Scale Parametric Knowledge Transfer in Large Language Models" investigates the challenge of transferring knowledge directly between Large Language Models (LLMs) of different sizes by manipulating their parameters (Parametric Knowledge Transfer or PKT). The authors argue that successful PKT requires *alignment* of the parametric spaces between the source and target LLMs. They introduce a novel "Locate-Then-Align" (LaTen) method to address this alignment issue before knowledge injection (Pre-Align PKT). Through experiments on various benchmarks, they demonstrate that both Post-Align (alignment *after* injection) and Pre-Align PKT face challenges in achieving consistent transfer. They identify "Neural Incompatibility," stemming from ethological and structural differences between LLMs of varying scales, as a fundamental obstacle to efficient PKT.  The paper suggests this incompatibility hinders consistent and effective knowledge transfer, hindering efficient transfer.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The concept of exploring knowledge transfer specifically through *parametric manipulation* across *different-scale* LLMs is reasonably novel. Previous work focused primarily on same-scale merging or knowledge distillation using language-based methods or logit manipulation.  The formalization of the problem as requiring "alignment" and distinguishing between Pre-Align and Post-Align PKT is a useful conceptual contribution. The LaTen method, attempting to pre-align parametric spaces, is also a novel technical contribution. However, some works like SEEKING explore similar concepts, which somewhat reduces the paper's initial novelty claims. The neural incompatibility angle is relatively new.

* **Significance:** If the conclusions hold, they are *potentially* significant. The finding that structural and behavioral differences limit parameter transfer between LLMs has implications for how we design and train future models for knowledge sharing. If it turns out, that models *cannot* efficiently transfer knowledge via parameter manipulation at differing scales, this affects future research directions. If proven, this changes approach to model training, efficient deployment and maintenance. It can potentially address catastrophic interference in models.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Formulation:** Clearly defining the PKT problem and highlighting the importance of alignment is a strong point. The Pre-Align vs. Post-Align dichotomy provides a useful framework for analyzing knowledge transfer methods.
    * **LaTen Method:** The proposed LaTen method is a concrete attempt to address the alignment challenge.
    * **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents results on several benchmarks, giving some support to their claims. They provide several experiments that clearly highlight key results.
    * **Identification of Neural Incompatibility:**  The hypothesis that structural and behavioral differences in LLMs hinder parameter transfer is a valuable direction for future research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Experiments:** While the benchmarks used are standard, the experiments are somewhat limited to a 13B to 7B scale. Wider exploration on different types of architectures or scaling parameters is needed.
    * **Lack of Robustness:** The paper admits that LaTen doesn't consistently achieve stable transfer. This suggests that the approach is not fully robust or reliable.
    * **Alternative Explanations:** The "Neural Incompatibility" hypothesis is compelling, but it's possible that other factors contribute to the observed difficulties. For example, the simple linear projection methods used in LaTen may not be sufficient to capture complex non-linear relationships between the parametric spaces of different LLMs.
    * **Incremental contribution:** The SEEKING and other related works already exist and the paper doesn't present a huge jump from that.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the direction of research in knowledge transfer, prompting researchers to explore methods for addressing neural incompatibility or develop alternative parameter transfer techniques. The "alignment" framing could become a standard way of thinking about this problem.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a worthwhile contribution by addressing a practical problem (transferring knowledge across LLMs of different sizes) and proposing a concrete solution (LaTen). The identification of Neural Incompatibility provides a valuable framing for future research. The empirical analysis, although not definitive, is a solid start.

However, the limited scope, the lack of robustness of the proposed method, and possible alternative explanations for the observed difficulties warrant a somewhat lower score. The existence of related papers reduce the contribution as well. Also, even though there are empirical analyses, their impact is not well-understood, due to the complexity of LLMs and parametric transfer,

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### S2SBench: A Benchmark for Quantifying Intelligence Degradation in Speech-to-Speech Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14438v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanbo Fang, Haoze Sun, Jun Liu, Tao Zhang, Zenan Zhou, Weipeng Chen, Xiaofen Xing, Xiangmin Xu
- **Abstract**: End-to-end speech large language models ((LLMs)) extend the capabilities of text-based models to directly process and generate audio tokens. However, this often leads to a decline in reasoning and generation performance compared to text input, a phenomenon referred to as intelligence degradation. To systematically evaluate this gap, we propose S2SBench, a benchmark designed to quantify performance degradation in Speech LLMs. It includes diagnostic datasets targeting sentence continuation and commonsense reasoning under audio input. We further introduce a pairwise evaluation protocol based on perplexity differences between plausible and implausible samples to measure degradation relative to text input. We apply S2SBench to analyze the training process of Baichuan-Audio, which further demonstrates the benchmark's effectiveness. All datasets and evaluation code are available at https://github.com/undobug/S2SBench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces S2SBench, a novel benchmark for quantifying the "intelligence degradation" observed when using end-to-end speech large language models (LLMs) compared to their text-based counterparts. The benchmark includes diagnostic datasets focused on sentence continuation and commonsense reasoning, evaluated under both audio and text input modalities. A pairwise evaluation protocol, based on perplexity differences between plausible and implausible samples, is proposed to measure the degradation. The benchmark is applied to analyze the training process of Baichuan-Audio, demonstrating its effectiveness. The authors make their datasets and evaluation code publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution lies in its systematic approach to measuring and quantifying intelligence degradation in speech LLMs. While the individual components (using story cloze datasets, perplexity-based evaluation) are not entirely novel, their combination, targeted specifically at addressing the audio-vs-text gap in LLMs, is a valuable contribution. The construction of the zh-sStoryCloze dataset is a minor, but useful addition. The pairwise evaluation protocol is a reasonable and appropriate method for the task.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical issue in the development of end-to-end speech LLMs: the performance gap compared to text-based LLMs. By providing a benchmark and analysis tools, the paper facilitates the development of models that can better handle audio input. This contributes to the broader field of multimodal LLMs and could lead to more robust and useful speech-based interfaces. The application to Baichuan-Audio is useful for demonstrating the efficacy of their training methodology.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the issue of intelligence degradation.
    *   Systematic approach: The S2SBench provides a structured framework for evaluation.
    *   Diagnostic datasets: The datasets are designed to pinpoint specific aspects of reasoning ability.
    *   Public availability: The open-sourcing of datasets and code promotes reproducibility and further research.
    *   Application to a real-world model: Analysis of Baichuan-Audio demonstrates the benchmark's practical value.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited scope: The evaluation focuses on two specific tasks (sentence continuation and commonsense reasoning). While important, these might not fully capture the breadth of "intelligence" in LLMs. The paper mentions limitations in speech-to-speech evaluation.
    *   Incremental improvement: The methods applied are reasonably standard and are used to validate known LLM training techniques.
    *   The zh-sStoryCloze dataset is essentially translated.

*   **Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable and timely contribution to the field of speech LLMs. By systematically studying and quantifying intelligence degradation, it provides a useful tool for researchers and practitioners. While the evaluation tasks could be expanded, and the technical novelty of the pairwise evaluation could be higher, the overall contribution to the field is significant. The availability of datasets and code further enhances the impact. The use of two-stage training is not that novel itself, but its usage is effectively showcased by this benchmark, making the paper of moderate significance.
