### Teaching Audio-Aware Large Language Models What Does Not Hear: Mitigating Hallucinations through Synthesized Negative Samples
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14518v1)
- **Authors**: Chun-Yi Kuan, Hung-yi Lee
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in audio-aware large language models (ALLMs) enable them to process and understand audio inputs. However, these models often hallucinate non-existent sound events, reducing their reliability in real-world applications. To address this, we propose LISTEN (Learning to Identify Sounds Through Extended Negative Samples), a contrastive-like training method that enhances ALLMs' ability to distinguish between present and absent sounds using synthesized data from the backbone LLM. Unlike prior approaches, our method requires no modification to LLM parameters and efficiently integrates audio representations via a lightweight adapter. Experiments show that LISTEN effectively mitigates hallucinations while maintaining impressive performance on existing audio question and reasoning benchmarks. At the same time, it is more efficient in both data and computation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of hallucinations in audio-aware large language models (ALLMs), where these models incorrectly identify sound events that aren't present in the audio. The authors propose LISTEN, a contrastive-like training method that enhances ALLMs' ability to distinguish between present and absent sounds. This method utilizes synthesized data generated from the LLM itself, including both positive (present sounds) and negative (absent sounds) samples. A lightweight adapter is trained to map audio representations to the LLM's input dimensions, while the LLM's parameters remain frozen. The experiments demonstrate that LISTEN effectively mitigates hallucinations, maintains performance on standard audio benchmarks, and is more data-efficient compared to existing approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel approach to address the practically important problem of hallucination in ALLMs, which is particularly important for real-world deployment of such models.  While using synthesized data and contrastive learning are not entirely new concepts, their application to mitigating hallucinations in ALLMs, especially with the self-generated negative samples, offers a fresh perspective. The efficient adapter-based training is also a valuable contribution. The most significant novelty lies in the targeted application to this specific problem. Previous research had noted hallucinations, but hadn't tackled them in a model training perspective.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of ALLMs in real-world applications.  Hallucinations can lead to errors in critical situations, like emergency response. By mitigating these errors, the paper contributes to the wider adoption and safe use of ALLMs. The data efficiency of the proposed method is also significant, as it reduces the need for extensive and expensive manually annotated datasets. Also the ablation studies are well designed, with clear conclusions on the importance of each component of the methodology.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Definition:** Clearly defines and addresses an important and timely problem in ALLMs.
    * **Methodological Innovation:** Introduces a practical and data-efficient training method with a clever contrastive-like approach.
    * **Experimental Validation:**  Provides comprehensive experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness of LISTEN on both hallucination mitigation and standard benchmark tasks. The ablation studies are particularly strong.
    * **Data Efficiency:** Demonstrates significant reduction in training data requirements compared to baseline methods.
    * **LLM Agnostic:** The methodology doesn't involve fine tuning the core LLM itself, reducing computational costs and avoiding catastrophic forgetting problems.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on Backbone LLM Quality:** The quality of the synthesized data is inherently tied to the capabilities of the backbone LLM.  While the experiments show that self-generated data is sufficient, there might be limitations when using less powerful LLMs.
    * **Limited Evaluation of Failure Cases:**  While the quantitative results are strong, a more in-depth analysis of specific types of hallucinations that are effectively mitigated (or not) would be beneficial. Analysis the types of samples that causes hallucinations or that cannot be solved with this methodology would be insightful.
    * **Generality on various LLMs and encoders:** The evaluation of this methodology is performed using specific LLMs and audio encoders. It will be helpful to demonstrate the effectiveness and ease of usage of this method using different backbone LLMs and audio encoders.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in ALLMs by highlighting the importance of hallucination mitigation and providing a practical and efficient approach to address this issue. The self-generated negative sampling technique could be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field. Also, the implementation details for the data construction and the adapter training could be open sourced to facilitate reproducibility and future research in the topic.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of audio-aware large language models by addressing the practical problem of hallucinations. The proposed LISTEN method is novel in its application of contrastive learning with self-generated negative samples and the use of a lightweight adapter for efficient training. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. The data efficiency and the fact that it doesn't modify the parameters of the LLM are key strengths.

However, the paper is not without limitations. The dependency on the backbone LLM's quality and the limited analysis of failure cases prevent it from achieving a higher score. Also there is a limited analysis of the influence on diverse LLMs and encoders. The paper has a clear and important contribution, but it could be strengthened by addressing these limitations in future work. Thus, a score of 7.5 reflects the paper's solid contribution and potential impact, while acknowledging the areas where further research is needed.

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 7/10

### SparC: Sparse Representation and Construction for High-Resolution 3D Shapes Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14521v1)
- **Authors**: Zhihao Li, Yufei Wang, Heliang Zheng, Yihao Luo, Bihan Wen
- **Abstract**: High-fidelity 3D object synthesis remains significantly more challenging than 2D image generation due to the unstructured nature of mesh data and the cubic complexity of dense volumetric grids. Existing two-stage pipelines-compressing meshes with a VAE (using either 2D or 3D supervision), followed by latent diffusion sampling-often suffer from severe detail loss caused by inefficient representations and modality mismatches introduced in VAE. We introduce SparC, a unified framework that combines a sparse deformable marching cubes representation SparseCubes with a novel encoder SparConv-VAE. SparseCubes converts raw meshes into high-resolution ($1024^3$) surfaces with arbitrary topology by scattering signed distance and deformation fields onto a sparse cube, allowing differentiable optimization. SparConv-VAE is the first modality-consistent variational autoencoder built entirely upon sparse convolutional networks, enabling efficient and near-lossless 3D reconstruction suitable for high-resolution generative modeling through latent diffusion. SparC achieves state-of-the-art reconstruction fidelity on challenging inputs, including open surfaces, disconnected components, and intricate geometry. It preserves fine-grained shape details, reduces training and inference cost, and integrates naturally with latent diffusion models for scalable, high-resolution 3D generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SparC: Sparse Representation and Construction for High-Resolution 3D Shapes Modeling":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SparC, a novel framework for high-resolution 3D shape modeling. It addresses limitations in existing two-stage pipelines (VAE + diffusion) that often suffer from detail loss due to inefficient representations and modality mismatches. SparC combines a sparse deformable marching cubes representation called SparseCubes with a novel sparse convolutional VAE (SparConv-VAE). SparseCubes converts raw, potentially non-watertight meshes into watertight surfaces at high resolution by scattering signed distance and deformation fields onto a sparse cube. SparConv-VAE is a modality-consistent VAE built entirely on sparse convolutional networks, enabling efficient and near-lossless 3D reconstruction. The framework achieves state-of-the-art reconstruction fidelity, preserves fine details, reduces training costs, and integrates well with latent diffusion models for scalable, high-resolution 3D generation. Key contributions include the SparCubes representation, the SparConv-VAE architecture, and their combination into a unified framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:
    *   **SparseCubes:** This sparse deformable marching cubes representation is a significant improvement over dense volumetric grids.  It provides a fast and near-lossless method for creating watertight meshes from raw data, something that has been a bottleneck in previous pipelines.
    *   **SparConv-VAE:** The modality-consistent sparse convolutional VAE is also novel. By working directly on the sparse representation and avoiding modality mismatches, it achieves better reconstruction performance with a lighter architecture compared to methods that use global attention or rely on 2D supervision.
    *   **Unified Framework:** The seamless integration of SparCubes and SparConv-VAE into a single framework is a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in 3D shape modeling: achieving high-resolution, detailed reconstructions and generations while maintaining efficiency. By tackling the representation and modality mismatch issues, SparC enables better results with reduced computational cost. The fact that SparC integrates well with existing latent diffusion pipelines further amplifies its potential impact. The near-lossless remeshing and reconstruction capabilities could have far-reaching implications for areas like 3D printing, AR/VR asset creation, and robotics simulations.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **State-of-the-art results:** The paper demonstrates superior reconstruction fidelity compared to previous methods on challenging datasets.
    *   **Efficiency:**  SparC reduces training costs and improves conversion speeds.
    *   **Watertight Meshes:** The framework produces watertight meshes suitable for real-world applications.
    *   **Integration with diffusion models:**  The ability to enhance existing latent diffusion models is a major advantage.
    *   **Modality consistency:** A key design consideration that resolves issues that existed in previous VAE designs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Texture Handling:** The method discards original texture information, which is a limitation for applications where texture is crucial. The paper mentions this limitation. This is a common issue in geometry-focused approaches, but it's still a noteworthy drawback.
    *   **Internal Structure Removal:** The method removes internal geometry when processing closed meshes. While this helps generate watertight surfaces, it could be a problem for cases where internal details are important. The paper acknowledges this limitation as well.
    *   **Dependence on VAE Training Data:** The performance hinges on the quality and diversity of the VAE training dataset. While the paper utilizes a large dataset, domain shift issues could arise when dealing with significantly different types of 3D shapes. This is a general weakness of data-driven approaches.

* **Potential Impact:** The framework presents an important improvement to the process of 3D generation, promising a path towards scalable high-resolution 3D asset creation. The modular design facilitates the easy integrations within existing diffusion model pipelines. The ability to generate water-tight meshes, with the capacity for 3D printing makes SparC applicable in various scenarios.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses of the paper, a score of 8 is appropriate. SparC presents a compelling solution to a significant problem in 3D shape modeling, achieving state-of-the-art results with improved efficiency. The SparCubes representation and SparConv-VAE architecture are novel and well-integrated. While the method has limitations regarding texture and internal structures, the paper acknowledges these drawbacks. Further refinement of SparC and extensions to handle textures and internal details could significantly enhance its impact. The score reflects the framework's solid contributions and its potential to influence future research in the field.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Guarded Query Routing for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14524v1)
- **Authors**: Richard Šléher, William Brach, Tibor Sloboda, Kristián Košťál, Lukas Galke
- **Abstract**: Query routing, the task to route user queries to different large language model (LLM) endpoints, can be considered as a text classification problem. However, out-of-distribution queries must be handled properly, as those could be questions about unrelated domains, queries in other languages, or even contain unsafe text. Here, we thus study a \emph{guarded} query routing problem, for which we first introduce the Guarded Query Routing Benchmark (GQR-Bench), which covers three exemplary target domains (law, finance, and healthcare), and seven datasets to test robustness against out-of-distribution queries. We then use GQR-Bench to contrast the effectiveness and efficiency of LLM-based routing mechanisms (GPT-4o-mini, Llama-3.2-3B, and Llama-3.1-8B), standard LLM-based guardrail approaches (LlamaGuard and NVIDIA NeMo Guardrails), continuous bag-of-words classifiers (WideMLP, fastText), and traditional machine learning models (SVM, XGBoost). Our results show that WideMLP, enhanced with out-of-domain detection capabilities, yields the best trade-off between accuracy (88\%) and speed (<4ms). The embedding-based fastText excels at speed (<1ms) with acceptable accuracy (80\%), whereas LLMs yield the highest accuracy (91\%) but are comparatively slow (62ms for local Llama-3.1:8B and 669ms for remote GPT-4o-mini calls). Our findings challenge the automatic reliance on LLMs for (guarded) query routing and provide concrete recommendations for practical applications. GQR-Bench will be released as a Python package -- \texttt{gqr}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Guarded Query Routing for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of efficiently and safely routing user queries to appropriate large language model (LLM) endpoints. It introduces the Guarded Query Routing Benchmark (GQR-Bench) to evaluate routing mechanisms against out-of-distribution (OOD) queries, covering various domains (law, finance, healthcare) and testing robustness against unrelated domains, unsupported languages, and unsafe content.  The paper compares the performance of various LLM-based routing (GPT-40-mini, Llama-3.x), standard guardrail approaches (LlamaGuard, NVIDIA NeMo Guardrails), continuous bag-of-words classifiers (WideMLP, fastText), and traditional machine learning models (SVM, XGBoost). The key finding is that efficient text classifiers like WideMLP and fastText, enhanced with OOD detection, offer a compelling alternative to relying solely on LLMs for query routing, achieving comparable accuracy with significantly reduced latency and computational cost.  The paper provides practical recommendations for optimizing the performance-cost-latency trade-off in domain-specific applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in framing query routing as a "guarded" problem explicitly addressing OOD scenarios, and providing a benchmark for it. Previous work on query routing often focuses on in-distribution accuracy or assumes a closed world, lacking explicit evaluation of robustness to unseen or adversarial inputs. The GQR-Bench is a valuable contribution for this aspect. However, using *existing* datasets to form the benchmark, while practical, somewhat diminishes the novelty. The adaptation of standard text classification techniques for OOD detection (e.g., thresholding with WideMLP, One-vs-Rest with fastText) is incremental rather than groundbreaking. While using efficient text classifiers is also not a novel concept in itself, the application and comparison to LLM routing and guardrails is a well-motivated idea.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance resides in its pragmatic approach and practical implications. The finding that relatively simple and efficient text classification models can perform comparably to, or even better than, LLMs for query routing in specific settings is a significant result, offering compelling arguments for cost optimization in real-world deployments. It challenges the automatic reliance on LLMs and provides concrete alternatives and benchmarks for evaluation. This could lead to substantial savings in computational resources and improvements in latency, particularly for high-volume applications. It emphasizes the importance of carefully considering the trade-offs between accuracy, latency, and cost when designing LLM-based systems. The revelation about the underperformance of fine-tuned LLM guardrail approaches in the ID routing task highlights the importance of evaluating these models using an appropriate framework.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem statement with a clear focus on guarded query routing.
    *   The introduction of GQR-Bench fills a gap in existing evaluation resources.
    *   The thorough empirical evaluation of various methods provides valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   The paper effectively highlights the trade-offs between accuracy, latency, and cost.
    *   The findings are well-supported by the experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the evaluation is comprehensive, the limited set of target domains (law, finance, healthcare) may limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The OOD datasets, although diverse, could benefit from the inclusion of more adversarial or deliberately crafted out-of-distribution examples.
    *   The method is quite specific and requires a careful selection of a probability threshold.
    *   Lack of deeper analysis regarding why standard guardrails underperform (beyond the potential interference mentioned).
    *   A better theoretical justification for why "relatively simple and efficient text classification models can perform comparably" to LLMs would add to the impact of this work.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence how practitioners approach query routing in LLM-based systems. It encourages a more nuanced and cost-aware approach, considering efficient text classifiers as viable alternatives to LLMs and emphasizing the importance of robustness against OOD queries. The GQR-Bench can serve as a valuable resource for future research and development in this area.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**
The paper makes a worthwhile contribution to the field of LLM deployment by highlighting the importance of guarded query routing and offering a practical benchmark (GQR-Bench) for evaluating robustness. The empirical findings, particularly the competitive performance of efficient text classifiers, are significant and challenge the automatic reliance on LLMs. While the methodological novelty is somewhat limited, the practical implications and the potential impact on cost optimization justify a score of 7. This suggests a solid and useful paper with practical implications, even if it doesn't present a groundbreaking theoretical advance.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### BugRepro: Enhancing Android Bug Reproduction with Domain-Specific Knowledge Integration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14528v1)
- **Authors**: Hongrong Yin, Tao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Mobile application development is a fast-paced process where maintaining high-quality user experiences is crucial. Current bug reproduction methods predominantly depend on precise feature descriptions in bug reports. However, the growing complexity and dynamism of modern software systems pose significant challenges to this crucial quality assurance process, as ambiguous or incomplete steps-to-reproduce (S2Rs) in reports frequently impede effective debugging and maintenance. To address these challenges, we propose BugRepro, a novel technique that integrates domain-specific knowledge to enhance the accuracy and efficiency of bug reproduction. BugRepro adopts a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) approach. It retrieves similar bug reports along with their corresponding S2R entities from an example-rich RAG document. This document serves as a valuable reference for improving the accuracy of S2R entity extraction. In addition, BugRepro incorporates app-specific knowledge. It explores the app's graphical user interface (GUI) and extracts UI transition graphs. These graphs are used to guide large language models (LLMs) in their exploration process when they encounter bottlenecks. Our experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of BugRepro. Our method significantly outperforms two state-of-the-art methods. For S2R entity extraction accuracy, it achieves improvements of 8.85% and 28.89%. For bug reproduction success rate, the improvements reach 74.55% and 152.63%. In reproduction efficiency, the gains are 0.72% and 76.68%.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "BugRepro: Enhancing Android Bug Reproduction with Domain-Specific Knowledge Integration".

**Summary:**

The paper presents BugRepro, a novel technique that integrates domain-specific knowledge to improve the accuracy and efficiency of Android bug reproduction. BugRepro utilizes a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) approach to retrieve similar bug reports and their corresponding step-to-reproduce (S2R) entities from an example-rich document. It also incorporates app-specific knowledge by exploring the app's graphical user interface (GUI) and extracting UI transition graphs to guide large language models (LLMs) in their exploration process when encountering bottlenecks. The authors demonstrate that BugRepro outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in S2R entity extraction accuracy and bug reproduction success rate, and offers improvements in reproduction efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Addressed:** The paper addresses a relevant and important problem in software quality assurance: the difficulty in reproducing bugs due to ambiguous or incomplete S2Rs in bug reports.
    *   **Novelty:** The integration of a RAG approach with app-specific GUI exploration techniques for LLM-guided bug reproduction seems reasonably novel. Prior LLM-based approaches typically rely on fewer contextual cues. The specific application of UI Transition Graphs (UTGs) is also a notable contribution.
    *   **Methodology:** The experimental methodology is sound. The authors compare BugRepro against state-of-the-art baselines, conduct ablation studies to evaluate individual components, and analyze the performance of BugRepro using different LLMs. The metrics are appropriate and well-defined. They chose metrics that adequately captured the improvements with using BugRepro as opposed to state-of-the-art.
    *   **Results:** The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of BugRepro. The reported improvements in S2R entity extraction accuracy and bug reproduction success rate are significant.
    *   **Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly organized. The authors effectively explain the motivation, approach, experimental setup, and results.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Generalizability:** While the dataset comprises real-world bugs, it is limited to four open-source datasets. The generalizability of BugRepro to other datasets or closed-source applications needs to be further investigated. The authors acknowledge some limitations in generalizability in the paper as well.
    *   **LLM Dependence:** The performance of BugRepro relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLM. While the authors evaluate BugRepro with two different LLMs, further evaluation with a wider range of LLMs would be beneficial.
    *   **UI Exploration Depth:** The fixed UI exploration depth might limit the exploration of more complex app workflows. A dynamic exploration depth might improve the reproduction success rate for certain bugs.
    *   **Limited Explanation of Error Cases:** While the paper provides quantitative results, a more in-depth discussion of the types of bugs that BugRepro struggles to reproduce would be valuable.
    *   **Potential for High Computational Cost:** Exploring UTGs of large apps can be computationally costly. The paper does not adequately discuss the scalability of BugRepro for extremely large or complex applications.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a promising solution to the challenges of bug reproduction in Android applications. The proposed technique has the potential to improve software quality assurance processes by reducing the time and resources required for debugging.
    *   The integration of RAG and app-specific knowledge offers a generalizable approach for enhancing the performance of LLMs in various software engineering tasks.
    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of research on LLM-based software engineering tools.

*Rationale for the Score:*

BugRepro presents a clear advancement over existing LLM-based methods for Android bug reproduction. The integration of RAG with UTG-driven exploration represents a novel and effective combination of techniques. The strong experimental results and comprehensive ablation studies support the claims made in the paper. However, there are several limitations with the dataset size and generalizability, and the dependence on specific LLMs, and possible scalability limitations with the UI graph exploration.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Internal Chain-of-Thought: Empirical Evidence for Layer-wise Subtask Scheduling in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14530v1)
- **Authors**: Zhipeng Yang, Junzhuo Li, Siyu Xia, Xuming Hu
- **Abstract**: We show that large language models (LLMs) exhibit an $\textit{internal chain-of-thought}$: they sequentially decompose and execute composite tasks layer-by-layer. Two claims ground our study: (i) distinct subtasks are learned at different network depths, and (ii) these subtasks are executed sequentially across layers. On a benchmark of 15 two-step composite tasks, we employ layer-from context-masking and propose a novel cross-task patching method, confirming (i). To examine claim (ii), we apply LogitLens to decode hidden states, revealing a consistent layerwise execution pattern. We further replicate our analysis on the real-world $\text{TRACE}$ benchmark, observing the same stepwise dynamics. Together, our results enhance LLMs transparency by showing their capacity to internally plan and execute subtasks (or instructions), opening avenues for fine-grained, instruction-level activation steering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit an "internal chain-of-thought" (ICoT), meaning they decompose and execute complex tasks sequentially across network layers, even when not explicitly prompted to do so. The authors present two key claims: (1) distinct subtasks are learned at different network depths, and (2) these subtasks are executed sequentially across layers.  They use layer-from context masking and a novel "cross-task patching" method to confirm claim (1). They employ LogitLens to decode hidden states and reveal a consistent layerwise execution pattern to support claim (2).  The analysis is extended to the real-world TRACE benchmark, observing similar dynamics. The authors argue this work enhances LLM transparency and could lead to fine-grained, instruction-level steering of model behavior.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of probing LLMs for latent, multi-stage processing isn't entirely new. Existing work has explored multi-hop reasoning and task vector representations. However, this paper's specific focus on **sequential subtask execution** *within* a single composite task, independent of explicit prompting, is a valuable contribution. The cross-task patching method seems particularly novel and designed to isolate and identify distinct task vectors. Also, extending the analysis to a complex real-world instruction following benchmark like TRACE makes the results more compelling.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in several areas:

*   **Enhanced Transparency:** Understanding how LLMs break down complex instructions internally helps make these models less of a "black box." This understanding is crucial for building trust and debugging unexpected behaviors.
*   **Fine-grained Control:** The potential to identify layers responsible for specific subtasks opens the door for targeted interventions. For example, identifying layers processing harmful instructions and intervening to prevent their execution.
*   **Improved Task Decomposition:** Revealing the LLMs' own methods to decompose complex tasks can help improving future models, as well as prompting strategies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Claims:** The paper presents clear and testable hypotheses.
*   **Multi-pronged Methodology:** The authors use multiple methods (context masking, cross-task patching, LogitLens) to validate their claims, providing strong empirical support.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The study covers multiple models, tasks, and benchmarks.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Task Complexity:** The composite tasks, while designed for sequential execution, are still relatively simple (antonym then uppercase, etc.).  It's not clear if the same patterns would emerge for significantly more complex or ambiguous tasks.
*   **LogitLens Limitations:** LogitLens provides a useful approximation, but is an indirect method of probing internal representations. It is possible the revealed patterns are artifacts of the decoding process, and not a direct reflection of the models internal processing.
*   **Model Size:** The study uses mid-sized models (3B-8B). The results may not generalize to much larger, state-of-the-art models with different architectures and training regimes.
*   **Lack of Direct Intervention:** The paper provides evidence for ICoT, but does not provide direct experiments to demonstrate effective instruction-level intervention based on their findings. Such an experiment would significantly strengthen the implications of the work.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper builds upon existing work in interpretability and task vector representations, the specific focus on internal chain-of-thought *without* explicit prompting, the novel cross-task patching method, and the extension to the TRACE benchmark contribute meaningfully to the field.  The limitations related to task complexity, model size, and the LogitLens methods weaken the claims somewhat. Crucially, the lack of direct control experiments limits the practical impact, and makes it a mainly theoretical discovery rather than a method to directly intervene with models. Therefore, I would assign the following score:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Energy-Efficient Deep Reinforcement Learning with Spiking Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14533v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammad Irfan Uddin, Nishad Tasnim, Md Omor Faruk, Zejian Zhou
- **Abstract**: Agent-based Transformers have been widely adopted in recent reinforcement learning advances due to their demonstrated ability to solve complex tasks. However, the high computational complexity of Transformers often results in significant energy consumption, limiting their deployment in real-world autonomous systems. Spiking neural networks (SNNs), with their biologically inspired structure, offer an energy-efficient alternative for machine learning. In this paper, a novel Spike-Transformer Reinforcement Learning (STRL) algorithm that combines the energy efficiency of SNNs with the powerful decision-making capabilities of reinforcement learning is developed. Specifically, an SNN using multi-step Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) neurons and attention mechanisms capable of processing spatio-temporal patterns over multiple time steps is designed. The architecture is further enhanced with state, action, and reward encodings to create a Transformer-like structure optimized for reinforcement learning tasks. Comprehensive numerical experiments conducted on state-of-the-art benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed SNN Transformer achieves significantly improved policy performance compared to conventional agent-based Transformers. With both enhanced energy efficiency and policy optimality, this work highlights a promising direction for deploying bio-inspired, low-cost machine learning models in complex real-world decision-making scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel Spike-Transformer Reinforcement Learning (STRL) algorithm.  STRL combines the energy efficiency of Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) with the decision-making capabilities of Transformers for reinforcement learning tasks. The core of the STRL is an SNN Transformer, built using multi-step Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) neurons and attention mechanisms.  It's designed to process spatio-temporal patterns, enhanced with state, action, and reward encodings similar to a Transformer structure. The authors demonstrate through experiments on maze navigation benchmarks that the proposed SNN Transformer achieves better policy performance than conventional agent-based Transformers while offering improved energy efficiency. The key contributions are a novel SNN Transformer architecture for RL and an algorithm that is energy-efficient due to the sparsity and event-driven nature of SNNs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of SNNs and Transformers for *reinforcement learning*. While both SNNs and Transformers are individually well-established, their synergistic use in the RL context with the proposed architecture (multi-step LIF neurons within a Transformer block) appears to be a novel approach. The incorporation of state, action and reward embeddings in a multi-step LIF based SNN transformer is another important aspect of this paper, enhancing the capability of RL task solving.

*   **Significance:** The significance hinges on the potential for STRL to address the energy consumption limitations of Transformers in real-world autonomous systems. The maze navigation tasks, while useful for demonstration, are relatively simple. The reported performance increase over a standard Decision Transformer is substantial on the chosen benchmark.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:**  The paper clearly identifies the energy inefficiency of standard Transformers in RL as a limitation for deployment in many real-world scenarios.
    *   **Well-Defined Architecture:** The SNN Transformer architecture is clearly described, including the use of multi-step LIF neurons and the integration of embeddings.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The empirical results on maze navigation benchmarks are compelling, showing a significant performance boost over a standard Decision Transformer. The added result of energy efficiency compared to existing DRL systems has the ability to create a significant impact on the field.
    *   **Good comparisons:** The paper has strong baseline DRL algorithms in terms of comparison. It also describes the baseline algorithms quite well for the readers.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Task Complexity:** Maze navigation, while a common RL benchmark, might not fully represent the complexity of real-world autonomous systems. The scale of tasks presented can also be enhanced for this paper. The addition of multiple tasks would have ensured the generalizability of the proposed approach.
    *   **Limited Energy Efficiency Data:** The paper claims energy efficiency improvements but lacks detailed energy consumption measurements of the SNN Transformer *in practice*. While the arguments about SNN sparsity leading to potential energy savings are valid, concrete energy efficiency experiments with neuromorphic hardware would significantly strengthen the claims.
    *   **Lack of comparison with other algorithms on different parameters**: Although, the paper compares with the baseline algorithm DT with different metrics like test accuracy, and test loss. It would be great if the paper had described the performance of DT algorithm on factors like energy efficiency as well.
    *   **No Hardware Implementation:** The algorithm proposed in the paper is completely theoretical as the experiments are simulated on machines with large GPUs. To achieve better performance in real world, it is imperative to implement the architecture in a hardware that supports SNN based algorithms.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research toward more energy-efficient RL algorithms. If the demonstrated performance and theoretical energy efficiency translate to tangible benefits in practical applications, it could drive further exploration of SNNs in RL.

*   **Missing Information**: It would be great if the paper included information like number of SNN layers, hidden dimensions, LIF threshold voltage of the neurons, or the overall computational cost. This extra details will help the researchers community to build upon the architecture.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-defined algorithm (STRL) that combines the energy-efficient aspects of SNNs with the strong decision making capabilities of Transformers for RL.  The experimental results clearly show a performance improvement on maze navigation tasks. This architecture is significant because current deep learning agents usually have limitations for real world applications that depends on high energy resources.  However, the task complexity is limited, and the energy efficiency claims lack thorough experimental validation, specifically, there's no hardware implementation. Given these limitations, the significance and potential for impact are somewhat limited, but the work provides a strong foundation and a promising direction for future research. If the efficiency claims can be verified in hardware, and if the method scales to complex tasks, this could become a more significant contribution. However, as it stands, a score of 7 reflects a solid, novel contribution with some limitations regarding real-world demonstration.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Breaking Bad Tokens: Detoxification of LLMs Using Sparse Autoencoders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14536v1)
- **Authors**: Agam Goyal, Vedant Rathi, William Yeh, Yian Wang, Yuen Chen, Hari Sundaram
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are now ubiquitous in user-facing applications, yet they still generate undesirable toxic outputs, including profanity, vulgarity, and derogatory remarks. Although numerous detoxification methods exist, most apply broad, surface-level fixes and can therefore easily be circumvented by jailbreak attacks. In this paper we leverage sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to identify toxicity-related directions in the residual stream of models and perform targeted activation steering using the corresponding decoder vectors. We introduce three tiers of steering aggressiveness and evaluate them on GPT-2 Small and Gemma-2-2B, revealing trade-offs between toxicity reduction and language fluency. At stronger steering strengths, these causal interventions surpass competitive baselines in reducing toxicity by up to 20%, though fluency can degrade noticeably on GPT-2 Small depending on the aggressiveness. Crucially, standard NLP benchmark scores upon steering remain stable, indicating that the model's knowledge and general abilities are preserved. We further show that feature-splitting in wider SAEs hampers safety interventions, underscoring the importance of disentangled feature learning. Our findings highlight both the promise and the current limitations of SAE-based causal interventions for LLM detoxification, further suggesting practical guidelines for safer language-model deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Breaking Bad Tokens: Detoxification of LLMs Using Sparse Autoencoders":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates using Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) for detoxification of Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is to leverage SAEs to identify toxicity-related directions within the LLM's residual stream and then perform targeted activation steering using the corresponding decoder vectors to suppress toxic outputs.  The authors introduce three levels of steering aggressiveness (constant, conditional per-input, conditional per-token) and evaluate their approach on GPT-2 Small and Gemma-2-2B models. They find that SAE-based steering can effectively reduce toxicity, even outperforming existing detoxification baselines, particularly at higher steering strengths. However, this improvement sometimes comes at the cost of reduced fluency, depending on the model and SAE used. They also show that model capabilities, measured by standard NLP benchmarks, remain largely unaffected by the steering process.  Furthermore, the paper explores how feature splitting in wider SAEs can hinder safety interventions and suggest methods to mitigate this. The work highlights the potential and limitations of SAE-based causal interventions for LLM detoxification and offers practical guidelines for safer language model deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its comprehensive evaluation of SAEs for LLM detoxification. While SAEs have been used for mechanistic interpretability and steering abstract concepts, this work provides the first in-depth analysis of their effectiveness in mitigating real-world toxic outputs. The introduction of a tiered steering approach is another notable contribution, allowing for finer-grained control over the detoxification process. The analysis of feature splitting's impact on detoxification performance is also a novel finding.
*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical issue in LLM deployment: the generation of toxic outputs. By exploring interpretable methods like SAEs, it offers a more transparent and potentially controllable approach to detoxification compared to black-box techniques like preference tuning. The findings have practical implications for building safer LLM-based applications. The demonstration that model capabilities are largely preserved is crucial, as it alleviates concerns about overly aggressive detoxification damaging the LLM's general usefulness. Furthermore, the findings regarding feature splitting are important for researchers designing and training SAEs for safety-critical applications.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper conducts a thorough evaluation across multiple models, steering approaches, and evaluation metrics (toxicity, fluency, capability).
    *   **Practical Focus:** The work is grounded in real-world concerns about LLM safety and provides practical guidelines for using SAEs in deployed systems.
    *   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology, results, and limitations.
    *   **Identification of Challenges:** The paper doesn't shy away from acknowledging the challenges of using SAEs for detoxification, such as the fluency trade-off and feature splitting.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Scope:** The evaluation is primarily limited to GPT-2 Small and Gemma-2-2B. While these are useful models for research, it's important to acknowledge that results might not directly translate to larger, more modern LLMs.
    *   **Manual Feature Selection:** The reliance on manual feature selection (using top-k activations and Neuronpedia) is a potential bottleneck. Developing automated or more scalable methods for identifying toxicity-related features would be a significant improvement.
    *   **Narrow Definition of Toxicity:** The focus on profanity, vulgarity, and derogatory remarks, while important, doesn't capture the full spectrum of toxic language (e.g., hate speech, misinformation). This could lead to an incomplete assessment of the detoxification method's effectiveness.
    *   **Dependence on external fluency metric** The fluency of the generated text is checked by a GPT-4o-mini. This could lead to bias depending on the external checker.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   It can inspire more research into interpretable detoxification methods based on SAEs and related techniques.
    *   It can motivate the development of automated feature selection methods for SAE-based interventions.
    *   It can encourage the development of SAE training regimes that promote disentangled feature learning and mitigate feature splitting.
    *   The tiered steering approach could be adopted and refined in other detoxification strategies.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of LLM safety by providing a comprehensive evaluation of SAEs for detoxification. It demonstrates the potential of interpretable methods for mitigating toxic outputs while preserving model capabilities. The identification of key challenges, such as fluency trade-offs and feature splitting, is also valuable for guiding future research. The paper is clearly written, well-executed, and has practical implications for building safer LLM-based applications.

However, the score is not higher due to limitations in model scope (GPT-2 Small and Gemma-2-2B), the reliance on manual feature selection, and a somewhat narrow definition of toxicity.  While these limitations are acknowledged by the authors, they prevent the paper from being a truly groundbreaking contribution that fundamentally changes the field. The dependence on external fluency metrics can also be seen as a limitation. Overall, the work represents a valuable and well-executed step towards interpretable and controllable LLM detoxification.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can Large Language Models Really Recognize Your Name?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14549v1)
- **Authors**: Dzung Pham, Peter Kairouz, Niloofar Mireshghallah, Eugene Bagdasarian, Chau Minh Pham, Amir Houmansadr
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being used to protect sensitive user data. However, current LLM-based privacy solutions assume that these models can reliably detect personally identifiable information (PII), particularly named entities. In this paper, we challenge that assumption by revealing systematic failures in LLM-based privacy tasks. Specifically, we show that modern LLMs regularly overlook human names even in short text snippets due to ambiguous contexts, which cause the names to be misinterpreted or mishandled. We propose AMBENCH, a benchmark dataset of seemingly ambiguous human names, leveraging the name regularity bias phenomenon, embedded within concise text snippets along with benign prompt injections. Our experiments on modern LLMs tasked to detect PII as well as specialized tools show that recall of ambiguous names drops by 20--40% compared to more recognizable names. Furthermore, ambiguous human names are four times more likely to be ignored in supposedly privacy-preserving summaries generated by LLMs when benign prompt injections are present. These findings highlight the underexplored risks of relying solely on LLMs to safeguard user privacy and underscore the need for a more systematic investigation into their privacy failure modes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Can Large Language Models Really Recognize Your Name?" challenges the assumption that LLMs can reliably detect personally identifiable information (PII), specifically human names, in privacy-preserving applications. The authors demonstrate that modern LLMs regularly overlook human names due to ambiguous contexts, a phenomenon they call Name Regularity Bias (NRB). They introduce AMBENCH, a benchmark dataset of ambiguous human names embedded within concise text snippets, along with benign prompt injections (BPI). Experiments on modern LLMs and specialized tools show a significant drop in recall for ambiguous names compared to recognizable ones, with ambiguous names being more likely to be ignored in privacy-preserving summaries generated by LLMs, especially when BPI is present.  The authors argue this highlights the risks of solely relying on LLMs for privacy safeguards and call for systematic investigation into privacy failure modes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel perspective by challenging the common assumption of LLM reliability in PII detection, particularly focusing on ambiguous names and the influence of context. While name recognition is a well-studied area, the systematic exploration of LLM failures in handling ambiguous names and the impact of BPI is a valuable contribution. AMBENCH dataset is a notable asset that will be valuable to the community.
*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for privacy-preserving applications that rely on LLMs for tasks like data minimization, prompt rewriting, and anonymization. The paper highlights a critical vulnerability that could lead to unintended PII leakage and potential legal and ethical ramifications.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of LLM failures in detecting ambiguous names.
    *   **Well-Designed Benchmark:** AMBENCH appears to be a well-designed benchmark dataset specifically targeting the identified vulnerabilities.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments provide strong empirical evidence supporting the authors' claims, demonstrating the performance degradation of LLMs in handling ambiguous names and the influence of BPI.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper discusses practical implications for LLM-based privacy applications.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the focus on ambiguous names is valuable, the paper's scope could be expanded to explore other types of PII and contextual ambiguities.
    *   **Mitigation Strategies:** The paper primarily focuses on identifying the problem and doesn't explore potential mitigation strategies in detail. While acknowledging the need for further countermeasures, the lack of initial exploration of mitigation strategies weakens the paper's immediate actionable value.
    *   **Survey Population:** While including human in-the-loop results, the paper mentions the survey respondents are predominantly members of the author's institutions. This may introduce biases into the study.
    *   **Attacker Model:** The paper mentions an attack model enabled by inconsistent PII detection, but could describe it in more detail and explore its potential damage.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper can significantly influence the design and evaluation of LLM-based privacy solutions, encouraging researchers to consider the vulnerabilities associated with ambiguous contexts and BPI.  It also underscores the need for more robust evaluation methodologies and the development of more reliable PII detection techniques. The dataset, AMBENCH, could become a standard benchmark for evaluating LLM privacy safeguards.

**Justification:**

The paper's novelty stems from its targeted focus on a specific weakness of LLMs in a crucial privacy task. It provides empirical evidence of a significant performance degradation due to a specific set of factors (ambiguous names and BPI) that existing literature has largely ignored. While the scope is somewhat limited, the clarity of the problem definition, the quality of the AMBENCH dataset, and the significance of the implications for privacy-preserving applications justify a solid rating. The paper's lack of detailed exploration of mitigation strategies and the limited survey scope prevent it from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### KORGym: A Dynamic Game Platform for LLM Reasoning Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14552v1)
- **Authors**: Jiajun Shi, Jian Yang, Jiaheng Liu, Xingyuan Bu, Jiangjie Chen, Junting Zhou, Kaijing Ma, Zhoufutu Wen, Bingli Wang, Yancheng He, Liang Song, Hualei Zhu, Shilong Li, Xingjian Wang, Wei Zhang, Ruibin Yuan, Yifan Yao, Wenjun Yang, Yunli Wang, Siyuan Fang, Siyu Yuan, Qianyu He, Xiangru Tang, Yingshui Tan, Wangchunshu Zhou, Zhaoxiang Zhang, Zhoujun Li, Wenhao Huang, Ge Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) underscore the need for more comprehensive evaluation methods to accurately assess their reasoning capabilities. Existing benchmarks are often domain-specific and thus cannot fully capture an LLM's general reasoning potential. To address this limitation, we introduce the Knowledge Orthogonal Reasoning Gymnasium (KORGym), a dynamic evaluation platform inspired by KOR-Bench and Gymnasium. KORGym offers over fifty games in either textual or visual formats and supports interactive, multi-turn assessments with reinforcement learning scenarios. Using KORGym, we conduct extensive experiments on 19 LLMs and 8 VLMs, revealing consistent reasoning patterns within model families and demonstrating the superior performance of closed-source models. Further analysis examines the effects of modality, reasoning strategies, reinforcement learning techniques, and response length on model performance. We expect KORGym to become a valuable resource for advancing LLM reasoning research and developing evaluation methodologies suited to complex, interactive environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the KORGym paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KORGym, a novel dynamic game platform for evaluating the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs).  KORGym, inspired by KOR-Bench and Gymnasium, features over fifty diverse games in textual and visual formats. The platform supports interactive, multi-turn assessments and reinforcement learning scenarios, enabling a comprehensive assessment of LLMs' adaptability, strategic planning, and decision-making capabilities. The authors conduct extensive experiments on various LLMs and VLMs using KORGym, analyzing the effects of modality, reasoning strategies, reinforcement learning, and response length on model performance.  The paper claims consistent reasoning patterns within model families and superior performance from closed-source models. KORGym aims to advance LLM reasoning research and develop more sophisticated evaluation methodologies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** The paper addresses a genuine need for more robust LLM evaluation beyond existing domain-specific benchmarks. KORGym's focus on games offers a potentially more challenging and knowledge-orthogonal testing ground, reducing reliance on memorization. The range of game types, spanning mathematical, logical, spatial, strategic, and multimodal reasoning, is a significant strength.
*   **Dynamic and Interactive:** The inclusion of multi-turn interactions and reinforcement learning support differentiates KORGym from simpler, single-turn game-based benchmarks like LogicGame. This enables evaluation of long-term planning and adaptive reasoning abilities.
*   **Extensible Framework:** The modular design of KORGym, with its inference, game interaction, evaluation, and communication modules, makes it extensible and adaptable for future development and integration with new games and models.
*   **Extensive Empirical Analysis:** The paper presents a thorough empirical analysis, evaluating a wide range of LLMs and VLMs. The analysis of different factors affecting performance (modality, reasoning strategies, response length) provides valuable insights.
*   **Addresses Limitations of Existing Approaches:** The paper explicitly addresses the shortcomings of existing game-based evaluations, such as the single-turn nature of LogicGame, the uncontrolled variability in TextArena and SPINBench, and the generative capacity dependence and RL integration issues in gg-bench.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Game Design:** While the paper emphasizes the diversity of games, it's not entirely clear how many are genuinely novel versus adaptations of existing games. The paper would be stronger if it provided more detail about the originality and design principles behind the specific games and detailed how they are distinct from existing games.
*   **Knowledge Orthogonality Concerns:** The claim of knowledge orthogonality, inspired by KOR-Bench, requires further justification. While games are less likely to be directly encountered in pretraining data, models may still leverage general knowledge or patterns learned during pretraining, thus potentially confounding the assessment of intrinsic reasoning skills. More detail around the mechanisms used to ensure that games do not rely on known solutions would be welcome.
*   **Evaluation Metric Sensitivity:** While the Dimension Aggregated Mean method attempts to address issues with simple scoring, the paper acknowledges that the model may learn from existing strategies, and does not test for how the strategies discovered may rely on pre-existing strategies. The paper provides no analysis of whether specific prompts are sensitive to existing solution methods, and thus whether the scoring may be sensitive to prompt selection.
*   **Open Source Models Lag Behind:** The finding that open source models lag behind closed source models is not surprising given the known benefits to closed-source models from human feedback and reinforcement learning from human feedback. The paper should provide analysis on how strategies discovered by closed-source strategies affect open source models, and provide methods to improve the results of open source models on the task.
*   **Lack of Detailed Game Descriptions:** The brief game descriptions in Table 1 and Figure 3 make it difficult to fully grasp the complexity and reasoning demands of each game. More detailed game mechanics and scoring rules should be provided to promote reproducibility and enable further research.
*   **Reliance on GPT-4 for annotation:** The annotation of reasoning paradigms by GPT-4 introduces a degree of subjectivity and potential bias. It's unclear how the authors ensured the reliability and consistency of the annotations. Human validation or alternative annotation methods could strengthen this analysis.

**Significance:**

KORGym has the potential to become a valuable resource for the LLM research community. Its focus on dynamic, interactive game-based evaluation could lead to the development of more robust and generalizable reasoning abilities in LLMs. By providing a standardized platform and comprehensive evaluation metrics, KORGym could facilitate more meaningful comparisons between different models and approaches.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution by introducing a comprehensive and extensible game based platform to address weaknesses in existing LLM evaluation frameworks. However, the paper lacks details around novelty and the mechanisms around strategy orthogonality and prompt sensitivity, thus undermining some of the paper's significance.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Dynadiff: Single-stage Decoding of Images from Continuously Evolving fMRI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14556v1)
- **Authors**: Marlène Careil, Yohann Benchetrit, Jean-Rémi King
- **Abstract**: Brain-to-image decoding has been recently propelled by the progress in generative AI models and the availability of large ultra-high field functional Magnetic Resonance Imaging (fMRI). However, current approaches depend on complicated multi-stage pipelines and preprocessing steps that typically collapse the temporal dimension of brain recordings, thereby limiting time-resolved brain decoders. Here, we introduce Dynadiff (Dynamic Neural Activity Diffusion for Image Reconstruction), a new single-stage diffusion model designed for reconstructing images from dynamically evolving fMRI recordings. Our approach offers three main contributions. First, Dynadiff simplifies training as compared to existing approaches. Second, our model outperforms state-of-the-art models on time-resolved fMRI signals, especially on high-level semantic image reconstruction metrics, while remaining competitive on preprocessed fMRI data that collapse time. Third, this approach allows a precise characterization of the evolution of image representations in brain activity. Overall, this work lays the foundation for time-resolved brain-to-image decoding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Dynadiff, a novel single-stage diffusion model for reconstructing images from dynamically evolving fMRI recordings.  It aims to address limitations in existing brain-to-image decoding methods that rely on complex multi-stage pipelines and often collapse the temporal dimension of fMRI data. Dynadiff simplifies the training process, demonstrates improved performance on time-resolved fMRI signals (particularly on high-level semantic image reconstruction metrics), and offers a way to characterize the evolution of image representations within brain activity.  The model is trained end-to-end and evaluated on the Natural Scenes Dataset (NSD), demonstrating state-of-the-art results compared to existing methods like Brain-Diffusers, MindEye, and WAVE.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the single-stage end-to-end training approach for reconstructing images from time-resolved fMRI data.  Previous methods typically involve multiple, separately trained components and often collapse the temporal dimension through preprocessing. Dynadiff's ability to directly process the continuous stream of fMRI data is a significant departure and a substantial simplification. The characterization of image representation evolution within brain activity is also a valuable contribution.

**Significance:** The paper's significance rests on several factors:

*   **Improved performance:**  Achieving state-of-the-art results on time-resolved fMRI signals, specifically on high-level semantic image reconstruction metrics is meaningful progress.  Outperforming existing methods like MindEye2 is a strong indicator of the model's effectiveness.
*   **Simplified Training:** The single-stage training pipeline makes the approach more accessible and efficient compared to complex, multi-stage methods.
*   **Time-Resolved Analysis:** Preserving the temporal information in fMRI data allows for a more nuanced understanding of how visual representations evolve in the brain, opening new avenues for research in cognitive neuroscience.
*   **Foundation for Future Work:** As the paper states, it lays a foundation for future time-resolved brain-to-image decoding.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear problem statement and well-defined goals.
*   A novel and efficient architecture that overcomes the limitations of existing methods.
*   Strong empirical results demonstrating improved performance on time-resolved fMRI data.
*   Thorough evaluation and ablation studies to validate the model's components.
*   Qualitative results that support the quantitative findings.
*   The model is open-sourced for greater reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The reliance on NSD dataset. While NSD is the largest fMRI dataset, the paper acknowledges that the image distribution within NSD can be stereotypical.  Evaluating the model on other datasets would strengthen the claims of generalizability.
*   The fMRI preprocessing steps, while designed to preserve the time dimension, might be improved by incorporating a foundation model of brain activity (akin to pre-trained image encoders).
*   Computational cost, while the model simplifies training in stages, the total computational cost might still be substantial because it is a diffusion model. This should be explored in the paper.
*   While the focus on static image decoding is justified as a robust baseline, the natural next step to decoding videos is not yet addressed.

**Potential influence:** Dynadiff has the potential to significantly impact the field by:

*   Encouraging a shift towards time-resolved analysis of fMRI data.
*   Simplifying the development of brain-to-image decoding models.
*   Enabling new research into the dynamics of visual representation in the brain.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has some limitations, its novelty, strong performance, simplified training process, and potential impact on the field justify a high score. The end-to-end approach for time-resolved decoding is a significant advancement, and the empirical results are compelling. Although the reliance on the NSD dataset and some computational concerns are present, the overall contribution warrants recognition.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Can Pruning Improve Reasoning? Revisiting Long-CoT Compression with Capability in Mind for Better Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14582v1)
- **Authors**: Shangziqi Zhao, Jiahao Yuan, Guisong Yang, Usman Naseem
- **Abstract**: Long chain-of-thought (Long-CoT) reasoning improves accuracy in LLMs, yet its verbose, self-reflective style often hinders effective distillation into small language models (SLMs). We revisit Long-CoT compression through the lens of capability alignment and ask: Can pruning improve reasoning? We propose Prune-on-Logic, a structure-aware framework that transforms Long-CoT into logic graphs and selectively prunes low-utility reasoning steps under self-verification constraints. Through systematic analysis across three pruning strategies -- targeting entire chains, core reasoning, and verification -- we find that pruning verification steps yields consistent accuracy gains while reducing inference cost, outperforming token-level baselines and uncompressed fine-tuning. In contrast, pruning reasoning or all-chain steps degrades performance, revealing that small models benefit not from shorter CoTs, but from semantically leaner ones. Our findings highlight pruning as a structural optimization strategy for aligning CoT reasoning with SLM capacity.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of distilling Long Chain-of-Thought (Long-CoT) reasoning into smaller language models (SLMs). It proposes a novel framework called "Prune-on-Logic," which transforms Long-CoT sequences into logic graphs and selectively prunes low-utility reasoning steps based on self-verification. The paper systematically compares three pruning strategies: pruning the entire chain, pruning only reasoning steps, and pruning only verification steps. The key finding is that pruning verification steps consistently improves accuracy and reduces inference cost, while pruning other types of steps degrades performance. This suggests that small models benefit more from semantically leaner, rather than simply shorter, CoTs and highlights pruning as a structure-aware optimization strategy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its structure-aware approach to Long-CoT compression. Unlike token-level compression techniques that risk losing critical information, Prune-on-Logic operates on a logical graph representation, preserving the deductive structure of the reasoning process. The focus on capability alignment with SLMs, rather than just length reduction, is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for distilling complex reasoning abilities into smaller models. The insight that pruning verification steps can improve performance is counterintuitive and could lead to more efficient and effective distillation techniques. By demonstrating that SLMs can benefit from semantically leaner CoTs, the paper challenges the conventional wisdom that simply shortening the reasoning chain is the best approach.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of Long-CoT distillation and the limitations of existing approaches.
*   **Well-defined framework:** Prune-on-Logic is a well-defined and structured framework with a clear methodology for logic graph construction, importance scoring, and pruning.
*   **Systematic analysis:** The paper conducts a thorough and systematic comparison of different pruning strategies, providing valuable insights into their effectiveness.
*   **Counterintuitive findings:** The discovery that pruning verification steps can improve performance is a novel and interesting finding.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper evaluates the proposed framework on multiple datasets and SLMs, providing robust evidence for its effectiveness.
* **Focus on SLM Capability:** The paper asks whether pruning can improve reasoning ability, shifting focus beyond simply reducing length.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on High-Quality Logic Graphs:** The framework relies on the accurate construction of logic graphs, which may be challenging in noisy or unsupervised settings. The paper uses a LLM to segment and assign nodes to create Logic graphs, a process which might be prone to errors.
*   **Limited Scope of Pruning:** The paper only considers pruning nodes with exactly one predecessor and successor. While this simplifies the process, it may limit the framework's ability to identify and remove more complex redundancies.
*   **Dataset Focus:** The empirical evaluation is limited to mathematical and algorithmic reasoning datasets. It is not clear whether the findings would generalize to other types of reasoning tasks.
*   **Black-box LLM and SFT Model:** Use of a frozen pre-SFT model and LLM segmentation hinders full interpretability.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of LLM distillation by promoting structure-aware pruning techniques and shifting the focus towards capability alignment with SLMs. The finding that pruning verification steps can improve performance could lead to new distillation strategies that prioritize semantic density over length reduction.

**Justification for the score:**

This paper presents a novel and rigorous approach to Long-CoT compression, moving beyond simple token-level pruning towards a structure-aware method guided by logical analysis. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating that pruning, particularly of verification steps, can enhance the reasoning capabilities of SLMs while reducing inference costs. Despite the weaknesses outlined above, which include reliance on high-quality logic graphs and limitations in the scope of pruning, the significance of the findings and the potential impact on the field of LLM distillation warrant a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL

### Context Reasoner: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability for Contextualized Privacy and Safety Compliance via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14585v1)
- **Authors**: Wenbin Hu, Haoran Li, Huihao Jing, Qi Hu, Ziqian Zeng, Sirui Han, Heli Xu, Tianshu Chu, Peizhao Hu, Yangqiu Song
- **Abstract**: While Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit remarkable capabilities, they also introduce significant safety and privacy risks. Current mitigation strategies often fail to preserve contextual reasoning capabilities in risky scenarios. Instead, they rely heavily on sensitive pattern matching to protect LLMs, which limits the scope. Furthermore, they overlook established safety and privacy standards, leading to systemic risks for legal compliance. To address these gaps, we formulate safety and privacy issues into contextualized compliance problems following the Contextual Integrity (CI) theory. Under the CI framework, we align our model with three critical regulatory standards: GDPR, EU AI Act, and HIPAA. Specifically, we employ reinforcement learning (RL) with a rule-based reward to incentivize contextual reasoning capabilities while enhancing compliance with safety and privacy norms. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that our method not only significantly enhances legal compliance (achieving a +17.64% accuracy improvement in safety/privacy benchmarks) but also further improves general reasoning capability. For OpenThinker-7B, a strong reasoning model that significantly outperforms its base model Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct across diverse subjects, our method enhances its general reasoning capabilities, with +2.05% and +8.98% accuracy improvement on the MMLU and LegalBench benchmark, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to improve the safety and privacy compliance of Large Language Models (LLMs) by incorporating Contextual Integrity (CI) theory and reinforcement learning (RL). It addresses the limitations of current mitigation strategies that rely on sensitive pattern matching and overlook established safety standards. The authors formulate safety and privacy issues as contextualized compliance problems, aligning their model with GDPR, EU AI Act, and HIPAA. They use RL with a rule-based reward system to incentivize contextual reasoning capabilities while enhancing compliance with safety and privacy norms. The experiments demonstrate significant improvements in legal compliance and general reasoning ability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of several elements:
    *   *Contextual Integrity (CI) Theory Application:* While previous work has touched upon privacy within the context of LLMs, explicitly formalizing and leveraging the CI framework is a significant step towards a more structured approach. The idea of viewing safety/privacy as information flows governed by context-specific norms provides a valuable abstraction.
    *   *Alignment with Legal Frameworks:* Explicitly targeting and aligning LLMs with specific legal frameworks (GDPR, EU AI Act, HIPAA) is a valuable contribution. Most existing work treats safety/privacy as abstract concepts, whereas the paper is grounded in regulatory realities.
    *   *Rule-Based RL Reward System:* While RL for LLM alignment is not new, using a rule-based reward based on *legal compliance outcomes* seems novel and well-suited for this problem. The deterministic nature of legal rules allows for clear, verifiable rewards, aiding in training.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in the field of LLMs. As LLMs are increasingly deployed in sensitive applications, ensuring safety and privacy is paramount.
    *   *Addressing Limitations of Existing Methods:* The paper correctly identifies and addresses the limitations of current approaches: limited contextual reasoning and disregard for established standards.
    *   *Performance Gains:* The reported accuracy improvements (+17.64% on safety/privacy benchmarks) are significant and demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   *Generalization*: It's particularly significant that the approach enhances both legal compliance *and* general reasoning capabilities (MMLU and LegalBench improvements). This suggests that focusing on legal alignment doesn't degrade, and possibly improves, other reasoning tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear Problem Formulation: Defining safety and privacy within the CI framework provides a solid theoretical foundation.
    *   Rigorous Methodology: The use of RL with a carefully designed rule-based reward is appropriate and well-explained.
    *   Comprehensive Experiments: The experiments cover various benchmarks, model families, and ablation studies, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    *   Legal grounding: The paper’s approach is explicitly grounded in legal frameworks, which can help to bridge the gap between academic research and practical applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Complexity of Legal Frameworks: The paper acknowledges the challenges in dealing with the full complexity of legal documents. It seems that the experimental setup simplifies these laws to facilitate RL training. This simplification may limit the scalability and applicability of the approach to real-world scenarios.
    *   Limited Scope of Legal Frameworks:  The focus on GDPR, EU AI Act, and HIPAA, while valuable, might limit the applicability to other legal and cultural contexts.
    *   Reliance on DeepSeek-R1 for Distillation: The reliance on a specific model (DeepSeek-R1) for generating reasoning trajectories might introduce bias or limit the generalizability of the approach. The choice of distillation model should be discussed more critically.
    *   Potential for Adversarial Exploitation: While the paper significantly enhances model performance, it also acknowledges the possibility of adversarial exploitation of remaining failure cases. This vulnerability needs further investigation.
    *   Simplified reward function: The reward function is binary (+1 for correct, 0 for incorrect). More nuanced reward structures that consider the degree of compliance or the importance of certain legal rules could improve performance and robustness.
    *   Lack of generalisation details: The discussion of the generalisation capabilities of the proposed approach would be further improved by a discussion of whether it generalises across different legal compliance rulesets.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM alignment, particularly in safety and privacy.
