The combination of CI theory, legal frameworks, and RL could serve as a blueprint for developing more robust and trustworthy LLMs. The emphasis on contextual reasoning and legal compliance is likely to become increasingly important as LLMs are integrated into real-world applications.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM safety and privacy by integrating Contextual Integrity theory and reinforcement learning with a strong focus on legal compliance. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating substantial improvements in both legal compliance and general reasoning ability. The work addresses crucial limitations of existing methods and provides a solid framework for future research. However, the inherent complexity of dealing with legal regulations could hinder its direct transferability to complex real-world scenarios and more legal rulesets without significant work in each scenario.

The impact is limited by the relatively high level of effort necessary to instantiate the framework, rather than something easily applied out-of-the-box. But overall this is a very solid contribution that makes a notable advance in the area.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Toward Reliable Biomedical Hypothesis Generation: Evaluating Truthfulness and Hallucination in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14599v1)
- **Authors**: Guangzhi Xiong, Eric Xie, Corey Williams, Myles Kim, Amir Hassan Shariatmadari, Sikun Guo, Stefan Bekiranov, Aidong Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown significant potential in scientific disciplines such as biomedicine, particularly in hypothesis generation, where they can analyze vast literature, identify patterns, and suggest research directions. However, a key challenge lies in evaluating the truthfulness of generated hypotheses, as verifying their accuracy often requires substantial time and resources. Additionally, the hallucination problem in LLMs can lead to the generation of hypotheses that appear plausible but are ultimately incorrect, undermining their reliability. To facilitate the systematic study of these challenges, we introduce TruthHypo, a benchmark for assessing the capabilities of LLMs in generating truthful biomedical hypotheses, and KnowHD, a knowledge-based hallucination detector to evaluate how well hypotheses are grounded in existing knowledge. Our results show that LLMs struggle to generate truthful hypotheses. By analyzing hallucinations in reasoning steps, we demonstrate that the groundedness scores provided by KnowHD serve as an effective metric for filtering truthful hypotheses from the diverse outputs of LLMs. Human evaluations further validate the utility of KnowHD in identifying truthful hypotheses and accelerating scientific discovery. Our data and source code are available at https://github.com/Teddy-XiongGZ/TruthHypo.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TruthHypo, a benchmark for evaluating the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate truthful biomedical hypotheses. It also presents KnowHD, a knowledge-based hallucination detection framework designed to assess the groundedness of these hypotheses. The benchmark utilizes a curated dataset derived from PubTator 3.0, split into "seen" and "unseen" subsets to simulate temporal progression in scientific discovery. The paper analyzes the performance of various LLMs in different knowledge augmentation settings (parametric knowledge alone, with knowledge graphs, with literature, or with both).  The study investigates the correlation between hallucination (as detected by KnowHD) and the truthfulness of generated hypotheses, and also shows a method of using the KnowHD outputs to filter LLM outputs and therefore to increase the percentage of truthful results. The paper concludes that LLMs struggle to generate truthful hypotheses and demonstrates the effectiveness of KnowHD in identifying more grounded and potentially truthful outputs. Finally, the authors perform a human study to validate the usefulness of KnowHD.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the construction of the TruthHypo benchmark and KnowHD hallucination detector specifically tailored for the biomedical domain. While other benchmarks and methods exist for evaluating LLMs, this work focuses on the critical aspect of *truthfulness* in hypothesis generation, a relatively underexplored area. The separation of the dataset into "seen" and "unseen" components to simulate scientific discovery also adds a layer of realism and allows for the evaluation of LLMs' ability to generate *novel* and truthful hypotheses within a scientific context. KnowHD's approach to analyzing reasoning chains and evaluating groundedness offers a method for assessing truthfulness.

**Significance:** The significance of this work is high, particularly given the increasing interest in using LLMs for scientific discovery.  The paper addresses a critical limitation of using LLMs in biomedicine: the tendency to hallucinate. By providing a benchmark and a method for detecting hallucinations, the authors contribute to the development of more reliable and trustworthy LLM-based hypothesis generation systems. The demonstration that groundedness scores correlate with truthfulness and can be used to improve hypothesis selection is particularly valuable. By showing how to filter hallucinated outputs, the work improves scientific outcomes. The human study further validates the potential of KnowHD to accelerate scientific discovery.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Benchmark:** The TruthHypo benchmark is clearly constructed and utilizes a realistic dataset. The temporal splitting of the data and the inclusion of negative samples enhances its rigor.
*   **Targeted Hallucination Detection:** KnowHD provides a structured approach to identifying hallucinated claims within the reasoning processes of LLMs.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper performs thorough experiments comparing several LLMs under different knowledge augmentation settings.
*   **Validation with Human Evaluation:** The open-ended hypothesis generation task and human evaluation adds a layer of real-world relevance to the study.
*   **Method to filter outputs:** The paper contains a method by which a filtering system may take the diverse outputs of LLMs and return the most truthful of the lot.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Relations:** The benchmark focuses on only three relation types ("Chemical & Gene", "Disease & Gene", and "Gene & Gene"). While these are important, expanding to other types could provide a more comprehensive evaluation.
*   **Focus on Extraction over Generation:** A large portion of KnowHD is focused on extracting facts as opposed to generating them.
*   **Reliance on LLMs for Claim Decomposition:** The reliance on LLMs for decomposing hypotheses into atomic claims introduces a potential source of error, as LLMs can be unreliable in this task as well. Using rule-based methods may allow for better fact extraction.
*   **Generalizability of the Human study:** While the study validates the efficacy of their method, the sample size of 54 pairs is relatively small for a definitive conclusion regarding its usability.
*   **Lack of Baseline Comparison:** A larger emphasis could be placed on comparing KnowHD to past hallucination detection systems.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

I assign a score of 8 because the paper makes a significant contribution to the field by addressing the crucial issue of truthfulness in LLM-generated biomedical hypotheses.  The TruthHypo benchmark provides a valuable tool for evaluating LLMs, and the KnowHD framework offers a promising method for detecting hallucinations and improving the reliability of generated hypotheses. The study is well-designed and comprehensively evaluated, with human validation adding weight to the findings. While there are some limitations in terms of the scope of relations covered and reliance on LLMs for claim decomposition, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. It opens up important avenues for future research on building more trustworthy and effective LLM-based tools for scientific discovery.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### SATBench: Benchmarking LLMs' Logical Reasoning via Automated Puzzle Generation from SAT Formulas
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14615v1)
- **Authors**: Anjiang Wei, Yuheng Wu, Yingjia Wan, Tarun Suresh, Huanmi Tan, Zhanke Zhou, Sanmi Koyejo, Ke Wang, Alex Aiken
- **Abstract**: We introduce SATBench, a benchmark for evaluating the logical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) through logical puzzles derived from Boolean satisfiability (SAT) problems. Unlike prior work that focuses on inference rule-based reasoning, which often involves deducing conclusions from a set of premises, our approach leverages the search-based nature of SAT problems, where the objective is to find a solution that fulfills a specified set of logical constraints. Each instance in SATBench is generated from a SAT formula, then translated into a story context and conditions using LLMs. The generation process is fully automated and allows for adjustable difficulty by varying the number of clauses. All 2100 puzzles are validated through both LLM-assisted and solver-based consistency checks, with human validation on a subset. Experimental results show that even the strongest model, o4-mini, achieves only 65.0% accuracy on hard UNSAT problems, close to the random baseline of 50%. SATBench exposes fundamental limitations in the search-based logical reasoning abilities of current LLMs and provides a scalable testbed for future research in logical reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SATBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the logical reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  SATBench leverages Boolean Satisfiability (SAT) problems to generate logical puzzles.  Unlike existing benchmarks that focus on inference-rule based reasoning, SATBench uses the search-based nature of SAT problems, where LLMs must find a truth assignment fulfilling specified logical constraints. The puzzles are created through an automated process involving: 1) sampling SAT formulas, 2) using LLMs to translate these formulas into story contexts and natural language conditions, and 3) validating the generated puzzles using LLM-assisted and solver-based consistency checks. The difficulty of the puzzles is controlled by varying the number of clauses in the SAT formulas. The paper evaluates several LLMs on SATBench, finding that even the strongest models struggle with UNSAT problems, often performing close to random baselines.  The authors analyze the results, highlighting the limitations of current LLMs in search-based logical reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to benchmarking LLMs' logical reasoning by utilizing SAT problems. The concept of generating logical puzzles from SAT formulas is reasonably novel, particularly the use of LLMs in both the generation and validation processes. The focus on search-based reasoning tasks, as opposed to inference-based reasoning, distinguishes it from many existing benchmarks.  The automated generation and validation pipeline is also a significant contribution, enabling the creation of a scalable and customizable benchmark. However, leveraging SAT solvers to generate logic problems isn't entirely new.  The specific implementation of this within an LLM benchmark, combined with LLM-driven translation and validation, adds a significant degree of novelty.

* **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its ability to expose limitations in the logical reasoning abilities of LLMs. The findings that even strong models struggle with UNSAT problems and perform poorly on harder instances demonstrate a fundamental challenge in search-based reasoning for LLMs. This provides valuable insights for future research aimed at improving LLMs' reasoning capabilities. SATBench could serve as a valuable testbed for evaluating new techniques and architectures designed to enhance logical reasoning. The automated pipeline makes the benchmark scalable and adaptable, allowing for the creation of puzzles with varying levels of difficulty and complexity.  However, the analysis could benefit from a deeper dive into the types of errors that LLMs make, moving beyond simple accuracy metrics to pinpoint specific reasoning failures.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Automated and Scalable Generation:** The automated pipeline for puzzle generation is a significant strength, allowing for the creation of a large and customizable dataset.
    * **Rigorous Validation:** The combination of LLM-assisted and solver-based validation ensures the quality and consistency of the generated puzzles.  The human validation step further enhances the reliability of the benchmark.
    * **Focus on Search-Based Reasoning:** The focus on search-based reasoning provides a different perspective on LLMs' logical abilities compared to benchmarks based on inference rules.
    * **Clear Analysis:**  The paper provides a clear analysis of the results, highlighting the challenges faced by LLMs in tackling UNSAT problems and the impact of puzzle difficulty.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **LLM Reliance for Evaluation:** The "LLM-as-a-judge" approach, while practical, introduces potential biases and inaccuracies. While human validation is used, it is only on a subset of the data. A more detailed error analysis and comparison with human performance would strengthen the evaluation.
    * **Limited Error Analysis:** While the overall performance is analyzed, a deeper dive into the *types* of logical errors models are making would be valuable. Are they misunderstanding negations, failing to track constraints, or struggling with combinatorial search?
    * **Narrow Focus:** The benchmark is focused solely on Boolean satisfiability.  While this allows for precise control over difficulty and correctness, it may not fully capture the breadth of logical reasoning skills needed in real-world applications.
    * **Lack of comparison to humans:** Including a study involving humans solving these puzzles would offer a more substantial comparative analysis. The "challenge" is only contextualized via comparison to other LLMs, and random baselines.

* **Potential Influence:**  SATBench has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    * **Driving Research on LLM Reasoning:**  The benchmark can be used to evaluate new techniques for improving LLMs' logical reasoning capabilities, particularly in search-based tasks.
    * **Guiding Architecture Development:**  The insights gained from SATBench can inform the design of new LLM architectures that are better suited for logical reasoning.
    * **Promoting Dataset Creation:**  The automated generation pipeline can be adapted and extended to create new benchmarks for other types of logical reasoning problems.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM evaluation by introducing SATBench, a benchmark that focuses on search-based logical reasoning. The automated generation and validation pipeline is a key strength, enabling the creation of a scalable and customizable dataset. The analysis reveals limitations in current LLMs' abilities to tackle UNSAT problems, highlighting the need for further research. However, the reliance on LLMs for evaluation, the lack of detailed error analysis, and the narrow focus on Boolean satisfiability limit the overall impact. Including human performance would greatly improve the comparative analysis. Despite these limitations, SATBench offers a valuable testbed for future research and has the potential to influence the development of more robust and capable LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Linear Control of Test Awareness Reveals Differential Compliance in Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14617v1)
- **Authors**: Sahar Abdelnabi, Ahmed Salem
- **Abstract**: Reasoning-focused large language models (LLMs) sometimes alter their behavior when they detect that they are being evaluated, an effect analogous to the Hawthorne phenomenon, which can lead them to optimize for test-passing performance or to comply more readily with harmful prompts if real-world consequences appear absent. We present the first quantitative study of how such "test awareness" impacts model behavior, particularly its safety alignment. We introduce a white-box probing framework that (i) linearly identifies awareness-related activations and (ii) steers models toward or away from test awareness while monitoring downstream performance. We apply our method to different state-of-the-art open-source reasoning LLMs across both realistic and hypothetical tasks. Our results demonstrate that test awareness significantly impact safety alignment, and is different for different models. By providing fine-grained control over this latent effect, our work aims to increase trust in how we perform safety evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Linear Control of Test Awareness Reveals Differential Compliance in Reasoning Models":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how reasoning-focused large language models (LLMs) alter their behavior when they perceive they are being evaluated, a phenomenon they term "test awareness." The authors introduce a white-box probing framework that: (i) linearly identifies awareness-related activations, and (ii) steers models toward or away from test awareness while monitoring downstream performance. They apply this method to various open-source reasoning LLMs across realistic and hypothetical tasks. The results indicate that test awareness significantly impacts safety alignment and varies between different models. By providing control over this latent effect, the work aims to increase trust in safety evaluation.  The key findings are that hypothetical tasks are more prone to elicit test awareness which influences harmful task compliance. Furthermore steering away from test awareness does not necessarily guarantee better alignment, and may even increase harmful task completion.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of "test awareness" in LLMs, analogous to the Hawthorne effect, is not entirely new. However, this paper provides a novel **quantitative** study of this phenomenon, particularly focusing on safety alignment. The white-box probing framework is a significant contribution, offering a method to identify and manipulate awareness-related activations, providing an improved explainability compared to prior work. The key lies in understanding if such probing is useful and more effective that directly optimizing reasoning traces, as alluded to in the paper. The work seems to focus on parameter editing while it could more directly impact generated reasoning traces.

*   **Significance:** Understanding and mitigating test awareness is crucial for building reliable and trustworthy LLMs, especially regarding safety alignment. This paper's findings have important implications for benchmarking, evaluating, and deploying LLMs in real-world scenarios.  It highlights that standard benchmarks may not accurately reflect a model's true behavior due to this latent effect. The finding that steering away from test awareness doesn't necessarily improve safety and can even increase harmful compliance is particularly important, pointing to the complexity of these interactions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined framework:** The paper presents a clear and well-executed methodology, including the synthetic dataset creation, probe training, and steering techniques.
    *   **Quantitative analysis:** The paper provides quantitative results demonstrating the impact of test awareness on safety alignment across different models and tasks.
    *   **Focus on open-source models:** The use of open-source models allows for white-box probing and enables further research and validation of the findings.
    *   **Practical implications:** The findings have direct implications for developing more robust and reliable safety evaluations.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Dataset Limitation:** While the synthetic dataset is a good starting point, it may not fully capture the complexity of real-world interactions and test scenarios, limiting the generalization of the result.
    *   **LLM Judge Reliability**: The reliance on an LLM-based judge for annotation introduces potential biases and inaccuracies, although this is partially mitigated by the explicit instruction and three-point scale annotation.
    *   **Limited scope**: The action evaluation dataset used might not reflect the variety and dynamics of complex, multi-turn interactions with LLMs, which are relevant in more realistic deployment scenarios. Also, the models could be sensitive to "test" scenarios and it would be good to understand how these results generalize to adversarial prompting that leads to "jailbreaking"
    *   **Interpretability of Representations:** While the paper identifies "awareness neurons," the interpretability of these activations remains limited. It would be beneficial to further understand what specific aspects of the model's internal representations are being modulated.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence the field by raising awareness of the importance of test awareness in LLMs and providing a concrete framework for studying and mitigating it. It could inspire further research on developing more robust and reliable safety evaluations.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field of LLM safety. The development of the white-box probing framework and the quantitative demonstration of test awareness's impact are valuable. However, the limitations of the synthetic dataset and the dependence on an LLM judge temper the overall impact. While the concept of test awareness is not entirely novel, the thoroughness of this investigation warrants a higher score. Future work should address the limitations of synthetic tasks and potentially use human in the loop evaluation.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing Learned Knowledge in LoRA Adapters Through Efficient Contrastive Decoding on Ascend NPUs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14620v1)
- **Authors**: Morgan Lindsay Heisler, Linzi Xing, Ge Shi, Hanieh Sadri, Gursimran Singh, Weiwei Zhang, Tao Ye, Ying Xiong, Yong Zhang, Zhenan Fan
- **Abstract**: Huawei Cloud users leverage LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) as an efficient and scalable method to fine-tune and customize large language models (LLMs) for application-specific needs. However, tasks that require complex reasoning or deep contextual understanding are often hindered by biases or interference from the base model when using typical decoding methods like greedy or beam search. These biases can lead to generic or task-agnostic responses from the base model instead of leveraging the LoRA-specific adaptations. In this paper, we introduce Contrastive LoRA Decoding (CoLD), a novel decoding framework designed to maximize the use of task-specific knowledge in LoRA-adapted models, resulting in better downstream performance. CoLD uses contrastive decoding by scoring candidate tokens based on the divergence between the probability distributions of a LoRA-adapted expert model and the corresponding base model. This approach prioritizes tokens that better align with the LoRA's learned representations, enhancing performance for specialized tasks. While effective, a naive implementation of CoLD is computationally expensive because each decoding step requires evaluating multiple token candidates across both models. To address this, we developed an optimized kernel for Huawei's Ascend NPU. CoLD achieves up to a 5.54% increase in task accuracy while reducing end-to-end latency by 28% compared to greedy decoding. This work provides practical and efficient decoding strategies for fine-tuned LLMs in resource-constrained environments and has broad implications for applied data science in both cloud and on-premises settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Contrastive LoRA Decoding (CoLD), a novel decoding framework designed to improve the performance of LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation)-adapted large language models (LLMs) on complex reasoning tasks. CoLD leverages contrastive decoding principles, using the LoRA-adapted model as an "expert" and the base model as an "amateur," scoring candidate tokens based on the divergence between their probability distributions. To address the computational overhead of contrastive decoding, the authors developed an optimized kernel for Huawei's Ascend NPUs, enabling efficient execution.  The results demonstrate that CoLD improves task accuracy (up to 5.54% on GSM8K) and reduces end-to-end latency compared to greedy decoding on these NPUs. The authors also highlight the practical implications of their work for resource-constrained environments and its successful integration into a public-facing feature on Huawei Cloud.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the *application* of contrastive decoding to LoRA-adapted models and the *optimization* of this process specifically for Ascend NPUs. While contrastive decoding itself isn't new, the authors tailor it to the specific challenges and opportunities presented by LoRA and the Ascend architecture.  The kernel optimization for NPUs is a significant contribution, given the lack of readily available tools equivalent to those for GPUs. The idea of formulating the base model as the amateur and LoRA adapted version as an expert, with specific prompting strategy, can also be considered an incremental improvement over standard contrastive decoding.
* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a real-world problem: the limitations of LoRA-adapted models on complex reasoning tasks due to decoding inefficiencies. The proposed CoLD framework improves accuracy and reduces latency, making LoRA more practical for applications in resource-constrained environments (e.g., Huawei Cloud). The Ascend NPU optimization directly tackles a significant obstacle to deployment in Huawei's ecosystem. This will have direct impact on Huawei Cloud and other industrial cloud settings.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Practical focus:** The paper is very practically oriented, addressing the challenges of deploying LLMs in real-world industrial settings.
    * **System-level optimization:** The work considers the entire pipeline, from decoding algorithm to hardware acceleration, resulting in tangible performance improvements.
    * **Empirical validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental results on relevant datasets (GSM8K, CSQA, ViGGO), including ablation studies and efficiency analysis.
    * **Clear writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, clearly explaining the methodology and results.
    * **Address significant challenge:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in current use case of LLMs. It highlights the limitations of standard decoding strategies with LoRA-adapted models and demonstrates how its proposed method improves performance on complex reasoning tasks.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Advance:** While the integration of the kernel in the cloud systems is an advance, it is more of an engineering novelty. It is more of an engineering problem to solve than theoretical research, therefore making the theoretical advance limited.
    * **Limited Generalizability:** The NPU-specific optimizations limit the generalizability of the work to other hardware architectures (although the CoLD framework itself is applicable). Future work could consider cross-platform optimization strategies.
    * **Modest Improvements:** While the 5.54% accuracy improvement on GSM8K is noteworthy, it is somewhat modest. Other improvements, specially in areas like Data-to-text generation, are almost negligible. The paper would benefit from an extended analysis comparing different decoding approaches to different problems.
    * **Dataset Choice:** While GSM8K, CSQA and Viggo are standard datasets, they might not fully capture the complexity of real-world industry use cases that Huawei Cloud customers are seeing. More relevant industry-specific datasets would strengthen the validation.

**Justification for Score:**

Given that the paper provides an incremental step forward by applying a known decoding technique in an innovative way, its significance can be considered limited to the industrial settings. Considering the strengths (practical focus, system-level optimization, empirical validation) and weaknesses ( limited generalizability, modest improvements, limited novelty), a score of **6** is appropriate.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### TinyV: Reducing False Negatives in Verification Improves RL for LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14625v1)
- **Authors**: Zhangchen Xu, Yuetai Li, Fengqing Jiang, Bhaskar Ramasubramanian, Luyao Niu, Bill Yuchen Lin, Radha Poovendran
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has become a powerful tool for enhancing the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs) by optimizing their policies with reward signals. Yet, RL's success relies on the reliability of rewards, which are provided by verifiers. In this paper, we expose and analyze a widespread problem--false negatives--where verifiers wrongly reject correct model outputs. Our in-depth study of the Big-Math-RL-Verified dataset reveals that over 38% of model-generated responses suffer from false negatives, where the verifier fails to recognize correct answers. We show, both empirically and theoretically, that these false negatives severely impair RL training by depriving the model of informative gradient signals and slowing convergence. To mitigate this, we propose tinyV, a lightweight LLM-based verifier that augments existing rule-based methods, which dynamically identifies potential false negatives and recovers valid responses to produce more accurate reward estimates. Across multiple math-reasoning benchmarks, integrating TinyV boosts pass rates by up to 10% and accelerates convergence relative to the baseline. Our findings highlight the critical importance of addressing verifier false negatives and offer a practical approach to improve RL-based fine-tuning of LLMs. Our code is available at https://github.com/uw-nsl/TinyV.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TinyV: Reducing False Negatives in Verification Improves RL for LLM Reasoning."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a significant issue in Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Large Language Models (LLMs): the problem of false negatives in answer verification. Rule-based verifiers, while common, often fail to recognize correct answers due to formatting inconsistencies, natural language variations, or notational differences.  The authors quantify this problem, showing a high prevalence of false negatives in a math reasoning dataset. They then demonstrate, both empirically and theoretically, that these false negatives impair RL training by reducing informative gradient signals and slowing convergence. To mitigate this, they introduce TinyV, a lightweight LLM-based verifier that augments existing rule-based verifiers by identifying and correcting potential false negatives.  Experiments across multiple math-reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that integrating TinyV boosts pass rates and accelerates convergence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several areas. First, it provides a systematic analysis and quantification of the false negative problem in the context of RL for LLM reasoning, which is a largely overlooked area. Second, the proposal of TinyV as a lightweight, LLM-augmented verifier is a practical and effective approach to addressing this issue. While LLMs have been used as judges in other contexts, the specific application to identifying and correcting false negatives in RL training is novel. The careful curation of real and synthetic data for TinyV is also a significant contribution. The idea to build a taxonomy of the types of errors also adds to the novelty, helping categorize verification errors.
*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for the field of RL for LLMs. The findings demonstrate the critical importance of reliable reward signals and highlight a major limitation of relying solely on rule-based verifiers. By addressing this issue, the paper offers a practical solution to improve the effectiveness of RL training for reasoning tasks. This can lead to more robust, generalizable LLMs with improved reasoning abilities. The introduced *HardVerify-Math Bench* is a valuable contribution to the community since it addresses a gap in existing benchmarks. The findings around the effect of using easy to verify data also opens avenues for further research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Analysis:** The paper provides a thorough analysis of the false negative problem, including empirical quantification, a taxonomy of error types, and a theoretical analysis of its impact on RL training.
    *   **Practical Solution:** TinyV is a practical and computationally efficient solution that can be easily integrated with existing RL frameworks.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of TinyV across multiple benchmarks, showing significant improvements in pass rates and convergence.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide code, detailed experimental setups and data curation process promoting reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the paper focuses on math reasoning tasks, it would be interesting to explore the prevalence and impact of false negatives in other RL domains for LLMs, such as code generation or robotics.
    *   **TinyV's Dependency:** TinyV still relies on Prime Verifier and its extraction mechanisms (within `\boxed{}`). It might be interesting to explore a more robust system that also considers reasoning processes, to allow more complex and abstract answers.
    *   **LLM Annotation Validation:** While the paper manually validates a portion of the LLM annotations, expanding this validation would further strengthen the confidence in the accuracy of the annotated dataset.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research in RL for LLMs by raising awareness of the false negative problem and promoting the use of more reliable verification methods. It offers a practical approach to improving reward accuracy and training efficiency, which can lead to more powerful and generalizable LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a strong contribution to the field of RL for LLMs by identifying and addressing a significant, yet often overlooked, issue. The analysis is rigorous, the proposed solution is practical, and the experimental results are compelling. While there are some limitations in scope and potential avenues for further research, the paper's novelty and significance warrant a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Debating for Better Reasoning: An Unsupervised Multimodal Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14627v1)
- **Authors**: Ashutosh Adhikari, Mirella Lapata
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) gain expertise across diverse domains and modalities, scalable oversight becomes increasingly challenging, particularly when their capabilities may surpass human evaluators. Debate has emerged as a promising mechanism for enabling such oversight. In this work, we extend the debate paradigm to a multimodal setting, exploring its potential for weaker models to supervise and enhance the performance of stronger models. We focus on visual question answering (VQA), where two "sighted" expert vision-language models debate an answer, while a "blind" (text-only) judge adjudicates based solely on the quality of the arguments. In our framework, the experts defend only answers aligned with their beliefs, thereby obviating the need for explicit role-playing and concentrating the debate on instances of expert disagreement. Experiments on several multimodal tasks demonstrate that the debate framework consistently outperforms individual expert models. Moreover, judgments from weaker LLMs can help instill reasoning capabilities in vision-language models through finetuning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Debating for Better Reasoning: An Unsupervised Multimodal Approach" extends the debate paradigm, previously used in text-only language models, to a multimodal visual question answering (VQA) setting.  The approach involves two "sighted" expert vision-language models debating the answer to a question about an image, while a "blind" (text-only) judge adjudicates based solely on the quality of their arguments. A key innovation is that experts only defend answers they believe to be true, removing the need for artificial role-playing and focusing the debate on instances of disagreement. The judgments from the blind judge are then used to fine-tune the expert models, potentially improving their reasoning capabilities. Experiments on multimodal datasets show that the debate framework consistently outperforms individual expert models and a simpler "consultancy" protocol.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel application of the debate paradigm to a multimodal context, specifically VQA.  While debate has been explored in text-based settings, the extension to vision-language models and the use of image descriptions to ground arguments is new. The paper’s shift towards "belief-aligned" debating, as opposed to assigned roles, simplifies the setup and potentially improves the quality of arguments. This aspect brings a distinct focus on disagreement resolution within models.
*   **Significance:** The paper tackles the important problem of scalable oversight for large language models. As models become more capable, ensuring their alignment with human values and truthfulness becomes increasingly difficult. The debate framework offers a potential solution by leveraging interactions between models to improve reasoning and identify errors, even when human supervision is limited. The experimental results, showing improvements in VQA accuracy and the ability to extract reasoning traces, are encouraging. The use of these traces to finetune expert models adds further value, demonstrating a practical application of the debate paradigm. The study also highlights differences in model pairings and how the "blind" judge’s decision-making impacts performance, offering insights into the dynamics of model interactions.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The extension of the debate paradigm to multimodal VQA is a novel and potentially useful contribution.
    *   The "belief-aligned" debating approach is a practical simplification.
    *   The experiments demonstrate consistent improvements over baseline models and the consultancy approach.
    *   The paper identifies useful reasoning traces that can be used for fine-tuning.
    *   The study systematically investigates how different model pairings and the judge’s decision-making impact performance.
    *   The out-of-domain evaluation provides valuable insights.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The framework relies on the assumption that the weaker (text-only) judge is capable of evaluating the quality of arguments made by stronger (vision-language) experts. While the authors use a strong language model for judging, there's a potential limitation in the judge's ability to fully understand the nuances of vision-grounded reasoning without visual input.
    *   The number of rounds in the debate is limited by context length. More rounds might lead to better reasoning, but the paper does not fully explore this tradeoff.
    *   The reliance on automatically generated image descriptions may introduce inaccuracies or biases. The impact of the quality of these descriptions on the overall performance of the debate framework is not thoroughly investigated.
    *   The specific prompts used for the debaters and judge are crucial to the success of the framework, but their design process is not fully transparent. A more detailed analysis of the prompt engineering process would be helpful.
    *   The paper does not address potential adversarial attacks on the debate framework. Experts could potentially manipulate the judge with deceptive or misleading arguments.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research on scalable oversight, multimodal reasoning, and model alignment. The idea of using weaker models to supervise stronger ones is an interesting avenue for exploration. The techniques for extracting reasoning traces could also be valuable for improving the explainability and interpretability of vision-language models.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution by successfully extending the debate paradigm to the multimodal VQA domain. The "belief-aligned" debate strategy is a significant improvement, streamlining the process and emphasizing genuine disagreement resolution. The experimental results, showing consistent performance gains and the ability to extract reasoning traces, are promising and warrant further investigation. However, the study is not without its limitations. The reliance on a text-only judge, context length limitations, and the potential for inaccuracies in automatically generated image descriptions raise concerns that must be addressed in future research. While the paper presents a solid initial exploration, the long-term impact will depend on how well the proposed framework can be scaled, made more robust to adversarial attacks, and refined. This makes the significance important yet not earth-shattering, balancing promising innovation with existing limitations.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### KERL: Knowledge-Enhanced Personalized Recipe Recommendation using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14629v1)
- **Authors**: Fnu Mohbat, Mohammed J Zaki
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) and the abundance of food data have resulted in studies to improve food understanding using LLMs. Despite several recommendation systems utilizing LLMs and Knowledge Graphs (KGs), there has been limited research on integrating food related KGs with LLMs. We introduce KERL, a unified system that leverages food KGs and LLMs to provide personalized food recommendations and generates recipes with associated micro-nutritional information. Given a natural language question, KERL extracts entities, retrieves subgraphs from the KG, which are then fed into the LLM as context to select the recipes that satisfy the constraints. Next, our system generates the cooking steps and nutritional information for each recipe. To evaluate our approach, we also develop a benchmark dataset by curating recipe related questions, combined with constraints and personal preferences. Through extensive experiments, we show that our proposed KG-augmented LLM significantly outperforms existing approaches, offering a complete and coherent solution for food recommendation, recipe generation, and nutritional analysis. Our code and benchmark datasets are publicly available at https://github.com/mohbattharani/KERL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KERL, a knowledge-enhanced personalized recipe recommendation system. It leverages food knowledge graphs (FoodKG) and large language models (LLMs) to provide personalized food recommendations, generate cooking steps, and provide micro-nutritional information.  The system takes a natural language query with user preferences and constraints, extracts entities, retrieves subgraphs from the FoodKG, and feeds them into an LLM (Phi-3-mini) for recipe selection.  Separate LoRA-tuned adapters generate cooking instructions and nutritional information.  The paper also presents two newly curated benchmark datasets for evaluating food recommendation and nutritional analysis tasks.  Experiments demonstrate that KERL outperforms existing approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a reasonably novel contribution by unifying food recommendation, recipe generation, and nutrition estimation within a single KG and LLM-based framework. While previous work has explored these aspects individually or in pairs, the integration of all three, with a focus on personalized and constrained queries, represents a valuable advancement. The use of LoRA adapters for each module is also a practical engineering approach. The curation of the benchmark datasets is a positive aspect of the work, contributing to the community's resources for evaluating similar systems.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from the potential to provide more comprehensive and helpful food recommendations that consider health constraints and user preferences.  The system's ability to generate recipes and nutritional details can empower users to make informed food choices. The benchmark datasets could facilitate further research and development in the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Unified Framework:** Integrating recommendation, recipe generation, and nutrition estimation.
    *   **KG Augmentation:**  Effectively using a knowledge graph to enhance LLM reasoning.
    *   **Personalization:**  Incorporating user preferences and dietary constraints.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** Uses computationally efficient LoRA-based fine-tuning.
    *   **Benchmark Datasets:**  Provides valuable resources for evaluation.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** Demonstrates clear improvements over baselines.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on FoodKG:** The system's performance depends heavily on the completeness and accuracy of the FoodKG. Missing or incorrect information in the KG can lead to poor recommendations. As acknowledged by the authors, it can also entirely fail if the knowledge graph does not contain a relevant recipe that meets the constraints.
    *   **Nuance in Preference Expression:** The natural language query processing might struggle with highly complex or nuanced user preferences.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:**  The evaluation, while thorough in terms of metrics, could be enhanced by human evaluation to assess the quality and coherence of the generated recipes and nutritional information. More qualitative analysis of the types of errors and failures would be beneficial.
    *   **Choice of LLM:** While Phi-3-mini is a strong choice, it is a relatively smaller model. Scaling up the model sizes may be possible to improve performance further, and future work should consider its performance with other open-source LLMs.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper's impact could be significant in the areas of personalized nutrition, health management, and food computing.  The KERL system has the potential to be a valuable tool for individuals seeking to improve their diet and health. The benchmark datasets could encourage further research in this area.

*   **Novelty Score Rationale:** While the individual components (LLMs, KGs, LoRA) are not entirely new, the combination of these elements into a unified food recommendation system with personalized constraints, recipe generation, and nutritional analysis is a clear advancement. The curation of new datasets also adds to the novelty.

**Score: 7**

The paper presents a strong, unified system with solid experimental results and the valuable contribution of benchmark datasets. However, the reliance on the underlying knowledge graph and potential limitations in handling complex user preferences temper the overall score. Future work addressing these weaknesses could significantly increase the impact and novelty of this research.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Think Only When You Need with Large Hybrid-Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14631v1)
- **Authors**: Lingjie Jiang, Xun Wu, Shaohan Huang, Qingxiu Dong, Zewen Chi, Li Dong, Xingxing Zhang, Tengchao Lv, Lei Cui, Furu Wei
- **Abstract**: Recent Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have shown substantially improved reasoning capabilities over traditional Large Language Models (LLMs) by incorporating extended thinking processes prior to producing final responses. However, excessively lengthy thinking introduces substantial overhead in terms of token consumption and latency, which is particularly unnecessary for simple queries. In this work, we introduce Large Hybrid-Reasoning Models (LHRMs), the first kind of model capable of adaptively determining whether to perform thinking based on the contextual information of user queries. To achieve this, we propose a two-stage training pipeline comprising Hybrid Fine-Tuning (HFT) as a cold start, followed by online reinforcement learning with the proposed Hybrid Group Policy Optimization (HGPO) to implicitly learn to select the appropriate thinking mode. Furthermore, we introduce a metric called Hybrid Accuracy to quantitatively assess the model's capability for hybrid thinking. Extensive experimental results show that LHRMs can adaptively perform hybrid thinking on queries of varying difficulty and type. It outperforms existing LRMs and LLMs in reasoning and general capabilities while significantly improving efficiency. Together, our work advocates for a reconsideration of the appropriate use of extended thinking processes and provides a solid starting point for building hybrid thinking systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Think Only When You Need with Large Hybrid-Reasoning Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Large Hybrid-Reasoning Models (LHRMs), a novel class of models that can adaptively determine whether to engage in extended reasoning processes based on the contextual information of user queries.  This addresses the "overthinking" problem in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), where excessive computation is expended on simple queries. The authors propose a two-stage training pipeline: Hybrid Fine-Tuning (HFT) for a cold start, followed by Hybrid Group Policy Optimization (HGPO), a reinforcement learning approach to learn when to activate reasoning.  They introduce a "Hybrid Accuracy" metric (HAcc) to evaluate the model's ability to choose the appropriate reasoning mode.  Experiments across various model scales (Qwen-2.5 series) and domains demonstrate that LHRMs can adaptively perform hybrid thinking, outperforming existing LRMs and LLMs while improving efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of adaptively selecting reasoning modes based on query complexity is a valuable contribution.  While test-time compute allocation and adaptive computation have been explored in other contexts, its application to the LRM space, with a specific focus on mitigating overthinking and improving efficiency, is novel. The HFT+HGPO training pipeline and the Hybrid Accuracy metric are also original contributions specifically tailored to this problem.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in the deployment of large reasoning models.  Overthinking not only wastes resources but also degrades the user experience due to increased latency.  By enabling LHRMs to reason only when necessary, the paper opens the door to more practical and efficient application of these models.  The improvements in efficiency (token consumption and latency) could be crucial for wider adoption.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the "overthinking" issue.
    *   **Well-Designed Approach:** The HFT and HGPO training pipeline is thoughtfully designed to address the problem, providing a structured way to learn the hybrid reasoning behavior.
    *   **Quantitative Evaluation:**  The Hybrid Accuracy metric provides a quantitative way to assess the model's hybrid thinking capabilities, supplementing traditional downstream task performance measures. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of LHRMs.
    *   **Generalization:** The results showing the model's ability to generalize the learned hybrid thinking patterns across different domains is a significant strength.
    *   **Thorough experimentation:** Experiments are thorough, spanning different model sizes and ablations.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Reward Model:**  The HGPO algorithm relies on a reward model (here, Llama-3.1-Tulu-3-8B-RM).  The performance of the LHRM is inherently tied to the quality and reliability of the reward model. The paper does not fully explore the sensitivity of LHRMs to variations in reward model quality or potential biases within the reward model. It would be beneficial to explore different reward models and analyze their impact on the final performance.
    *   **Scalability of HGPO:** While the experiments cover models up to 7B parameters, the scalability of the HGPO training process to much larger models remains an open question. The computational cost of sampling and evaluating multiple responses for each query could become prohibitive for extremely large models.
    *   **Limited User Studies:** While the authors mention a improved user experience, explicit user studies demonstrating reduced latency or improved user satisfaction are missing.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a solid foundation for future research in hybrid reasoning systems. The idea of adaptively choosing computation based on query complexity could be extended to other areas of NLP and AI. The HFT+HGPO pipeline could serve as a starting point for building more efficient and user-friendly LRMs.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable and novel contribution to the field of large reasoning models by addressing the "overthinking" problem and introducing a practical approach to mitigate it. The experimental results and the proposed HAcc metric provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of LHRMs. The main weakness is the dependency to a reward model. The lack of user-centric evaluation is another concern. Nevertheless, given the significance of the problem, the novelty of the approach, and the solid experimental results, the paper warrants a reasonably high score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### General-Reasoner: Advancing LLM Reasoning Across All Domains
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14652v1)
- **Authors**: Xueguang Ma, Qian Liu, Dongfu Jiang, Ge Zhang, Zejun Ma, Wenhu Chen
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has recently demonstrated strong potential in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Particularly, the "Zero" reinforcement learning introduced by Deepseek-R1-Zero, enables direct RL training of base LLMs without relying on an intermediate supervised fine-tuning stage. Despite these advancements, current works for LLM reasoning mainly focus on mathematical and coding domains, largely due to data abundance and the ease of answer verification. This limits the applicability and generalization of such models to broader domains, where questions often have diverse answer representations, and data is more scarce. In this paper, we propose General-Reasoner, a novel training paradigm designed to enhance LLM reasoning capabilities across diverse domains. Our key contributions include: (1) constructing a large-scale, high-quality dataset of questions with verifiable answers curated by web crawling, covering a wide range of disciplines; and (2) developing a generative model-based answer verifier, which replaces traditional rule-based verification with the capability of chain-of-thought and context-awareness. We train a series of models and evaluate them on a wide range of datasets covering wide domains like physics, chemistry, finance, electronics etc. Our comprehensive evaluation across these 12 benchmarks (e.g. MMLU-Pro, GPQA, SuperGPQA, TheoremQA, BBEH and MATH AMC) demonstrates that General-Reasoner outperforms existing baseline methods, achieving robust and generalizable reasoning performance while maintaining superior effectiveness in mathematical reasoning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "General-Reasoner: Advancing LLM Reasoning Across All Domains":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GENERAL-REASONER, a new training paradigm for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) across a wider range of domains than is typically seen. Current approaches are largely focused on mathematics and coding due to data availability and ease of answer verification. The authors address this limitation by:

1.  Constructing a large-scale, high-quality dataset of questions with verifiable answers curated from the web, covering various disciplines.
2.  Developing a generative model-based answer verifier, which replaces traditional rule-based verification with a chain-of-thought and context-aware verification process.

The authors train a series of models using Zero-RL, meaning reinforcement learning directly from the base LLM, without supervised fine-tuning. They evaluate their models on a variety of datasets covering areas like physics, chemistry, finance, electronics, and several math benchmarks. The results show that GENERAL-REASONER outperforms existing baseline methods while maintaining performance on mathematical reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the combination of two key elements: 1) the creation of a large-scale, diverse, and *verifiable* question-answering dataset, and 2) the use of a model-based verifier instead of traditional rule-based verifiers for RL. While Zero-RL has been explored before, its application to general reasoning across diverse domains is a valuable step. The idea of a model-based verifier is not entirely new, but the authors implement a specifically fine-tuned generative model for this purpose, which potentially enhances robustness and addresses limitations of rule-based systems. However, previous work like CrossThink and RLVR also aimed at expanding RL for reasoning across broader domains. A better discussion of the differences and advantages of the present method relative to these works would strengthen the novelty claim.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to make LLMs more useful in real-world scenarios where reasoning extends beyond mathematical or coding problems. By creating a diverse and verifiable dataset, the authors unlock the potential for LLMs to be trained and applied in fields like science, finance, and the humanities. The model-based verifier is significant because it can handle a wider range of answer formats and complexities compared to rule-based systems. The empirical results appear promising, demonstrating performance improvements on diverse benchmark tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly addresses a significant limitation in existing LLM reasoning research.
    *   The creation of the new dataset is a valuable contribution to the community. Its public release would further amplify the impact.
    *   The empirical results on multiple benchmarks are compelling, showing improvements in various domains.
    *   Ablation studies effectively highlight the importance of both the diverse training data and the model-based verifier.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper comparison to related work, particularly CrossThink and RLVR, to better highlight the unique contributions of GENERAL-REASONER.  Specifically, it needs to address how the model-based verifier improves over existing approaches.
    *   The computational cost of training and using the model-based verifier should be discussed in more detail.  Is the performance gain worth the extra overhead?
    *   The paper could be strengthened by providing more qualitative examples of the reasoning capabilities learned by the model. Showing specific examples where the model-based verifier enables more accurate and generalizable reasoning would be beneficial.
    *   The analysis of the performance across different subfields within the benchmarks (i.e. within MMLU-Pro) could provide more insights into the strengths and weaknesses of the approach.

*   **Impact:** The potential impact of this work is reasonably high.  If the dataset and training paradigm prove to be effective and scalable, they could significantly contribute to advancing LLM reasoning across various domains.  This would lead to the development of more robust and general-purpose AI systems. The adoption of Zero-RL further increases the accessibility of the methods. However, its impact depends on the reproducibility of the results and the community's adoption of the proposed dataset and training techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty and significance of the paper, but also the weaknesses mentioned above, I assign it a score of **7**. The combination of a diverse verifiable dataset and a model-based verifier for Zero-RL is a valuable contribution to the field. The empirical results are promising, and the paper addresses a significant limitation in current LLM reasoning research. However, the limited comparison to existing works, lack of qualitative analysis, missing release plans for data/code and concerns regarding the computational overhead of the model-based verifier prevent it from achieving a higher score. A score of 7 reflects a solid contribution with the potential for further impact, but with areas for improvement and further validation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SAFEPATH: Preventing Harmful Reasoning in Chain-of-Thought via Early Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14667v1)
- **Authors**: Wonje Jeung, Sangyeon Yoon, Minsuk Kahng, Albert No
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have become powerful tools for complex problem solving, but their structured reasoning pathways can lead to unsafe outputs when exposed to harmful prompts. Existing safety alignment methods reduce harmful outputs but can degrade reasoning depth, leading to significant trade-offs in complex, multi-step tasks, and remain vulnerable to sophisticated jailbreak attacks. To address this, we introduce SAFEPATH, a lightweight alignment method that fine-tunes LRMs to emit a short, 8-token Safety Primer at the start of their reasoning, in response to harmful prompts, while leaving the rest of the reasoning process unsupervised. Empirical results across multiple benchmarks indicate that SAFEPATH effectively reduces harmful outputs while maintaining reasoning performance. Specifically, SAFEPATH reduces harmful responses by up to 90.0% and blocks 83.3% of jailbreak attempts in the DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B model, while requiring 295.9x less compute than Direct Refusal and 314.1x less than SafeChain. We further introduce a zero-shot variant that requires no fine-tuning. In addition, we provide a comprehensive analysis of how existing methods in LLMs generalize, or fail, when applied to reasoning-centric models, revealing critical gaps and new directions for safer AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "SAFEPATH: Preventing Harmful Reasoning in Chain-of-Thought via Early Alignment" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the vulnerability of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) to harmful prompts, which can lead to unsafe outputs due to their structured reasoning processes. The authors propose SAFEPATH, a lightweight alignment method that fine-tunes LRMs to emit a short "Safety Primer" ("Let's think about safety first") at the beginning of their reasoning process when exposed to harmful prompts. The rest of the reasoning process remains unsupervised. The authors demonstrate that SAFEPATH effectively reduces harmful outputs while maintaining reasoning performance, achieving lower attack success rates and preserving reasoning accuracy compared to baseline methods like Direct Refusal and SafeChain.  They also present a zero-shot variant and analyze the performance of existing LLM safety methods when applied to LRMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel and pragmatic approach to aligning LRMs. While the concept of safety alignment is not new, the specific approach of using a short, early-stage "Safety Primer" in an unsupervised manner for the remainder of the reasoning chain is a valuable contribution. It leverages the inherent reasoning capabilities of LRMs rather than relying on strict refusals or heavy supervision, which often degrade performance. The zero-shot variant further adds to the practical appeal. Comparing against LLM based safety models highlight the gap and novelty.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial challenge in the development of LRMs: ensuring safety without sacrificing reasoning performance. The vulnerability of LRMs to harmful prompts is a significant concern, and SAFEPATH offers a promising solution. The empirical results, demonstrating reduced harmfulness and preserved reasoning ability, are compelling. The computational efficiency of SAFEPATH, particularly the significantly lower training costs compared to baselines, is also a significant advantage, making it more practical for real-world deployment. Qualitative case studies are a valuable addition.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The proposed method is simple, efficient, and effective.
    *   The empirical evaluation is thorough, comparing SAFEPATH against strong baselines on multiple benchmarks and adversarial attacks.
    *   The analysis of existing LLM safety methods in the LRM setting is insightful, highlighting the need for LRM-specific approaches.
    *   The paper provides a clear explanation of the method and its benefits.
    *  The work clearly highlights the trade-off between safety and utility in LRM alignment, and makes targeted modifications to increase both.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the results are promising, the method is still vulnerable to more sophisticated adversarial attacks (e.g., PAIR). This vulnerability is acknowledged by the authors but indicates that further research is needed to improve the robustness of SAFEPATH.
    *   The choice of the 8-token "Safety Primer" is somewhat arbitrary. While it performs well in the experiments, the paper could benefit from further exploration of alternative primer phrases and their impact on performance. A hyperparameter study for the a tuning would make it more rigorous.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of LRM safety alignment by demonstrating a practical and effective approach to mitigating harmful outputs without sacrificing reasoning performance. The insights gained from analyzing existing LLM safety methods in the LRM setting can also guide future research in this area. The approach offers a new direction and demonstrates the importance of early-stage interventions.

* **Overall:**

SAFEPATH is an innovative approach to address safety in LRMs. While vulnerabilities still exist, the method's simplicity, efficiency, and relatively light fine-tuning make it a valuable advancement for both academic researchers and practitioners looking for deployable LRM safety solutions.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a novel approach to LRM safety alignment with strong empirical results, particularly regarding efficiency. However, the limitations, particularly the vulnerability to highly optimized jailbreak techniques, prevent a higher score. The need for further refinement and exploration of alternative designs/paradigms lowers the impact and influence.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### ContextAgent: Context-Aware Proactive LLM Agents with Open-World Sensory Perceptions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14668v1)
- **Authors**: Bufang Yang, Lilin Xu, Liekang Zeng, Kaiwei Liu, Siyang Jiang, Wenrui Lu, Hongkai Chen, Xiaofan Jiang, Guoliang Xing, Zhenyu Yan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have propelled intelligent agents from reactive responses to proactive support. While promising, existing proactive agents either rely exclusively on observations from enclosed environments (e.g., desktop UIs) with direct LLM inference or employ rule-based proactive notifications, leading to suboptimal user intent understanding and limited functionality for proactive service. In this paper, we introduce ContextAgent, the first context-aware proactive agent that incorporates extensive sensory contexts to enhance the proactive capabilities of LLM agents. ContextAgent first extracts multi-dimensional contexts from massive sensory perceptions on wearables (e.g., video and audio) to understand user intentions. ContextAgent then leverages the sensory contexts and the persona contexts from historical data to predict the necessity for proactive services. When proactive assistance is needed, ContextAgent further automatically calls the necessary tools to assist users unobtrusively. To evaluate this new task, we curate ContextAgentBench, the first benchmark for evaluating context-aware proactive LLM agents, covering 1,000 samples across nine daily scenarios and twenty tools. Experiments on ContextAgentBench show that ContextAgent outperforms baselines by achieving up to 8.5% and 6.0% higher accuracy in proactive predictions and tool calling, respectively. We hope our research can inspire the development of more advanced, human-centric, proactive AI assistants.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ContextAgent: Context-Aware Proactive LLM Agents with Open-World Sensory Perceptions":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ContextAgent, a novel framework for creating proactive LLM agents that leverage extensive sensory context from wearable devices (e.g., smart glasses, earphones) to provide intelligent assistance. Unlike existing proactive agents that rely primarily on desktop UI information or rule-based notifications, ContextAgent aims to understand user intentions and provide relevant services in open-world environments. The framework extracts multi-dimensional contexts from sensory perceptions, integrates persona information, and employs tool-augmented LLM reasoning for proactive predictions and service delivery. The authors also introduce ContextAgentBench, a new benchmark for evaluating context-aware proactive LLM agents, comprising 1,000 samples across diverse daily scenarios and twenty tools. Experimental results demonstrate that ContextAgent outperforms baseline approaches on ContextAgentBench in proactive predictions and tool calling accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a reasonably novel approach to proactive LLM agents by focusing on open-world sensory input from wearable devices. This is a significant departure from existing proactive agents primarily confined to desktop environments. ContextAgentBench is also a valuable contribution, as it specifically addresses the need for evaluating context-aware proactive agents. However, the core components (LLMs, tool use, context extraction) are not entirely new, but rather integrated in a novel way.

*   **Significance:** The research has the potential to significantly advance the development of more useful and human-centric AI assistants. The use of wearable sensors allows for a more natural and unobtrusive form of assistance, potentially reducing cognitive load on users. The ContextAgentBench provides a standardized way to evaluate and compare different approaches, which can accelerate research in this area. The quantitative and qualitative results indicate the practical value of the proposed system.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined framework with modular components.
    *   Introduction of a new and relevant benchmark.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with strong results.
    *   The inclusion of qualitative examples helps to understand how the components integrate with each other.
    * The paper has thorough experimentation and ablation study.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper's claims regarding the leap in "proactive predictions" may not be accurate, as the data has some inherent limitations. The "true" necessity for proactive service is somewhat subjective, and determined by the experiment designer.
    *   Reliance on pre-defined tool sets. While understandable for evaluation purposes, this may limit the adaptability of the agent in real-world scenarios.
    *   Ethical considerations of continuous sensory data collection and potential privacy risks are not thoroughly addressed.
    *   Limited discussion on the computational costs and practical deployment challenges of such a system on wearable devices.
    *   The work doesn't address the limitations of current sensor technology and their impact on context extraction.
    *   The paper needs to acknowledge how a failure in proactive services would affect the agent in a more realistic setting.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper is likely to inspire further research on proactive LLM agents in open-world environments.
    *   ContextAgentBench may become a widely used benchmark for evaluating context-aware proactive agents.
    *   The framework may serve as a foundation for developing more advanced personal AI assistants.
    *   The work could influence the design of wearable devices and their integration with AI systems.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

While the paper introduces a valuable integration of existing technologies to address a relevant and promising research area, it doesn't involve a groundbreaking theoretical breakthrough or address all practical implementation concerns. The limitations relating to evaluation, pre-defined toolsets and deployment needs to be addressed. The work certainly warrants future efforts to make the agent more robust.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Quartet: Native FP4 Training Can Be Optimal for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14669v1)
- **Authors**: Roberto L. Castro, Andrei Panferov, Soroush Tabesh, Oliver Sieberling, Jiale Chen, Mahdi Nikdan, Saleh Ashkboos, Dan Alistarh
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) has been paralleled by unprecedented increases in computational demands, with training costs for state-of-the-art models doubling every few months. Training models directly in low-precision arithmetic offers a solution, by improving both computational throughput and energy efficiency. Specifically, NVIDIA's recent Blackwell architecture facilitates extremely low-precision operations, specifically FP4 variants, promising substantial efficiency gains. Yet, current algorithms for training LLMs in FP4 precision face significant accuracy degradation and often rely on mixed-precision fallbacks. In this paper, we systematically investigate hardware-supported FP4 training and introduce Quartet, a new approach enabling accurate, end-to-end FP4 training with all the major computations (in e.g. linear layers) being performed in low precision. Through extensive evaluations on Llama-type models, we reveal a new low-precision scaling law that quantifies performance trade-offs across varying bit-widths and allows us to identify a "near-optimal" low-precision training technique in terms of accuracy-vs-computation, called Quartet. We implement Quartet using optimized CUDA kernels tailored for NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs, and show that it can achieve state-of-the-art accuracy for FP4 precision, successfully training billion-scale models. Our method demonstrates that fully FP4-based training is a competitive alternative to standard-precision and FP8 training. Our code is available at https://github.com/IST-DASLab/Quartet.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Quartet: Native FP4 Training Can Be Optimal for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Quartet, a new algorithm for training large language models (LLMs) natively in the FP4 precision format. It addresses the challenge that existing FP4 training methods often suffer from accuracy degradation and rely on mixed-precision fallbacks. The authors perform a systematic study of hardware-supported FP4 training, develop optimized CUDA kernels for NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs, and demonstrate that Quartet achieves state-of-the-art accuracy for FP4, potentially surpassing both standard-precision and FP8 training. The paper introduces a new scaling law to analyze performance tradeoffs at varying bit-widths, isolating parameter and data efficiency. They claim that Quartet provides an optimal accuracy-efficiency trade-off, maximizing accuracy within a given computational budget.  Crucially, the paper provides a GPU implementation and experimentally validates the algorithm by pre-training Llama-family models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Hardware-Aware Algorithm:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the hardware-aware design of the Quartet algorithm, specifically tailored for NVIDIA's Blackwell architecture. This goes beyond simply applying existing quantization techniques to FP4; the authors explicitly optimize for the architecture's capabilities, which is a significant contribution.
*   **Performance Scaling Law:** The introduction of a performance scaling law to analyze different precision approaches is a novel analytical technique that can be used to compare training methods by accounting for both model-parameter and data efficiencies. This provides a more systematic approach for comparing algorithms and understanding the accuracy-vs-efficiency trade-offs.
*   **GPU implementation:** The implementation of quartet is novel and highly optimized for the new Blackwell architecture.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing Computational Cost:** Training LLMs is computationally expensive. The potential to drastically reduce costs via FP4 while maintaining accuracy is highly significant. If FP4 training can become a mainstream approach, it could democratize access to training frontier models.
*   **Pushing the Precision Frontier:** The paper pushes the boundaries of low-precision training, challenging the established wisdom that 8-bit or mixed-precision approaches are necessary for acceptable accuracy. This encourages further research in extremely low-precision training methods.
